Part 0 — Headlines

  1. Medical-only, still. North Dakota has no adult-use programme. Only registered qualifying patients (and their registered designated caregivers) may lawfully purchase, possess and use usable marijuana, and only from ND-registered dispensaries, under NDCC ch. 19-24.1. Adult-use possession, sale and cultivation remain unlawful under NDCC chs. 19-03.1 / 19-03.4, subject only to the tiered small-amount penalties in Part 1, §1.3. Sources: NDCC ch. 19-24.1 — https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30; NDCC §19-03.1-23(7) as amended by 2025 HB 1367 — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-1001-02000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  2. The 2025 medical expansion is the most recent major change — effective 1 August 2025. HB 1203 creates NDCC §19-24.1-24.1 and amends §19-24.1-01: low-dose cannabinoid edible products are now lawful, but ONLY as a soft or hard lozenge in a geometric square shape, max 5 mg THC per serving and 50 mg per package; other THC food/drink remains outside the definition; prior department approval is required; marketing may not target minors. Source: HB 1203 — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-0503-01000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. SB 2294 creates a new section for qualifying nonresident patients; card term moves from 1 year to 2 years; patient fee “not to exceed forty dollars for a registry identification card valid for two years” (§19-24.1-03(2)(a)); telehealth is reported in secondary sources as allowed — Verify the exact statutory telehealth sentence. Source: SB 2294 engrossed text — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-1233-02002m.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. SB 2293: 1-gram maximum container for a cannabinoid concentrate (now §19-24.1-01(7)); alternative department-approved documentation where a patient cannot obtain ND identification because of a medical condition; caregiver minimum age 21 confirmed. SB 2308 (2025) eliminates the Medical Marijuana Advisory Board and the annual-report requirement — secondary report only; Verify on ndlegis.gov, and see Part 5, §5.2 for the repeal marking (S.L. 2025, ch. 479, §39) found in the current NDCC PDF.
  3. Federal Schedule III / 280E — what it means for ND. See the federal baseline box above. Two Verify items: (a) tribal medical programme as “state-licensed” — Verify, not assumed; (b) Board of Pharmacy action under §19-03.1-02(4) — Verify. Source for §19-03.1-02(4) (Justia mirror — confirm against legis.nd.gov ch. 19-03.1 PDF before Run 2): https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-19/chapter-19-03-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  4. Hemp-derived THC: ND is a prohibition state for intoxicating hemp, not a regulated-intoxicating-hemp state. Delta-8 THC, THC-O acetate, HHC and THCP are expressly excluded from “hemp commodity or product” (NDCC §4.1-18.1-01, as amended by 2023 SB 2096); a chemical compound extracted from hemp used to make an inhalant is excluded; a licensee may not chemically modify/convert hemp extract or CBD into delta-9, delta-8, delta-10 or other THC isomers/analogues/derivatives (§4.1-18.1-04.3); the Agriculture Commissioner may disapprove in writing any psychotropic hemp commodity/product. NDDA guidance adds: a product containing ONLY delta-9 and no other cannabinoids is “deemed to be psychotropic” and is not legal; THCA flower must be under 0.3% total THC; “If the answer is to produce a high in the user, the product is not allowed for retail in North Dakota.” Sources: NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1 (Justia mirror) — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-4-1/chapter-4-1-18-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30; SB 2096 — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/68-2023/regular/documents/23-8110-01000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30; NDDA Hemp page — https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  5. No cannabis measure on the 3 November 2026 ballot — and that election had NOT yet occurred on 30 Oct 2026. The SOS Measures-on-Ballot page lists only Constitutional Measure 1 (HCR 3003 — 60% threshold for constitutional amendments) and Constitutional Measure 2 (free school breakfasts/lunches). There is NO marijuana measure listed and NO official 2026 result to report. The last adult-use vote was 2024 Measure 5 (rejected; secondary reports ~47.5% yes / ~53% no — official SOS canvass to be cited in Part 9, Run 2). Sources: ND SOS, Measures on Ballot — https://www.sos.nd.gov/elections/voter/ballot-measures/measures-ballot — Accessed 2026-10-30; SOS press release 21 Sept 2026 — https://www.sos.nd.gov/news/ballot-measure-guide-sent-voters-ahead-november-3-election — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  6. 2025 penalty-reduction bill failed in the Senate — penalties did NOT change in 2025. HB 1596 (69th Assembly) would have made <½ oz possession a noncriminal $150 citation and stepped down higher tiers. It passed the House 76–17 (Feb 2025) but failed in the Senate 9 Apr 2025 (secondary reports: 13–33 — vote-count discrepancy flagged, see Gaps). Current law remains the infraction / Class B / Class A tiers in Part 1, §1.3. Sources (secondary, for status only): North Dakota Monitor, 9 Apr 2025 — https://northdakotamonitor.com/2025/04/09/north-dakota-holds-steady-on-marijuana-penalties/ — Accessed 2026-10-30; HB 1596 tracking — https://fastdemocracy.com/bill-search/nd/69/bills/NDB00006585 — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  7. Key numbers (official-text numbers only). 2 manufacturing facilities and 8 dispensaries are the statutory compassion-center caps; patient flower limits 2.5 oz purchase / 3 oz possession per 30 days (standard) and 6 oz / 7.5 oz (enhanced, cancer only); non-flower THC 6,000 mg per 30 days; edible possession 500 mg at any time, edible purchase 310 mg edible-THC purchase limit (HHS reads as daily; the statute does not say “per day”) (HHS reading — see §1.4.5 wording note); patient card fee not to exceed $40 (two-year card); compassion-center application fee not to exceed $5,000, certification fees $90,000 (dispensary) / $110,000 (manufacturing facility) in the original SB 2344 text — Verify current certification-fee amounts, as NDAC fee amendments are reported to have changed fees (see Part 4, §4.6, for the three fee layers). Sources: official ch. 19-24.1 PDF above; SB 2344 enrolled text — https://ndlegis.gov/api/assembly/65-2017/regular/documents/17-0630-05000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  8. Biennial session note. No 2026 regular session. Interim committees may meet in 2026, but no new regular-session cannabis bill can be enacted in 2026 outside a special session (none identified in sources read — Verify on ndlegis.gov if a special session is called before Run 2).

Part 1 — Laws

How to read “last amended” below: the official ch. 19-24.1 PDF on legis.nd.gov is a compiled current text; it does not print a single “last amended” date on its face. Amendment dates are therefore given as the session law that last amended that section, where that bill was located. Where the amending bill for a specific section could not be confirmed, the entry says “Last amendment: Verify (compiled text as read 2026-10-30)” rather than inventing a date. Do NOT treat the PDF's crawl date as an amendment date. One paginated re-read of the official ch. 19-24.1 PDF hit a 429 rate limit and was NOT retried; facts from that PDF rest on the first successful read plus official bill texts and HHS/NDDA pages. Sections beyond §19-24.1-01 that are summarised (not quoted) should be re-read in full from the compiled PDF in Run 2.

1.1 Legal status: medical only

North Dakota is a medical-only state. A person may lawfully acquire, possess and use usable marijuana ONLY if that person is a registered qualifying patient (or a registered designated caregiver acting for such a patient), holds a valid registry identification card, and obtains the product from an ND-registered dispensary, within the allowable amounts and product forms in NDCC ch. 19-24.1. Everyone else — including an adult with no card, a visitor with only another state's card (except through the new nonresident pathway, §1.4.7, whose details are Verify), and a patient who exceeds the allowable amount or uses a non-allowed form — is outside the medical protection and is governed by NDCC chs. 19-03.1 and 19-03.4.

What is NOT legal for non-patients: possession of marijuana or THC in any amount (tiered penalties, §1.3 — the <½ oz tier is an infraction, i.e. non-jailable, but it is still an offence, not legalisation); sale, delivery, or possession with intent to manufacture/deliver marijuana or THC — Class C felony under §19-03.1-23(1)(c); cultivation / home grow — prohibited for everyone, including registered patients (only a registered manufacturing facility may produce marijuana; the 2016 Measure 5 home-grow provisions in the original initiated ch. 19-24 were repealed/replaced by SB 2344 in 2017); purchase from any source other than an ND-registered dispensary; import/transport across state lines into ND (including from Minnesota, Montana, Manitoba/Saskatchewan — detail in Run 2, Part 10); public consumption / consumption in the places listed in §19-24.1-33 (§1.3.5); driving / being in actual physical control of a vehicle, aircraft, train or motorboat while under the influence of marijuana (§19-24.1-33(5)); hemp-derived intoxicating products as listed in §1.5.

Verbatim — medical-use and marijuana definitions (current ch. 19-24.1, §19-24.1-01):

“29. 'Medical use of marijuana' means the acquisition, use, and possession of usable marijuana to treat or alleviate a qualifying patient's debilitating medical condition.”
“24. 'Marijuana' means all parts of the plant of the genus cannabis; the seeds of the plant; the resin extracted from any part of the plant; and every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of the plant, the seeds of the plant, or the resin extracted from any part of the plant. The term marijuana does not include: a. Hemp as regulated under section 4.1-18.1-01; or b. A prescription drug approved by the United States food and drug administration under section 505 of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act [21 U.S.C. 355].”

Source: official ch. 19-24.1 PDF — https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

1.2 Every statute and regulation — citation, summary, verbatim operative text, last-amended date, official link

1.2.1 Measure 5 / SB 2344 history — where the legislature changed the voters' text

  • 8 November 2016 — Initiated Measure 5 (North Dakota Compassionate Care Act) approved by voters (secondary reports: ~63.7–63.79% yes; official SOS canvass to be cited in Part 9). It created the original NDCC ch. 19-24, including patient home cultivation and a different compassion-center structure.
  • 2017 — SB 2344 (65th Legislative Assembly; LC 17.0630.05000), signed 17 April 2017, effective 18 April 2017 (emergency clause). SB 2344 REPEALED ch. 19-24 in full and CREATED ch. 19-24.1 in its place — a legislature's rewrite, not a mere amendment. Its title (verbatim from the enrolled bill):
“AN ACT to create and enact chapter 19-24.1 of the North Dakota Century Code, relating to medical marijuana; to amend and reenact section 54-60-03, paragraph 3 of subdivision a of subsection 15 of section 57-02-08, and paragraph 2 of subdivision b of subsection 15 of section 57-02-08 of the North Dakota Century Code, relating to primary sector business certification and property tax exemptions for farm buildings and residences; to repeal chapter 19-24 of the North Dakota Century Code, relating to medical marijuana; to provide a statement of legislative intent; to provide for a report; to provide a penalty; to provide a continuing appropriation; and to declare an emergency.”

Material legislature-made changes identifiable from the enrolled text and programme history: (a) home cultivation by patients removed — production only by registered manufacturing facilities in enclosed, locked facilities; (b) the original 2,000 mg THC / 30-day non-flower limit structure was retained at enactment and only later raised (see §1.2.2); (c) edibles in food/drink form were NOT authorised (the original SB 2344 product list was solutions/capsules/patches/topicals/concentrates/flower); (d) compassion-center caps, fees and application structure in §§19-24.1-14–19 were set by the legislature; (e) the “minor” threshold was set at under 19, not 18. Source: SB 2344 enrolled text — https://ndlegis.gov/api/assembly/65-2017/regular/documents/17-0630-05000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

1.2.2 NDCC ch. 19-24.1 — Medical Marijuana (current compiled text)

Official link: https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Sections (as listed in the compiled PDF's table/text read; operative sections are quoted below, the remainder summarised with subject so Run 2 can quote them in full): §19-24.1-01 Definitions; §19-24.1-02 department duties / rulemaking — subject only, Verify full text; §19-24.1-03 Qualifying patients — Registration (quoted in §1.4); §19-24.1-03.1 Qualifying patients — Veterans (pre-2025 section; SB 2294 amended it in 2025 — Verify current text); §19-24.1-04 Designated caregivers — Registration; §19-24.1-04.1 Designated caregivers — Criminal history record check exemption (terminal illness / hospice only, per 2023 HB 1478 text); §19-24.1-05 Identification cards — Issuance and denial (30-calendar-day issuance after approval; patient may have no more than five registered designated caregivers); §§19-24.1-06–19-24.1-13 cardholder duties, confidentiality, verification system, compassion-center registration framework; §19-24.1-14 Compassion centers — Application (1,000-foot school buffer + local-zoning compliance evidence, §1.6; application fee not to exceed $5,000; certification fees $90,000 dispensary / $110,000 manufacturing facility in SB 2344 text — Verify current amounts); §§19-24.1-15–19-24.1-20 registration, renewal, operations, inventory, dispensing (§19-24.1-21: dispensary may not dispense unless it first confirms card validity via the department verification system — cited in HHS guidance); §19-24.1-24.1 Cannabinoid edible products (created by 2025 HB 1203 — prior department approval required); §§19-24.1-32–19-24.1-38 rulemaking, limitations (§19-24.1-33, §1.3.5), acts not prohibited / not required (§19-24.1-34, §1.8), penalties, confidentiality/disclosure (§19-24.1-37 as amended by SB 2294), advisory board (§19-24.1-38 — repealed/eliminated by 2025 SB 2308 per secondary report, Verify).

Later amendments to ch. 19-24.1 located in this session (not exhaustive — a full session-law audit is a Run 2 follow-up): 2019 HB 1119 (66th Assembly): amended §§19-24.1-01, -03, -04, -15, -18, -32, -37; changed “tincture” to “solution”; addressed SSN destruction/redaction. 2019 HB 1155: marijuana/paraphernalia penalties — FAILED in House (43–47, 20 Feb 2019) — NOT law. 2021 HB 1201: marijuana penalties — FAILED in Senate (13–32) — NOT law. 2021 HB 1359: designated caregivers — patient may have no more than five registered designated caregivers; advisory-board composition. 2021 HB 1045: hemp/THC definitions — see §1.2.4 (amended ch. 4.1-18.1 and ch. 19-03.1, not ch. 19-24.1, but is the delta-8/isomerisation ban's first step). 2023 HB 1478: senior (65+) / hospice self-certification pathway in lieu of written certification; caregiver criminal-history waiver for terminal-illness/hospice patients only; department to waive hospice patient fee; card within 14/30 days (engrossment shows both figures — Verify enrolled text). 2023 SB 2096: hemp — see §1.2.4. 2023 SB 2388: reapplication after revocation (1 year after first revocation, 5 years after second, disqualified after third); employment language in §19-24.1-34 (see §1.8); §§19-24.1-05, -18, -19 amended. 2023 HB 1202: edible products bill — did NOT become the 2025 edible law; the 2023 version carried a contingent effective date and did not enact edibles. Do not cite HB 1202 as the edible authority — HB 1203 (2025) is. 2025 HB 1203, SB 2293, SB 2294, SB 2308, HB 1367 (penalties/paraphernalia — HB 1367 DID pass and amended §19-03.1-23(7) and §19-03.4-03(1); see §1.3), HB 1596 (failed, §1.9). Non-flower THC limit raised from 2,000 mg to 6,000 mg per 30 days effective 1 August 2023 (secondary Dutchie/HHS summary; current §19-24.1-01(3)(c) now reads 6,000 mg — the amending session law for that specific raise is Verify; do not attribute it to HB 1203).

1.2.3 NDCC ch. 19-03.1 — Uniform Controlled Substances Act

Official link (compiled chapter PDF via legis.nd.gov pattern; Justia mirror read here): https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-19/chapter-19-03-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. Confirm against https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c03-1.pdf in Run 2 (that exact URL was not returned as a verified link in this session and is therefore not asserted as a working link).

§19-03.1-02 Authority to control — Board of Pharmacy administers the chapter and may add/delete/reschedule substances. Verbatim, subsection 4 (the federal-following rule — central to the Schedule III question):

“4. If any substance is designated, rescheduled, or deleted as a controlled substance under federal law and notice thereof is given to the board, the board shall similarly control the substance under this chapter after the expiration of thirty days from publication in the federal register of a final order designating a substance as a controlled substance or rescheduling, or deleting a substance, unless within that thirty-day period, the board objects to inclusion, rescheduling, or deletion. In that case, the board shall publish the reasons for objection and afford all interested parties an opportunity to be heard.”

Application to the given federal baseline (analysis, not a located ND order): On its face, §19-03.1-02(4) means a federal final order rescheduling state-licensed medical marijuana to Schedule III would, after 30 days from Federal Register publication, similarly control it under ND law UNLESS the Board objected in that window. The 28 April 2026 effective date is ~30+ days after any March/April 2026 Federal Register publication, so the window has likely closed — but no ND Board of Pharmacy order, objection, or amended Schedule I/III list was located, and the ND Schedule I text read still lists marijuana/THCs. Therefore: Verify — do NOT state as fact that ND state schedules have already moved medical marijuana to Schedule III. The federal Schedule III status for state-licensed medical activity stands on the federal baseline alone; the ND state-schedule position is unresolved on the sources read. This distinction matters for ND prosecutions (which proceed under state schedules) vs. federal 280E (which turns on federal scheduling).

§19-03.1-04 Schedule I tests; §19-03.1-05 Schedule I — verbatim (operative cannabis entries, as amended by 2025 SB 2064, 69th Assembly, Judiciary Committee at the request of the State Board of Pharmacy — scheduling bill, emergency clause):

“g. Marijuana.”
“m. (1) Tetrahydrocannabinols, meaning tetrahydrocannabinols naturally contained in a plant of the genus Cannabis (cannabis plant), as well as synthetic equivalents of the substances contained in the cannabis plant, or in the resinous extractives of such plant, including synthetic substances, derivatives, and their isomers with similar chemical structure and pharmacological activity to those substances contained in the plant; such as the following: (a) Delta-1 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and their optical isomers. Other names: Delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol. (b) Delta-6 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and their optical isomers. Other names: Delta-8-tetrahydrocannabinol. (c) Delta-3,4 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and its optical isomers. (Since nomenclature of these substances is not internationally standardized, compounds of these structures, regardless of numerical designation of atomic positions covered.) (2) Tetrahydrocannabinols do not include: (a) The allowable amount of total tetrahydrocannabinol found in hemp or an allowed hemp commodity or product as defined in chapter 4.1-18.1; or (b) A prescription drug approved by the United States food and drug administration under section 505 of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act [21 U.S.C. 355].”

Source: SB 2064 text — https://legis.nd.gov/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-8048-01000.pdf and https://legis.nd.gov/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-8048-02000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. §19-03.1-23 Prohibited acts / penalties — quoted in §1.3. §19-03.1-22.3 (ingestion), §19-03.1-23.1, §19-03.1-23(9) (under-21 education programme for first-offence ≤½ oz) — full text Verify.

1.2.4 NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1 — Hemp (and related hemp statutes)

Official link (compiled chapter; Justia mirror read): https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-4-1/chapter-4-1-18-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. History in brief (from statute text and bill titles): ND first authorised hemp as a commercial crop in 1999 (HB 1428, secondary); pilot programme under the 2014 Farm Bill; 2019 HB 1349 + HB 1113 aligned the programme with the 2018 Farm Bill and removed hemp/CBD from the controlled-substances schedules; 2021 HB 1045 redefined THC to include delta-7/delta-8/delta-10 isomers and banned licensee isomerisation/conversion (CBD → delta-8 etc.); 2023 SB 2096 rewrote the hemp definitions, created §4.1-18.1-01.1 (administrative rules) and nine new sections (commissioner powers, civil enforcement), and expressly excluded delta-8 / chemically derived cannabinoids / hemp-extract inhalants / commissioner-disapproved psychotropic products from “hemp commodity or product.”

§4.1-18.1-01 Definitions — verbatim (operative parts):

“4. 'Hemp' means the plant cannabis sativa L. and any part of the plant, including the seeds and flowers, whether growing or not, with a total tetrahydrocannabinol concentration in an amount determined by the commissioner. The term does not include hemp extract, commodity or product using hemp, or a hemp substance or product prohibited by this chapter.”
“8. 'Tetrahydrocannabinol' means delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol and any structural, optical, or geometric isomers of tetrahydrocannabinol, including: a. Delta-7 tetrahydrocannabinol; b. Delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol; or c. Delta-10 tetrahydrocannabinol.”
“9. 'Total tetrahydrocannabinol' means the sum of the percentage, by weight, of tetrahydrocannabinolic acid multiplied by eight hundred seventy-seven thousandths plus the percentage of weight of tetrahydrocannabinol.” [i.e. (THCa × 0.877) + delta-9 THC]
“1. 'Broad spectrum' means hemp extract or hemp commodity or product containing naturally occurring hemp-derived cannabinoids, terpenes, and other naturally occurring compounds, but where tetrahydrocannabinol has been removed to nondetectable levels using a fit-for-purpose method, with a total tetrahydrocannabinol level not to exceed an amount determined by the commissioner.”

“Hemp commodity or product” — includes (in §4.1-18.1-01(6) as read): cloth, cordage, fiber, food, fuel, paint, paper, particleboard, plastics, seeds, seed meal, seed oil for consumption, a hemp tincture, and a hemp topical; and specifically includes CBD and CBG products (broad spectrum, full spectrum, isolate) with total THC not to exceed the commissioner's amount, and any hemp commodity/product approved in writing by the commissioner. It does NOT include: (1) Hemp that has been chopped, separated, or dried for purposes of transfer or storage; (2) A chemical compound extracted from hemp used to formulate, process, or otherwise make an inhalant; (3) A product containing delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol, also known as delta-8 THC; (4) A product containing chemically derived cannabinoids, including: (a) Tetrahydrocannabinol acetate, also known as THC-O-Acetate and THC-O; (b) Hexahydrocannabinol, also known as HHC; and (c) Tetrahydrocannabiphorol, also known as THCP; or (5) A psychotropic hemp commodity or product disapproved in writing by the commissioner.

§4.1-18.1-01.1 Administrative rules: “The commissioner may adopt and amend rules consistent with this chapter governing the sale, distribution, testing, labeling, and regulation of hemp and hemp commodities or products, and substances and products prohibited by this chapter.” (created by SB 2096, 2023). §4.1-18.1-02 Hemp — Licensure: “Any person desiring to grow or process hemp shall apply to the agriculture commissioner for a license on a form prescribed by the commissioner. A license must be obtained before a person purchases or obtains hemp material for planting or propagation.” Initial licensure requires statewide + nationwide criminal history record check; licensure must be denied to a person convicted of a felony relating to a controlled substance under state or federal law in the last ten years. (Fees and full licence detail are Part 7, Run 2 — NDDA page gives grower $100/$200/$300/$350 by lot count and processor $200, plus $40 background-check payment.) §4.1-18.1-03 License fee: commissioner shall assess each producer and processor a fee not to exceed $350 (2019 text; current fee schedule on NDDA page is within that cap). §4.1-18.1-04.1 Commissioner — Authority — Tetrahydrocannabinol concentration: “The commissioner shall determine the total tetrahydrocannabinol concentration under this chapter up to an amount not to exceed the federal Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 [Pub. L. 115-334]” — i.e. the commissioner's amount may not exceed the federal 2018 Farm Bill ceiling (0.3% in practice; NDDA guidance states flower must be <0.3% total THC and all cannabinoid hemp products must be tested for total THC with a COA available).

§4.1-18.1-04.3 Prohibited acts — Licensee (verbatim, operative):

“A licensee may not: 1. Chemically modify or convert a hemp extract, or engage in any process that converts cannabidiol into delta-9, delta-8, delta-10-tetrahydrocannabinol, or other tetrahydrocannabinol isomers, analogs, or derivatives; and 2. Sell or distribute hemp or hemp commodities or products that contain chemically derived cannabinoids or were created by chemically modifying or converting a hemp extract.”

§4.1-18.1-05 Violations (negligence vs. culpable mental state > negligence → report to Attorney General; negligent failure to provide legal description / obtain licence / exceed total THC → compliance deadline + ≥2 years additional reporting); §4.1-18.1-06 Confiscation and disposal. Last-amended note for ch. 4.1-18.1: substantively rewritten by SB 2096 (2023, filed 2023); no 2025 amendment to ch. 4.1-18.1 was located in this session. Verify against the legis.nd.gov compiled ch. 4.1-18.1 PDF and the 2025 session-law index — a 2025 technical amendment cannot be ruled out on the sources read.

1.2.5 NDCC ch. 19-03.4 — Drug Paraphernalia

§19-03.4-03 — quoted in §1.3.3. Amended by 2025 HB 1367, subsection 1 only (the enrolled HB 1367 text amends §19-03.4-03(1); the remaining subsections are as previously enacted — Verify whether HB 1367 changed any classification in subsection 1 beyond wording, as the enrolled text excerpt in search did not reproduce the full amended subsection 1).

1.2.6 ND Administrative Code — Medical Marijuana, art. 33-44, ch. 33-44-01 (and ch. 33-44-02 testing)

Official link (NDAC PDF): https://www.ndlegis.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30 (via search; section list confirmed). Sections include: 33-44-01-01 Definitions; -02 Cardholder notification of change; -03 Fees for failure to provide notice; -04 Cardholder disposal; -05 Expiration of registry identification cards; -06 Compassion center application process; -07 Establishing additional compassion centers; -08 Inventory limits; -09 Use of pesticides prohibited; -10 Pesticide presence; -11 Operations manual; -12 Restricted access areas; -13 Dispensary display areas; -14 Usable marijuana take-back; -15 Waste disposal; -16 Recall procedures; -17 Surveillance; -18 Alarm systems; -21 Personnel record retention; -22 Incidents; -23 Advertising and marketing; -24 Strain/brand names; -24.1 Medical cannabinoid product formulation; -24.2 Cannabinoid edible product; -25 Usable marijuana packaging; -26 Manufacturing facility labeling; -27 Dispensary labeling; -28 Removal of product labels; -29 Transportation authorization; -30 Transportation requirements; -31 Inspections/compliance; -32 Plan of correction; -33 Data reporting; -34 Law-enforcement reportable incidents; -35 Adverse reactions; -36 Laboratory procurement; -44 Compliance testing for medical cannabinoid products; -44.1 Terpene analysis; -45 Batch requirements for compliance testing; -46 Manufacturing facility labeling/storing (section numbering as returned — Verify exact numbering against the current NDAC PDF, as the search index shows both old and amended numbering).

2025 NDAC amendments: HHS told the Administrative Rules Committee it amended ch. 33-44-01 to implement 2025 legislation: resident patient fee $20.25 → $40 with the move to a two-year card; new $40 nonresident application fee; lost/replacement-card fee changes; edible-product labelling/marketing/ingredient/testing rules; inventory/reserve-sample changes (secondary Citizen Portal report of the committee hearing — treated as secondary; the rule-change PDF on ndlegis.gov — https://ndlegis.gov/sites/default/files/rule-changes/changes/dhhs061225changes.pdf — was returned in search but not opened in full in this session, Verify).

Manufacturing-facility labelling, NDAC §33-44-01-26 (verbatim warnings, via Cornell LII mirror of NDAC — confirm against NDAC PDF):

“This product is not approved by the Food and Drug Administration to treat, cure, or prevent any disease.” / “For use by North Dakota registered qualifying patients only.” / “Keep out of reach of children.” / “It is illegal to drive or to be in actual physical control of a motor vehicle while under the influence of marijuana.”

Plus, per §33-44-01-26: manufacturer name + registry certification number, container ID, batch/process-lot number, harvest/manufacture date, strain name, net weight, total THC + total CBD as identified by the department-selected laboratory (§33-44-01-36), activation time (words/pictogram), expiration date, universal symbol, serving size/servings, ingredients in descending order (for medical cannabinoid products).

1.2.7 ND Administrative Code — Hemp Products, art. 7-20

NDAC art. 7-20 (Agriculture — Hemp Products) is the rule article cited in secondary sources for hemp-product labelling/testing. The full text of art. 7-20 was NOT opened in this session. Secondary summaries state it requires: all cannabinoid hemp products tested for total THC; label stating “This product is not for minors”; no marketing to/encouraging minors to purchase hemp products; and (in a proposed-rule report, NOT adopted text) a 5 mg total THC per serving (adopted, NDAC §7-20-01-01(2)) / CBD:THC >15:1 proposal that was reported as a proposal only — do NOT cite the 5 mg (adopted NDAC figure) figure as law. The adopted labelling items reported for the July 2023 (effective 1 July, year per secondary report — Verify year) NDDA hemp-product rules are: all ingredients, allergens, expiration date, recommended serving suggestion, maximum total THC in milligrams, nutritional panel, consumer warnings, minor-access prevention messages, no health claims. All art. 7-20 specifics are Verify against the current NDAC art. 7-20 PDF before Run 2.

1.2.8 Attorney General opinions — labelled as OPINIONS, not law

No ND Attorney General opinion on delta-8, hemp, or medical marijuana was located as a full official text in this session. Secondary sources conflate ND with other states' AG opinions (e.g. a December 2021 AG statement about delta-8 in the search results belongs to a different state context in one source, and a Kansas AG opinion is cited for Kansas). The ND AG enforcement action reported in secondary sources — a settlement with Delta 8 Resellers (delta8resellers.com) for alleged consumer-protection violations (JD Supra, State AG Report 25 May 2023) — is an enforcement settlement, not an opinion and not a statute. Do NOT cite any “ND AG opinion that delta-8 is illegal” as law or as an opinion unless the official AG opinion letter is located on attorneygeneral.nd.gov. The delta-8 ban in ND rests on statute (HB 1045 (2021) + SB 2096 (2023)), not on an AG opinion located here. Flagged Verify for Run 2: search attorneygeneral.nd.gov opinions index for “hemp,” “delta-8,” “tetrahydrocannabinol,” “medical marijuana.”

1.3 Possession penalties, cultivation, paraphernalia, public consumption, medical product forms

1.3.1 Possession penalties for NON-PATIENTS — NDCC §19-03.1-23(7) (as amended by 2025 HB 1367)

Verbatim (enrolled HB 1367, §19-03.1-23(7)(d) — the marijuana/THC tiers; general rule in (7)(b) is Class A misdemeanour first offence / Class C felony second-or-subsequent for non-marijuana/THC substances, which does NOT apply to the marijuana/THC tiers below):

“d. A person who violates this subsection by possessing: (1) Marijuana: (a) In an amount of less than one-half ounce [14.175 grams] is guilty of an infraction. (b) At least one-half ounce [14.175 grams] but not more than 500 grams of marijuana is guilty of a class B misdemeanor. (c) More than 500 grams of marijuana is guilty of a class A misdemeanor. (2) Tetrahydrocannabinol: (a) In an amount less than two grams is guilty of an infraction. (b) At least two grams but not more than six grams of tetrahydrocannabinol is guilty of a class B misdemeanor. (c) More than six grams of tetrahydrocannabinol is guilty of a class A misdemeanor.”
Substance / amount (non-patient possession)ClassificationSentence exposure (NDCC ch. 12.1-32 — general sentencing, NOT quoted verbatim here; Verify maxima)
Marijuana < ½ oz (14.175 g)InfractionNo jail; fine up to $1,000 (infraction maximum per §12.1-32-01 — Verify)
Marijuana ≥ ½ oz to 500 gClass B misdemeanourUp to 30 days jail; fine up to $1,500 (Verify)
Marijuana > 500 gClass A misdemeanourUp to 360 days jail; fine up to $3,000 (Verify)
THC < 2 gInfractionNo jail; fine up to $1,000 (Verify)
THC 2 g to 6 gClass B misdemeanourUp to 30 days; up to $1,500 (Verify)
THC > 6 gClass A misdemeanourUp to 360 days; up to $3,000 (Verify)

History of the small-amount reduction: Possession of <½ oz was reduced from a misdemeanour to an infraction in 2019 (HB 1155 lineage is NOT the vehicle — HB 1155 failed in 2019; the 2019 decriminalisation-style change is widely attributed to 2019 legislation, but the specific 2019 session law number was NOT confirmed in this session — Verify; do not attribute the infraction tier to HB 1155 or HB 1596, both of which failed). The infraction is a criminal infraction, not a civil citation — HB 1596 (2025) would have converted it to a noncriminal $150 citation and failed (see §1.9). School-property enhancement does NOT apply to marijuana/THC: §19-03.1-23(7)(c): possession on real property comprising a public/private elementary or secondary school or public career/technical school is a Class B felony “unless the offense involves marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol.” Under-21 first-offence ≤½ oz: §19-03.1-23(12) (per FindLaw summary): court may also sentence the individual to an evidence-based alcohol and drug education programme — Verify full text. Ingestion: §19-03.1-22.3 is referenced in HB 1596/HB 1201 titles; whether ingestion of marijuana/THC by a non-patient carries a separate penalty, and its classification, is Verify — not stated here. Patients exceeding limits: a registered patient possessing MORE than the allowable amount, or a non-allowed form, is outside ch. 19-24.1 protection for the excess; the excess is governed by §19-03.1-23. The precise interaction (whether the whole possession or only the excess is charged) is Verify.

1.3.2 Manufacture / delivery / cultivation penalties — NDCC §19-03.1-23(1)

Verbatim (FindLaw mirror of current §19-03.1-23(1), confirmed in structure by HB 1367-era text):

“1. Except as authorized by this chapter, it is unlawful for a person to willfully, as defined in section 12.1-02-02, manufacture, deliver, or possess with intent to manufacture or deliver, a controlled substance, or to deliver, distribute, or dispense a controlled substance by means of the internet… A person who violates this subsection with respect to: a. A controlled substance classified in schedule I or II which is a narcotic drug, or methamphetamine, is guilty of a class B felony. b. Any other controlled substance classified in schedule I, II, or III, or a controlled substance analog, except marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol is guilty of a class B felony. c. Marijuana, tetrahydrocannabinol, or a substance classified in schedule IV, is guilty of a class C felony. d. A substance classified in schedule V, is guilty of a class A misdemeanor.”

Home grow: PROHIBITED. No provision in ch. 19-24.1 authorises a patient, caregiver or any non-manufacturing-facility person to plant, cultivate, grow or harvest marijuana. Cultivation by a non-licensee is “manufacture” under §19-03.1-23(1)(c) (Class C felony for marijuana/THC, before any enhancement or separate cultivation provision — Verify whether a separate cultivation-specific section exists; none was located). This applies even to registered patients. Only a registered manufacturing facility, in an enclosed, locked facility not visible from the street/public areas, may produce/process marijuana (§19-24.1-14 application description, §1.6). Delivery/sale by a non-dispensary: Class C felony under (1)(c) above, plus any separate delivery-to-minor or school-zone provision — Verify enhancements.

1.3.3 Paraphernalia — NDCC ch. 19-03.4, §19-03.4-03 (as amended in subsection 1 by 2025 HB 1367)

Verbatim (LawServer mirror of §19-03.4-03, subsections 1–4 as previously enacted; HB 1367's subsection-1 amendment wording is Verify):

“1. A person may not use or possess with intent to use drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, or conceal a controlled substance in violation of chapter 19-03.1. A person violating this subsection is guilty of a class C felony if the drug paraphernalia is used, or possessed with intent to be used, to manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, or analyze a controlled substance, other than marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol, classified in schedule I, II, or III of chapter 19-03.1.”
“2. A person may not use or possess with the intent to use drug paraphernalia to inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise induce into the human body a controlled substance, other than marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol, classified in schedule I, II, or III of chapter 19-03.1. A person violating this subsection is guilty of a class A misdemeanor. If a person previously has been convicted of an offense under this title, other than an offense related to marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol, or an equivalent offense from another court in the United States, a violation of this subsection is a class C felony.”
“3. A person may not use or possess with intent to use drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, or repack marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol in violation of chapter 19-03.1. A person violating this subsection is guilty of a class A misdemeanor.”
“4. A person may not use or possess with the intent to use drug paraphernalia to ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol or possess with the intent to use drug paraphernalia to store or contain marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol in violation of chapter 19-03.1. A person violating this subsection is guilty of an infraction.”

Summary table: marijuana/THC production paraphernalia = Class A misdemeanour; marijuana/THC personal-use or storage paraphernalia = infraction; non-marijuana Schedule I–III production paraphernalia = Class C felony; non-marijuana personal-use paraphernalia = Class A misdemeanour (Class C felony on a qualifying prior). Delivery/manufacture of paraphernalia and delivery to a minor (≥3 years younger) are in §§19-03.4-04–05 — Verify classifications (secondary summary: delivery generally Class C felony; delivery to a minor ≥3 years younger Class C felony — not quoted verbatim here). Patient paraphernalia: a registered patient's use/possession of paraphernalia for lawful medical use within ch. 19-24.1 is not “in violation of chapter 19-03.1” and is therefore outside §19-03.4-03 by its own terms — but the statute does not contain an express patient-paraphernalia exemption sentence located here; Verify whether §19-24.1-32 or NDAC addresses patient paraphernalia expressly.

1.3.4 Cross-reference

Manufacture/delivery penalties are in §1.3.2 above — not repeated here to avoid divergence. If a later part cites a manufacture penalty, it must match §1.3.2 (Class C felony for marijuana/THC).

1.3.5 Public consumption and other limitations — NDCC §19-24.1-33 (Limitations)

Verbatim (operative list, FindLaw mirror of §19-24.1-33, current text as of 1 Jan 2024 mirror):

“This chapter does not authorize a person to engage in, and does not prevent the imposition of any civil liability or criminal liability or other penalties for engaging in the following conduct: 1. Undertaking an activity under the influence of marijuana if doing so would constitute negligence or professional malpractice. 2. Possessing or consuming usable marijuana: a. On a school bus or school van that is used for school purposes; b. On the grounds of any public or private school; c. At any location while a public or private school sanctioned event is occurring at that location; d. On the grounds of a correctional facility; or e. On the grounds of a child care facility or licensed home day care, unless authorized under rules adopted by the department. [subsection 3 in the SB 2344 original: undertaking any activity prohibited by the state smoking provisions §§23-12-09, 23-12-10, 23-12-10.2, 23-12-10.4, 23-12-10.5, 23-12-11 — i.e. the statewide smoke-free / public-place smoking rules apply to marijuana smoking/vaping as they do to tobacco — Verify current subsection numbering, as the FindLaw excerpt jumps from 2 to 4] 4. Using a combustible delivery form of usable marijuana or vaporizing usable marijuana under this chapter if the smoke or vapor would be inhaled by a minor who is not the registered qualifying patient for whom the usable marijuana is intended. 5. Operating, navigating, or being in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft, train, or motorboat, while under the influence of marijuana.”

Plain English for patients: No general “public consumption” permission exists. Smoking/vaping in a place where tobacco smoking is prohibited is prohibited for marijuana too (via the cross-referenced smoking sections). Possession/consumption on school, correctional and childcare grounds is expressly not authorised. Driving etc. while under the influence is expressly not authorised — and ND has NO per se THC blood limit in §19-24.1-33 (impairment-based; any per se driving provision elsewhere is Verify — Part 12, Run 2). For non-patients: any public consumption is simply unlawful possession/use under ch. 19-03.1; there is no lawful public-consumption venue, lounge or event exception in the statutes read.

1.3.6 Allowed medical product forms and THC limits — NDCC §19-24.1-01

Verbatim definitions (current compiled text, official PDF):

  • Dried leaves/flowers (combustible delivery form): purchase ≤2.5 oz / 30 days (standard) or ≤6 oz / 30 days (enhanced, cancer only); possession at any time ≤3 oz (standard) or ≤7.5 oz (enhanced). See §1.4.5.
  • Cannabinoid concentrate: “a concentrate or extract obtained by separating cannabinoids from marijuana by a mechanical, chemical, or other process. A container holding a cannabinoid concentrate for dispensing may not exceed one gram.” (§19-24.1-01(7))
  • Cannabinoid solution: “a solution consisting of a mixture created from cannabinoid concentrate and other ingredients. A container holding a cannabinoid solution for dispensing may not exceed thirty milliliters.” (§19-24.1-01(9))
  • Cannabinoid capsule: “a small, soluble container, usually made of gelatin, which encloses a dose of a cannabinoid product or a cannabinoid concentrate intended for consumption. The maximum concentration of amount of tetrahydrocannabinol permitted in a serving of a cannabinoid capsule is fifty milligrams.” (§19-24.1-01(6))
  • Cannabinoid topical: “a cannabinoid product intended to be applied to the skin or hair. The maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol permitted in a cannabinoid topical is six percent.” (§19-24.1-01(10))
  • Cannabinoid transdermal patch: “an adhesive substance applied to the skin which contains a cannabinoid product or cannabinoid concentrate for absorption into the bloodstream. The maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol permitted in a serving of a cannabinoid transdermal patch is fifty milligrams.” (§19-24.1-01(11))
  • Cannabinoid edible product (2025, HB 1203): “a soft or hard lozenge in a geometric square shape into which a cannabinoid concentrate or the dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis is incorporated. a. The maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol permitted in a serving of a cannabinoid edible product is five milligrams and in a package is fifty milligrams. b. The term does not include a hard or soft lozenge in a geometric square shape into which a cannabinoid concentrate or the dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis is incorporated if the form, packaging, or labeling is target marketed to minors.” (§19-24.1-01(8))
  • Medical cannabinoid products are LIMITED to: “(1) Cannabinoid solution; (2) Cannabinoid capsule; (3) Cannabinoid transdermal patch; (4) Cannabinoid topical; and (5) Cannabinoid edible products.” (§19-24.1-01(26)(a)) — and “does not include: (1) A cannabinoid concentrate by itself; or (2) The dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis by itself.” (§19-24.1-01(26)(b))
  • “Maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol” means “the total amount of tetrahydrocannabinol and tetrahydrocannabinolic acid in a medical cannabinoid product or a cannabinoid concentrate.” (§19-24.1-01(25)) — i.e. THC + THCA, not delta-9 alone.
  • Pediatric medical marijuana: “a medical marijuana product containing cannabidiol which may not contain a maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol of more than six percent.” (§19-24.1-01(35))

HHS product-types summary (official HHS document, Aug 2025, “Products and Limits Information”): Types of usable marijuana are: (1) Dried Leaves and Flowers — adults only; (2) Cannabinoid Concentrates or Extracts (examples include vapes — HHS wording, not statute); (3) Medical Cannabinoid Products. Minors: cannot have dried leaves/flowers; cannot have any product with >6% THC. Purchase limits (HHS, Aug 2025): up to 2.5 oz flower / 30 days (6 oz enhanced, cancer); up to 6,000 mg THC total of other products / 30 days, including an edible daily purchase limit of 310 mg edible-THC purchase limit (HHS reads as daily; the statute does not say “per day”). Possession limits (HHS, Aug 2025): up to 3 oz flower (7.5 oz enhanced); up to 500 mg THC in a cannabinoid edible product at any time; “no other possession limit exists” for other products beyond the purchase limit (HHS wording — read with §19-24.1-01(3)(c), which imposes the 6,000 mg / 30-day purchase cap, not a possession cap, for concentrates/products). Source: HHS Products and Limits Information (PDF) — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Products%20and%20Limits%20Information.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

What is NOT an allowed medical form: Any food, beverage, gummy, chocolate, baked good, drink or other edible form other than the square lozenge above; any product form not listed in §19-24.1-01(26)(a); smokable flower for minors; any product >6% THC for minors. Vapes are concentrates/extracts in HHS's product-type list, but the statute's own form list treats concentrates separately — Verify whether NDAC §33-44-01-24.1 (formulation) restricts vape hardware/flavours; not stated here.

1.4 Medical programme

1.4.1 Qualifying (debilitating medical) conditions — NDCC §19-24.1-01(16) (verbatim list)

“16. 'Debilitating medical condition' means one of the following: a. Cancer; b. Positive status for human immunodeficiency virus; c. Acquired immune deficiency syndrome; d. Decompensated cirrhosis caused by hepatitis C; e. Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis; f. Posttraumatic stress disorder; g. Agitation of Alzheimer's disease or related dementia; h. Crohn's disease; i. Fibromyalgia; j. Spinal stenosis or chronic back pain, including neuropathy or damage to the nervous tissue of the spinal cord with objective neurological indication of intractable spasticity; k. Glaucoma; l. Epilepsy; m. Anorexia nervosa; n. Bulimia nervosa; o. Anxiety disorder; p. Tourette syndrome; q. Ehlers-Danlos syndrome; r. Endometriosis; s. Interstitial cystitis; t. Neuropathy; u. Migraine; v. Rheumatoid arthritis; w. Autism spectrum disorder; x. A brain injury; y. A terminal illness; or z. A chronic or debilitating disease or medical condition or treatment for such disease or medical condition that produces one or more of the following: (1) Cachexia or wasting syndrome; (2) Severe debilitating pain that has not responded to previously prescribed medication or surgical measures for more than three months or for which other treatment options produced serious side effects; (3) Intractable nausea; (4) Seizures; or (5) Severe and persistent muscle spasms, including those characteristic of multiple sclerosis.”

Petition to add a condition: The chapter provides a petition process (public petition, department review) — the precise section, signature threshold (secondary sources say 50 practitioner signatures; Verify section number and threshold) and 180-day decision period are Verify against current §§19-24.1-02/19-24.1-32. Do not cite the 50-signature figure as statute text here. PTSD definition: §19-24.1-01(38): patient meets DSM-5-TR (2013 text revision as cited) diagnostic criteria for PTSD.

1.4.2 Practitioner certification — bona fide relationship, who may certify, telehealth

Who: “Health care provider” means “a physician, a physician assistant, or an advanced practice registered nurse” (§19-24.1-01(21)); physician = licensed under ch. 43-17; physician assistant = licensed under ch. 43-17; APRN = defined under §43-12.1-02. Bona fide provider-patient relationship — §19-24.1-01(4) (verbatim, operative elements):

“a. The health care provider has reviewed the patient's relevant medical records and completed a full assessment of the patient's medical history and current medical condition, including a relevant medical evaluation of the patient. b. The health care provider has created and maintained records of the patient's condition in accordance with medically accepted standards. c. The patient is under the health care provider's continued care for the debilitating medical condition that qualifies the patient for the medical use of marijuana. d. The health care provider has a reasonable expectation that provider will continue to provide followup care to the patient to monitor the medical use of marijuana as a treatment of the patient's debilitating medical condition. e. The relationship is not for the sole purpose of providing written certification for the medical use of marijuana.”

Written certification contents — §19-24.1-03(2)(b) (verbatim list): provider practice name/address/telephone; ND licence number; medical/nursing specialty; applicant name + date of birth; debilitating condition + medical justification; attestation of bona fide relationship; whether the provider authorises an enhanced amount of dried leaves/flowers in combustible form for cancer; provider signature + date. Telehealth: SB 2294 (2025) struck “in person” from the relevant evaluation provision in committee (secondary committee report) and HB 1203/SB 2294 summaries state telehealth is now allowed for first-time certification. The exact current statutory telehealth sentence is Verify — the compiled §19-24.1-01(4) text read here does not contain the word “telehealth” in the bona fide definition, so telehealth permission, if any, sits elsewhere (possibly §19-24.1-03 or NDAC) and must be quoted from the enrolled SB 2294 text in Run 2. Do not state telehealth as unqualified law on the basis of secondary summaries alone. No provider is required to participate: HHS programme guidance (secondary report quoting programme director Jason Wahl, 2025) states the state cannot require a provider to participate — treated as programme guidance, not statute.

1.4.3 Patient and caregiver registry cards and fees

Patient — §19-24.1-03(1): “A qualifying patient is not eligible to purchase, use, or possess usable marijuana under the medical marijuana program unless the qualifying patient has a valid registry identification card.” Patient application fee — §19-24.1-03(2)(a): “A nonrefundable application fee in an amount not to exceed forty dollars for a registry identification card valid for two years.” (2025, SB 2294 — up from the prior $25 / one-year card; NDAC 2025 amendments report the resident fee moved $20.25 → $40 — the $20.25 figure is the NDAC fee actually charged before 2025, the $25 figure is the prior statutory cap in secondary summaries; both are noted here to avoid presenting either as the current fee. Current fee to charge: not to exceed $40, two-year card.) Patient application contents — §19-24.1-03(2)(c)–(e): name/address/date of birth; proposed designated caregiver name/address/date of birth if any; photographic copy of ND identification (available for inspection/verification on request; if the applicant cannot obtain current ND identification due to a medical condition, other department-approved documentation may be submitted — SB 2293); if a minor, certified birth record or photographic ND identification + parent/legal guardian signature (guardian with health-care decision responsibility); signed consent for release of medical information; recent 2×2-inch photograph.

Caregiver: “Designated caregiver” means “an individual who is at least twenty-one years of age and agrees to manage the well-being of a registered qualifying patient with respect to the qualifying patient's medical use of marijuana.” (§19-24.1-01(18)) Caregiver application fee: prior statutory text was $50/year (2021 HB 1359 engrossment: “A nonrefundable annual application fee in the amount of fifty dollars”) — Verify current caregiver fee and term in current §19-24.1-04 (the 2025 two-year change may or may not have applied to caregivers; the enrolled SB 2294 text must be checked). Caregiver must have a separate registry card for each registered qualifying patient (§19-24.1-05, per SB 2388 text). Patient may have no more than five registered designated caregivers (§19-24.1-05(10)/(11) numbering varies by engrossment — Verify subsection number). Issuance: department shall issue a registry identification card within 30 calendar days of approving an application or renewal (§19-24.1-05, per SB 2388 text). Denial/revocation/reapplication: department shall deny if requirements not met, information not provided, card previously revoked, or false/material misstatement; reapplication barred 1 year from initial revocation, 5 years from second revocation, disqualified on third revocation (SB 2388 text — Verify current §19-24.1-05 subsections).

Senior / hospice pathway (2023 HB 1478, as engrossed): In lieu of written certification under §19-24.1-03, an individual 65+ may submit a department form identifying a qualifying diagnosis; an individual admitted to a hospice programme (ch. 23-17.4) may submit medical records showing hospice admission; department may use the form/records in place of certification to approve/deny under §19-24.1-05; hospice patient fee waived. Verify enrolled HB 1478 text for the final age threshold (65+ in engrossment; some summaries say the senior provision was narrowed/removed in conference) and the issuance deadline (14 vs. 30 days in engrossment). Do not rely on the engrossed version alone. Veterans pathway (§19-24.1-03.1): In lieu of written certification, a veteran may submit VA medical records / discharge documentation as specified in that section (pre-2025 text; SB 2294 amended the section — Verify current text and whether the veterans pathway still exists in its prior form).

1.4.4 Cross-reference

Fees for compassion centers are in Part 4 scope; the $5,000 / $90,000 / $110,000 figures are noted in §1.2.2 and must be verified against current §§19-24.1-14–15 in Run 2 (see Part 4, §4.6 for the three fee layers).

1.4.5 Purchase and possession limits by product type and period — NDCC §19-24.1-01(3) (verbatim, operative)

“3. 'Allowable amount of usable marijuana' means the amount of usable marijuana a registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver may purchase in a thirty-day period under this chapter. a. Except as provided under subdivision b: (1) During a thirty-day period, a registered qualifying patient may not purchase or have purchased by a registered designated caregiver more than two and one-half ounces [70.87 grams] of dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis in a combustible delivery form. (2) At any time a registered qualifying patient, or a registered designated caregiver on behalf of a registered qualifying patient, may not possess more than three ounces [85.05 grams] of dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis in a combustible delivery form. (3) At any time, a registered qualifying patient, or a registered designated caregiver on behalf of a registered qualifying patient, may not possess more than five hundred milligrams of a cannabinoid edible product. b. Notwithstanding subdivision a, if a registered qualifying patient has a registry identification card authorizing an enhanced allowable amount: (1) During a thirty-day period a registered qualifying patient may not purchase or have purchased by a registered designated caregiver more than six ounces [170.01 grams] of dried leaves or flowers of the plant of genus cannabis in a combustible delivery form. (2) At any time a registered qualifying patient, or a registered designated caregiver on behalf of a registered qualifying patient, may not possess more than seven and one-half ounces [212.62 grams] of dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis in a combustible delivery form. (3) At any time, a registered qualifying patient, or a registered designated caregiver on behalf of a registered qualifying patient, may not possess more than five hundred milligrams of a cannabinoid edible product. c. A registered qualifying patient may not purchase or have purchased by a registered designated caregiver more than the maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol permitted in a thirty-day period. The maximum concentration or amount of tetrahydrocannabinol permitted in a thirty-day period for a cannabinoid concentrate or medical cannabinoid product, or the cumulative total of both, is six thousand milligrams. At any time, a registered qualifying patient, or a registered designated caregiver on behalf of a registered qualifying patient, may not purchase more than three hundred ten milligrams of tetrahydrocannabinol in the form of a cannabinoid edible product.”
LimitStandard patientEnhanced patient (cancer, provider-authorised on the card)
Flower purchase / 30 days2.5 oz (70.87 g)6 oz (170.01 g)
Flower possession at any time3 oz (85.05 g)7.5 oz (212.62 g)
Concentrate + medical cannabinoid product THC purchase / 30 days (cumulative)6,000 mg6,000 mg (no enhanced non-flower amount in §19-24.1-01(3))
Edible possession at any time500 mg THC500 mg THC
Edible purchase (per day, per §19-24.1-01(3)(c) last sentence as worded “At any time…may not purchase more than 310 mg” — HHS reads this as a daily purchase limit)310 mg THC / day310 mg THC / day (HHS reading)

Note on the 310 mg wording: The statute sentence reads “At any time…may not purchase more than three hundred ten milligrams” — HHS's Aug 2025 limits document glosses this as a “daily purchase limit of 310 mg of THC per day” for edibles. The discrepancy between “at any time” and “per day” is flagged Verify — the HHS reading is the operational rule patients see in the limit meter, but the statutory wording should be quoted as written above. Note on older figures to NOT use: 2,000 mg (pre-Aug 2023 non-flower limit) and 4,000 mg (appearing in one secondary guide for 2026) are superseded/incorrect against current §19-24.1-01(3)(c) (6,000 mg). Do not use them in Run 2. “Usable marijuana” for minors: “In the case of a registered qualifying patient who is a minor, 'usable marijuana' is limited to pediatric medical marijuana.” (SB 2294 engrossment amending §19-24.1-01(47) / usable-marijuana definition — Verify subsection number in compiled text.)

1.4.6 Minors' rules

“Minor” means “an individual under the age of nineteen.” (§19-24.1-01(31)) — i.e. 18-year-olds are minors for this chapter. Minors may only obtain pediatric medical marijuana (≤6% THC, containing CBD) — see §1.3.6. Minors cannot have dried leaves/flowers and cannot have any product >6% THC (HHS product-types document). Minor applications require a parent/legal guardian with health-care decision responsibility to sign, and the department must explain potential risks of pediatric medical marijuana to that parent/guardian (per SB 2388-era §19-24.1-05 text — Verify current wording). Combustible/vaporised use whose smoke/vapour would be inhaled by a minor who is not the patient is not authorised (§19-24.1-33(4)). Caregivers for minors: a minor patient must have a designated caregiver (parent/guardian) purchase on the patient's behalf; the caregiver must be 21+.

1.4.7 Out-of-state / visiting patients — and the new nonresident pathway

Prior rule (pre-2025, HHS guidance): “Patients and Caregivers must have a ND registry identification card. (We do not recognize other state's cards.) Patients and Caregivers must purchase their product from registered ND dispensaries.” (HHS Division presentation, Mar 2018 — historical guidance; the non-recognition rule was the programme's stated position before SB 2294.) 2025 change — SB 2294 creates a new section for “qualifying nonresident medical marijuana patients.” Secondary summaries describe an electronic card for nonresidents valid for 60 days, with a $40 nonresident application fee (NDAC 2025 amendments). The enrolled SB 2294 text for this new section was NOT read in full in this session, and the current compiled §19-24.1 text section number for nonresidents was not confirmed. Therefore: nonresident eligibility criteria, required out-of-state card/certification, fee, duration (60 days per secondary reports), purchase limits for nonresidents, and whether a nonresident may use a caregiver are ALL Verify against the enrolled SB 2294 text and current ch. 19-24.1 before any Run 2 statement. Do NOT state “visitors can use their home-state card in ND” — that was false before 2025 and remains unconfirmed in its new form. What can legally be brought INTO ND: Nothing in ch. 19-24.1 authorises import of marijuana from another state, even by a registered ND patient or a nonresident patient. Product must be purchased from an ND-registered dispensary. Interstate detail is Part 10, Run 2. See also HHS out-of-state page: https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/out-of-state — Accessed 2026-10-30.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

1.5.1 Hemp definition and THC measurement (state law)

Definition: §4.1-18.1-01(4) — see §1.2.4 verbatim. Key point: ND does NOT fix a percentage in the definition itself; it delegates the total-THC amount to the Agriculture Commissioner, capped at the federal 2018 Farm Bill ceiling (§4.1-18.1-04.1). In practice NDDA applies 0.3% total THC (flower <0.3% total THC; cannabinoid products tested for total THC with COA available). Measurement: Total THC = (THCa × 0.877) + delta-9 THC, by weight (§4.1-18.1-01(9)). NDDA's worked example (official page): THCa 25% + delta-9 0.2% = (25 × 0.877) + 0.2 = 22.13% total THC — illegal in ND despite a “0.2% delta-9” label claim. “Tetrahydrocannabinol” for hemp purposes INCLUDES delta-7, delta-8 and delta-10 isomers (§4.1-18.1-01(8)) — so a product cannot evade the total-THC calculation by labelling its THC as delta-8 or delta-10. The “0.1% ND limit” claim in some secondary CBD guides is NOT supported by the statute or NDDA page read here. NDDA states 0.3% total THC for flower and commissioner-determined amounts for products, capped at the federal ceiling. Do NOT cite 0.1% as ND law. Source: NDDA Hemp page — https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp — Accessed 2026-10-30.

1.5.2 Delta-8, delta-9, THCA and other hemp-derived THC — legal status table

Product / cannabinoidND status (statute + NDDA guidance)Basis
Delta-8 THC (any source, natural or converted)Prohibited. Excluded from hemp commodity/product; Schedule I tetrahydrocannabinol (delta-6 = delta-8 in §19-03.1-05).§4.1-18.1-01(6)(b)(3); §4.1-18.1-04.3; §19-03.1-05(m)(1)(b); SB 2096 (2023); HB 1045 (2021) conversion ban
Delta-10 THCProhibited as a converted/psychotropic product. Included in “tetrahydrocannabinol” definition (delta-10 isomer); conversion of CBD → delta-10 banned for licensees; delta-9-only psychotropic logic applies (NDDA). A naturally occurring trace amount within a lawful full-spectrum product under the total-THC cap is a different question — Verify whether NDDA treats trace delta-10 in full-spectrum CBD as lawful.§4.1-18.1-01(8); §4.1-18.1-04.3
Delta-9 THC as a standalone product (drink, edible, tincture marketed for its delta-9)Not legal in ND per NDDA: “If a product contains only Delta-9 and no other cannabinoids this product is deemed to be psychotropic and is not legal in North Dakota.” This is NDDA guidance applying the commissioner's psychotropic-disapproval power, not a sentence in §4.1-18.1-01 that names delta-9-only products — flag the distinction. A full-spectrum CBD/CBG product that naturally contains trace delta-9 within the total-THC cap is lawful (see below).NDDA Hemp page, FAQ 3 (official guidance)
THCA flower / THCA productsEffectively prohibited at market potencies. THCA flower must be <0.3% total THC; THCA vape products are banned as hemp-extract inhalants. Typical THCA flower (>20% total THC by the (THCa×0.877)+delta-9 formula) is illegal in ND. THCA itself is not separately named in §4.1-18.1-01's prohibited list — its illegality flows from the total-THC calculation, not a THCA-specific ban.NDDA Hemp page, FAQs 6–7; §4.1-18.1-01(9)
