CBD / CBG — broad spectrum, full spectrum, isolateLawful if total THC does not exceed the commissioner's amount (0.3% framework) and the product is not prohibited on another ground (inhalant, medical claim, dietary supplement — see below).§4.1-18.1-01(6)(a)(4); NDDA Allowed list
CBN in tinctures/topicals/ediblesListed as lawful by NDDA (“Tinctures, topicals, and edibles that contain CBD, CBG, and CBN if these products do not contain any synthetic cannabinoids”) — NDDA guidance, not a separate statutory CBN definition. Verify against NDAC art. 7-20.NDDA Hemp page, FAQ 2
Cured hemp flower (untreated)Lawful if from legally harvested hemp, <0.3% total THC, not treated with any extracted cannabinoid.NDDA Allowed list + FAQ 5
Hemp seed (incapable of germination, for human consumption), hemp oil/hearts/protein (FDA GRAS), fibre/textiles/rope/paper/hempcrete/building materialsLawful.NDDA Allowed list; §4.1-18.1-01(6)(a)
Hemp-extract inhalants (vapes, vape cartridges, disposables — any cannabinoid)Prohibited. “A chemical compound extracted from hemp used to formulate, process, or otherwise make an inhalant” is excluded from hemp commodity/product. This covers CBD-only vapes too.§4.1-18.1-01(6)(b)(2); NDDA FAQ 2
Hemp dietary supplements; products with medical claimsProhibited per NDDA Prohibited list. Statutory hook (NDAC art. 7-20 / FDA GRAS framework) is Verify — NDDA lists them as prohibited, but the §4.1-18.1-01 text read does not itself name “dietary supplements” or “medical claims” in the (6)(b) list.NDDA Prohibited list (guidance)
Hemp that has been chopped/separated/dried for transfer/storageExcluded from “hemp commodity or product” as defined — a definitional exclusion whose practical scope (does it ban sale of dried hemp biomass?) is Verify; do not over-read it as a ban on all dried hemp.§4.1-18.1-01(6)(b)(1)

1.5.3 Minimum age for hemp products

NDAC art. 7-20 (per secondary summaries) requires the label “This product is not for minors” and prohibits marketing to / encouraging minors to purchase hemp products. No statutory minimum purchase age (e.g. 21) for lawful CBD/CBG hemp products was located in §4.1-18.1 in this session. Do NOT state a 21+ purchase age for hemp CBD in ND — that figure appears in some secondary guides by analogy to other states, but ND's hemp statute as read sets a labelling/marketing rule, not a 21+ sales ban. Verify NDAC art. 7-20 for any age rule, and distinguish: medical marijuana minors = under 19 (§19-24.1-01(31)); hemp “minors” for labelling = undefined in the material read (presumably under 18 or under 19 — Verify). THC beverages: No lawful THC-beverage category exists in ND. A delta-9 drink/edible that is only delta-9 is psychotropic and not legal per NDDA (FAQ 3); a CBD/CBG beverage within the total-THC cap and not making medical claims would fall under the general cannabinoid-product rules, but no ND statute or NDAC section specifically authorising “THC beverages” was located. Verify NDAC art. 7-20 beverage provisions before Run 2, Part 12.

1.5.4 Testing and labelling for hemp products (state law + NDDA guidance)

Testing: “North Dakota Century Code requires that all cannabinoid hemp products sold in North Dakota must be tested for Total THC. All products offered for sale must have a certificate of analysis (COA) available for review.” (NDDA Hemp page, FAQ 7 — NDDA states this as a Century Code requirement; the specific §4.1-18.1 testing section number is Verify.) Labelling (NDDA July-rules summary, secondary report — Verify against NDAC art. 7-20): all ingredients, allergens, expiration date, recommended serving suggestion, maximum total THC in milligrams, nutritional panel, consumer warnings, minor-access prevention messages, no health claims; child-resistant, tamper-evident packaging is reported for the proposed rule but the adopted packaging standard is Verify. Do NOT cite the reported proposed-rule figures (5 mg total THC per serving (adopted, NDAC §7-20-01-01(2)), CBD:THC >15:1) as law — they were a proposal reported by an industry group, with no adoption confirmed in this session.

1.5.5 Hemp vs. marijuana boundary in ND law

Hemp as regulated under §4.1-18.1-01 is expressly carved OUT of “marijuana” in §19-24.1-01(24)(a) and out of Schedule I tetrahydrocannabinols in §19-03.1-05(m)(2)(a) (“the allowable amount of total tetrahydrocannabinol found in hemp or an allowed hemp commodity or product”). Anything above the allowable total THC, or any prohibited hemp product, falls back into marijuana / Schedule I THC and the §19-03.1-23 penalties.

1.5.6 ND response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates

No ND response was located. No NDDA bulletin, HHS notice, NDAC emergency rule, or 2025/2026 bill in the sources read addresses PL 119-37 §781 (12 Nov 2026) or PL 119-103 §2019 (11 Dec 2026), the federal total-THC definition, or the federal 0.4 mg total THC per container cap. Analysis (not a located ND rule): ND law ALREADY uses a total-THC calculation ((THCa×0.877)+delta-9) and ALREADY bans the main intoxicating-hemp categories the federal change targets (delta-8, converted cannabinoids, hemp-extract inhalants). The federal per-container cap (0.4 mg total THC per container after 11 Dec 2026, on the given baseline) is a DIFFERENT metric from ND's percentage-based commissioner amount — whether ND will conform its commissioner amount, adopt a per-container cap, or treat the federal cap as a ceiling/floor is Verify and is the single most important hemp follow-up for Run 2. Until NDDA or the legislature acts, ND retailers cannot assume federal compliance = ND compliance, or vice versa: a product under a federal per-container cap could still be psychotropic/disapproved in ND, and a product lawful under ND's percentage framework could exceed a federal per-container cap. USDA DEA-lab testing delay to 31 Dec 2026 (given baseline): No NDDA statement on DEA-registered lab testing for ND hemp was located — Verify with NDDA's hemp programme (USDA-approved state plan vs. USDA programme is Part 7, Run 2).

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer rules (medical)

  • Packaging — NDAC §33-44-01-25 (Usable Marijuana Packaging): Section title confirmed; full packaging specifications (opaque? tamper-evident? child-resistant? — secondary guides conflict: one says opaque/tamper-evident/child-resistant is required, another says child-resistant is “strongly encouraged” not required) are Verify against the NDAC PDF. Do NOT state a packaging specification here beyond the section's existence and the labelling contents in §33-44-01-26/27.
  • Labelling — NDAC §§33-44-01-26 (manufacturing facility) and -27 (dispensary): Contents listed in §1.2.6 (manufacturer/dispensary name + certification number, container/batch/process-lot IDs, harvest/manufacture dates, strain, net weight, total THC/CBD per department-selected lab, activation time, expiration, universal symbol, servings, ingredients descending, four consumer warnings). Edible-specific labelling (HB 1203 / NDAC §33-44-01-24.2): black Arial font, THC mg per serving + servings per package, and marketing not targeted to minors (per HB 1203 bill text excerpt — Verify full edible labelling rule in NDAC §33-44-01-24.2).
  • Advertising / marketing — NDAC §33-44-01-23 (Advertising and Marketing) and §33-44-01-24 (Strain or Brand Names): A compassion center's name/logo on labels, signs, websites and informational material provided to registered patients/caregivers may NOT include: (1) images of marijuana or marijuana paraphernalia; (2) colloquial references to marijuana; (3) names of marijuana plant strains; (4) medical symbols bearing a reasonable resemblance to established medical associations (e.g. AMA, AAP) — per NDAC excerpt returned in search (confirm against NDAC PDF). A website may contain: facility name, phone number, and other department-approved information (per same excerpt). All advertising must avoid appealing to minors and misleading claims (secondary summary of NDAC — Verify full §33-44-01-23 text). Edible marketing may not target minors (§19-24.1-01(8)(b), §19-24.1-24.1).
  • Delivery: Dispensary delivery to registered patients/caregivers is permitted in ND (secondary Dutchie guide: “delivery is permitted, and must be accompanied by a manifest completed on a form approved by the department”; NDAC §§33-44-01-29/30 cover transportation authorisation/requirements). The statutory delivery authorisation section in ch. 19-24.1 was NOT quoted verbatim in this session — Verify the delivery section before Run 2 states delivery as unqualified law. No delivery to non-cardholders, no interstate delivery, and no delivery to a school/correctional/childcare ground (see §1.3.5) in any event. Note: Parts 2–3 also flag that delivery legality is described inconsistently across secondary sources (HHS materials describe dispensary delivery; some secondary sources say delivery is illegal) — NOT resolved in Run 1; referred to Part 1.
  • Zoning / buffer — NDCC §19-24.1-14 (compassion-center application): Applicant must provide (1) evidence of approval from local officials as to compliance with local zoning laws for the physical address, and (2) “Evidence the physical address of the proposed compassion center is not located within one thousand feet [304.80 meters] of a property line of a preexisting public or private school.” (Verbatim from SB 2344 / compiled text search excerpt; the older Justia excerpt misprints the metric conversion as 604.80 m — the correct conversion of 1,000 ft is 304.80 m, as in the current text.) Manufacturing-facility applicants must also describe the enclosed, locked facility and steps to ensure production/processing is not visible from the street or other public areas. Local zoning authority itself is Part 3 — not duplicated here except to note the state buffer is a floor, not a local cap.
  • Hemp zoning: No state hemp zoning/buffer rule was located in §4.1-18.1 (hemp is an agricultural crop; local zoning for hemp retail is Part 3 scope).

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking system

BioTrackTHC (now BT Government, Inc. for state traceability, per 2025 secondary reporting). The ND Department of Health awarded BioTrackTHC the medical-marijuana seed-to-sale traceability contract in February 2018 (press release 15 Feb 2018, via Cannabis Business Times). NDAC / programme materials describe a department inventory-tracking system: compassion centers must employ a bar-coding inventory control system to track batch, strain and inventory amounts and host a secure computer interface transferring inventory/transaction information to the department; for dispensaries, amounts sold by registry identification number (secondary Flourish summary of NDAC — Verify NDAC section number for the inventory-tracking mandate). Patient dispensation records are also described (secondary Dutchie guide) as managed in the state's Prescription Management Program (PMP) — Verify against HHS/Board of Pharmacy PMP documentation; do not state PMP integration as statute text. Hemp tracking: No seed-to-sale system for hemp analogous to BioTrack was located; hemp compliance is via licensure, lot/field legal descriptions, and pre-harvest sampling (Part 7/11, Run 2). Do not apply BioTrack to hemp.

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections for patients — there are effectively none, and the statute expressly preserves employer discipline

Verbatim principle — NDCC §19-24.1-34 (Acts not prohibited — Acts not required), as amended by 2023 SB 2388 (secondary summaries + toolkit text, to be confirmed verbatim against compiled §19-24.1-34): “This chapter does not prohibit an employer from disciplining an employee for possessing or consuming usable marijuana in the workplace, working while under the influence of marijuana, or working with marijuana in the employee's system.” (The third clause — “working with marijuana in the employee's system” — was ADDED by SB 2388, signed 12 Apr 2023, precisely because no test reliably proves present impairment; prior text covered only workplace possession/consumption and working while under the influence.)

What the chapter does NOT require (§19-24.1-34(1), per SB 2344 original): (a) a government medical assistance programme or private insurer to reimburse medical-marijuana costs; (b) a person in lawful possession of property to allow a guest/client/customer/visitor to possess/consume usable marijuana on that property; (c) a landlord to allow production/processing/use on leased property (wording per SB 2344 excerpt — Verify current §19-24.1-34(1) subsections). Workers' compensation: WSI may not pay for medical marijuana (NDCC §§65-05-07 / 65-05-08 / 65-05-39 as cited in secondary employer guide — Verify section numbers); wage-loss benefits related to medical-marijuana use/presence are excluded per that guide. Drug testing generally: ND has no comprehensive private-employer drug-testing statute (secondary employer guides agree). Employers may test (pre-employment, random, reasonable suspicion, post-accident) under their own written policies, subject to federal law (DOT, ADA) and the rule that an employer who requires a test to obtain/retain employment must pay for it (NDCC §34-01-15 / related — Verify section). A positive test, even for a registered patient, may lawfully lead to discipline/termination under §19-24.1-34 as above. There is NO ND anti-discrimination protection for patient status or for a positive marijuana test alone (contrast states with such protections — ND is expressly in the “no protection” group in secondary toolkits). Bottom line for Part 1: Do not describe ND patients as having employment or drug-testing protections. The protection that DOES exist is narrow: lawful medical use is not itself a ch. 19-03.1 offence when within ch. 19-24.1 limits — that is a criminal-law protection, not an employment protection.

Session structure: ND Legislative Assembly, 69th Assembly, regular session 7 Jan – May 2025. No regular session in 2026. Next regular session: 70th Assembly, Jan 2027. Interim committees in 2026 may study issues but cannot enact regular-session bills. No special session on cannabis was located in sources read (Verify ndlegis.gov session calendar before Run 2 if a special session is called).

Part 2 — Taxes

Sourcing note for Part 2: official sources were searched first (ndlegis.gov, tax.nd.gov, hhs.nd.gov, attorneygeneral.nd.gov, official city/county sites). Where only a search-result extract of an official document was available, or a secondary source summarised an official document, that is labelled as such. One official-page read (HHS FY2024 Annual Report, second half) hit a browser rate limit on 30 October 2026 and was NOT retried; figures from that half are therefore cited via the search extract of the same official PDF and labelled accordingly. No fact below is presented as verified from a page that was not in fact read.

2.1 Headline answer

  • North Dakota imposes NO cannabis-specific excise tax on medical marijuana. There is no state or local marijuana excise, privilege or gross-receipts tax in current law.
  • Medical marijuana IS subject to the ordinary state sales tax (5%) plus any applicable local (city/county) sales, use and gross-receipts tax at the place of delivery/sale. It does not qualify for the prescription-drug sales-tax exemption, because a North Dakota patient is certified, not prescribed marijuana.
  • Hemp and hemp-derived products that are lawful tangible personal property are taxed the same way — ordinary 5% state sales/use tax plus local tax — with no hemp-specific excise tax found in current law. See §2.5.
  • Licence/certification fees function as the programme's main revenue device: statutory maxima of $5,000 (application), $90,000 (dispensary certification, 2 years) and $110,000 (manufacturing-facility certification, 2 years), plus patient/agent card fees. The programme has been funded from special funds (fees) since the 2019–2021 biennium. See §2.4 and Part 4, §4.6 for the three fee layers (statute ceilings vs. NDAC actuals vs. the reported 1 Nov 2026 step-up).
  • Income tax: North Dakota starts from federal taxable income (NDCC §57-38-01(13)), so federal §280E, while it applied, flowed into the state computation; no North Dakota decoupling statute for cannabis was found. Post–28 April 2026 state flow-through of Schedule III relief is VERIFY (no Tax Commissioner guidance found). See §2.7.
  • Proposed adult-use taxes are all PROPOSED / FAILED — never law: 2018 Measure 3 (no new tax; 5% sales tax would have applied), 2022 Measure 2 (5% sales tax, no additional marijuana tax), 2024 Measure 5 (no tax in the measure text; fiscal note assumed 5% sales tax), 2021 HB 1420 / HB 1501 (15% gross-receipts + 10% wholesale excise proposed, both failed in the Senate). See §2.8. None of these may be quoted as a current rate.

2.2 State sales tax — the 5% rate, what it applies to, and why medical marijuana is not exempt

Statute — rate. NDCC §57-39.2-02.1 imposes “a tax of five percent upon the gross receipts of retailers from all sales at retail” of tangible personal property (and the other listed items), except as otherwise expressly provided in Chapter 57-39.2. The Legislative Council's own memorandum on the 2018 ballot measure restates this as: “Pursuant to NDCC Section 57-39.2-02.1, sales tax at a rate of 5 percent is applied to the gross receipts of retailers for all retail sales of tangible personal property, unless specifically exempted.” Source (official, Legislative Council memorandum, opened via search extract): https://ndlegis.gov/sites/default/files/resource/research-document/19.9352.01000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

Statute — prescription-drug exemption, and why it does not cover medical marijuana. NDCC §57-39.2-04(7) exempts: “Gross receipts from the sale of drugs sold under a doctor's prescription.” Sources (statute text via LawServer/FindLaw mirrors of NDCC §57-39.2-04, search extracts; official text is on ndlegis.gov): https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/north-dakota/nd-code/north_dakota_code_57_39-2_04 and https://codes.findlaw.com/nd/title-57-taxation/nd-cent-code-sect-57-39-2-04/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. The Tax Commissioner's position, as recorded in the official 2016 fiscal note for Measure 5 and repeated in the 2018 Legislative Council memorandum, is:

“The initiated measure does not impose any state taxes. However, under existing law, the state sales tax of 5% would apply to the retail purchase price of all medical marijuana products sold in the state, including seeds and plants for 'grow your own' usage, as well as products sold in final form.” — Office of the State Tax Commissioner, quoted in the Department of Health fiscal note for Measure 5, 22 September 2016.
“A sales tax exemption is not provided for the sale of medical marijuana, because the drug is not sold under a doctor's prescription, or the sale of recreational marijuana.” — Legislative Council memorandum 19.9352.01000 (August 2018).

Sources: https://ndlegis.gov/files/committees/64-2014%20appendices/17_5151_03000appendixg.pdf and https://ndlegis.gov/sites/default/files/resource/research-document/19.9352.01000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

Reasoning in plain English: A North Dakota health-care provider executes a written certification that the patient has a debilitating medical condition; the provider does not write a prescription for marijuana, and a dispensary is not a pharmacy dispensing a prescribed drug. The §57-39.2-04(7) exemption therefore does not attach. Every retail sale by a registered dispensary to a registered qualifying patient or designated caregiver is a taxable retail sale of tangible personal property at 5% state tax, before local tax. This is the single most important tax fact in Part 2, and it is the reason secondary summaries that say “medical marijuana is exempt” or “patients pay no tax” are wrong for North Dakota. No other sales-tax exemption in Chapter 57-39.2 was found that covers medical marijuana. In particular: no exemption for sales to registered patients, no exemption for sales by compassion centres, and no exemption for medical marijuana as a “drug” generally — the exemption is expressly limited to drugs “sold under a doctor's prescription.” VERIFY (narrow point): Whether any individual dispensary sale could fall under a different exemption (e.g. a sale to a governmental unit under §57-39.2-04(6), or a sale for resale) is transaction-specific; no such general exemption for patient sales was found, and none should be assumed.

What is taxed: The retail purchase price of all medical marijuana products sold in final form. The 2016 Tax Commissioner statement expressly included “seeds and plants for 'grow your own' usage” — that limb is now historical only, because home cultivation was removed by the 2017 rewrite (SB 2344); it is retained here only to show the Commissioner's original reasoning that form does not matter — flower, concentrate, edible/lozenge, tincture, topical and any other usable-marijuana product sold at retail is taxed the same way. No reduced rate, no zero rate, and no product-type carve-out was found in current law. Who collects and remits: The dispensary as retailer collects the tax from the patient/caregiver at the point of sale and remits it to the Office of the State Tax Commissioner, through the ND Taxpayer Access Point (ND TAP) sales-and-use-tax return, on the filing frequency assigned by the Commissioner (monthly for most dispensaries, per secondary tax-practice summaries — VERIFY against the Tax Commissioner's current Sales and Use Tax Requirements guideline, which was not directly opened in this run). The tax is added to the purchase price and is a debt of the purchaser to the retailer under the general sales-tax scheme (NDCC §57-39.2-08.2, as referenced in older bill text — current text VERIFY).

2.3 Use tax — the mirror of sales tax

North Dakota use tax (NDCC Chapter 57-40.2) applies at the same combined rate as sales tax when taxable tangible personal property is used, stored or consumed in North Dakota and North Dakota sales tax was not paid — classically, an out-of-state purchase brought into the state. For medical marijuana this has almost no lawful application, because a patient may lawfully obtain usable marijuana only from a North Dakota registered dispensary (see Part 1 / Part 10 — out-of-state purchases may not lawfully be brought in). It is included here only to distinguish it from sales tax: use tax is not a second tax on dispensary sales; it is the backstop for untaxed out-of-state purchases, at the same 5% state rate plus applicable local tax. The use-tax rate provision most often cited is NDCC §57-40.2-02.1 (3% for certain manufactured/mobile-home transactions is a special case and does NOT apply to marijuana; the general use-tax rate tracks the sales-tax rate). VERIFY: The current text of §57-40.2-02.1 was not directly opened in this run; do not quote a use-tax subsection number for marijuana without re-checking the current ndlegis.gov text. Source for the general sales/use-tax framework (Tax Commissioner, via secondary summary checked against statute): https://www.tax.nd.gov/local-tax-refund-local-maximum-tax-amounts — Accessed 2026-10-30 (for local-tax mechanics; see §2.6). The Tax Commissioner's sales-and-use-tax pages are at tax.nd.gov; the specific “Sales and Use Tax” guideline URL was not returned verbatim in this run and is therefore not asserted here — follow-up: open tax.nd.gov sales-and-use-tax guidance directly and record the exact guideline URL.

2.4 Licence, certification and registration fees that function like a tax

These are not called taxes in the statute, but they are compulsory, state-collected, biennial, and — since 2019–2021 — the sole funding source for the medical marijuana programme. For revenue purposes they must be counted alongside sales tax.

FeeStatutory maximumPeriodSource

Dispensary certification feeNot to exceed $90,000Per 2-year registration certificateNDCC §19-24.1-14: “in an amount not to exceed ninety thousand dollars for a dispensary and one hundred ten thousand dollars for a manufacturing facility”
Manufacturing-facility certification feeNot to exceed $110,000Per 2-year registration certificateSame provision

Note on source quality: Justia is a mirror, not ndlegis.gov. The figures are corroborated by the HHS programme's own presentations and by every secondary source checked, but the Run 1 Part 1 researcher should re-verify the subsection numbers against the current ndlegis.gov Chapter 19-24.1 PDF before final publication, and record the ndlegis.gov URL there. Corroboration (HHS presentation to the ND League of Cities, 7 March 2018, via SlideToDoc extract): dispensaries “$90,000/2 years for registration”, manufacturing facilities “$110,000/2 years for registration” — https://slidetodoc.com/north-dakota-department-of-health-division-of-medical/ — Accessed 2026-10-30.

Administrative (NDAC) fee schedule vs. statutory caps — a live change to flag, not to blur: Secondary reporting in September 2026 (Marijuana Herald, 17 September 2026; Cannabis Business Advisors licensing update, week of 21 September 2026) states that HHS adopted updated NDAC Article 33-44-01 certification fees effective 1 November 2026 (i.e. after this report's 30 October 2026 access date), splitting the old two categories into four:

NDAC category (from 1 Nov 2026, per secondary reporting — VERIFY against NDAC)Old NDAC feeNew NDAC fee
Full manufacturing facility$75,000$110,000
Dispensary$60,000$90,000
Production-only manufacturing facility$40,000$70,000
Processor-only facility$20,000$50,000

Sources (secondary, dated, for a prospective administrative change — NOT current law on 30 Oct 2026): https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/north-dakota-to-sharply-increase-medical-marijuana-business-fees-with-some-rising-150/ (17 Sept 2026) and https://thecannabisbusinessadvisors.com/cannabis-licensing-update-week-of-september-21-2026/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. How to read this correctly: The statutory caps ($90,000 dispensary / $110,000 manufacturing) did not change; what changed (if the reporting is accurate) is the administrative fee actually charged within those caps, and the creation of two sub-categories (production-only, processor-only) whose fees sit below the full-manufacturing cap. The pre-November NDAC fees ($60,000 / $75,000 / $40,000 / $20,000) were below the statutory caps — which is why the programme's fee revenue in any given year cannot be derived simply by multiplying $90,000 × 8 + $110,000 × 2. VERIFY before Run 2 / before publication: open the current NDAC 33-44-01-07.3 (or successor) text on ndlegis.gov / the ND Administrative Code site and confirm (a) whether the 1 November 2026 schedule took effect, and (b) the exact fee for each of the four categories. Do not present the November schedule as the 30 October 2026 position. Also: The 2023 reduction (manufacturing $110,000 → $75,000, dispensary $90,000 → $60,000) reported in the same September 2026 article explains why older sources quote $90,000/$110,000 as actual fees and newer pre-November sources quote $60,000/$75,000 — both are “correct” for their dates; the statute always stated the maxima. Full NDAC detail is in Part 4, §4.6.

Other fee-like charges: Per-plant surcharge (historical/secondary): A secondary county summary states a manufacturing facility “must pay $5,000 payable (ceiling $7,500) more in the certification fee for every 500 cannabis plants after the first 1,000 plants.” — https://northdakotacannabis.org/cass — Accessed 2026-10-30. VERIFY: This surcharge was not confirmed in the statute extracts opened in this run; it may reflect an older NDAC provision or a misreading. Do not include it in any revenue calculation without NDAC verification. (Part 4, §4.6 confirms the NDAC rule amount is $5,000 per 500 plants over 1,000 — see the conflict note in the Gaps Table.) Patient/caregiver card fees are capped in statute (see table) and are programme revenue, not a tax on marijuana itself, but they are a cost of lawful access and are therefore noted here. The hospice exception (no application fee for a hospice-admitted qualifying-patient applicant submitting medical records in lieu of a written certification, effective 1 August 2023) is recorded in the HHS FY2024 Annual Report — see §2.9. Failed fee-reduction proposal (PROPOSED, FAILED): HB 1321 (2025) would have reduced renewal fees from $90,000 to $81,000 (dispensary) and $110,000 to $100,000 (manufacturing). It failed in the House on 10 February 2025 (yeas 37, nays 53). It is NOT law. — Source (secondary bill tracker, for status only): https://www.billtrack50.com/billdetail/1779471 — Accessed 2026-10-30. Follow-up: confirm the vote on ndlegis.gov before final publication. No local compassion-centre licence fee was found in current law. Cities may charge ordinary business-licence / special-use-permit application fees under their general powers, but no cannabis-specific local fee schedule was located for any jurisdiction checked in Part 3 (see §3.4). Any such fee, if it exists for Dickinson's annual special-use-permit review or Williston's special-use permit, is VERIFY / Unavailable on the sources opened. Hemp licence fees (tax-like, for completeness — full detail is Part 7): Hemp grower/processor fees are set by the Department of Agriculture under NDCC Chapter 4.1-18.1 and are programme fees, not a product tax. No hemp fee figure is asserted in Part 2, because the task assigns hemp licence types to Part 7 and no NDDA fee schedule was opened in this run. Do not import a hemp fee from a secondary source into Part 2 without an NDDA source. (Part 5, §5.4, from the NDDA page opened, gives grower $100/$200/$300/$350 by lot count, processor $200, plus $40 background-check payment.)

2.5 Hemp product tax

  • No hemp-specific excise, privilege or product tax was found in North Dakota law. Hemp, hemp grain/fibre, CBD and other lawful hemp products are tangible personal property (or, for unprocessed agricultural commodities, subject to the ordinary agricultural-sale rules) and are taxed, when sold at retail, at the ordinary 5% state sales tax plus applicable local tax — the same treatment secondary tax guides describe (“Hemp-derived products (CBD, Δ8, etc.) that meet the 2018 Farm Bill's ≤0.3% Δ9-THC limit are taxed at the normal sales-and-use rate (5% plus local)”). Source (secondary tax guide, 1 July 2025, for the absence of a special hemp excise — the absence claim itself is necessarily a negative search result, so it is flagged as such): https://cannabiscpa.tax/north-dakota-cannabis-hemp-tax-guide-2025-edition/ — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Delta-8 and chemically derived cannabinoids: North Dakota prohibits sales of hemp products containing delta-8 THC or chemically derived cannabinoids under NDCC Chapter 4.1-18.1 (full detail is Part 1). A prohibited product has no lawful retail tax treatment; its sale is not “taxed at a hemp rate” — it is unlawful. Do not assign a tax rate to a prohibited product.
  • Tobacco-tax analogy — do NOT apply it to hemp/CBD: Tobacco products are taxed under NDCC Chapter 57-36 (28% of wholesale purchase price for tobacco products other than cigarettes, per secondary summaries; cigarette rates are separate). Hemp/CBD products are NOT tobacco products under Chapter 57-36 and are NOT subject to that tax merely because they are sold in a smoke/vape shop. A shop that sells both tobacco and CBD pays tobacco tax on the tobacco and sales tax on the CBD; the two regimes do not merge. VERIFY: The 28% figure was not confirmed against the current §57-36 text in this run; it is noted only to prevent the analogy error, not as a verified rate.
  • Agricultural exemptions — VERIFY, do not assume: North Dakota sales tax has agricultural exemptions (e.g. for certain farm machinery, seed, feed, fertiliser — see NDCC §57-39.2-04). Whether a particular hemp transaction (e.g. sale of hemp seed for planting, sale of hemp grain to a processor for resale/manufacturing) falls under an agricultural or resale exemption is transaction-specific and was NOT determined in this run. No blanket “hemp is exempt as agriculture” claim is made.
  • Local tax on hemp/CBD retail: The same local-tax rules as for medical marijuana apply (see §2.6) — a CBD/smoke-shop sale in Fargo is subject to Fargo + Cass County local tax on top of the 5% state tax, because local taxes “must conform in all respects with regard to the taxable or exempt status of items under chapters 57-39.2, 57-39.5, 57-39.6, and 57-40.2” (home-rule limitation, see §2.6). A city may not create a separate local hemp tax rate; it may only apply its general local sales-tax rate to a taxable sale.

2.6 Local sales, use and gross-receipts taxes — how they work, and the rates at dispensary locations

Legal basis — home rule, not a cannabis power. Cities and counties do not levy a “cannabis local tax.” They levy a general local sales, use and gross-receipts tax under home-rule authority, and that general tax automatically applies to a taxable medical-marijuana or hemp sale in the same way it applies to any other taxable retail sale.

Home-rule cities: NDCC §40-05.1-06(16) (and the parallel county provision, NDCC §11-09.1-05) authorises a home-rule city/county to levy sales, use, farm-machinery gross-receipts and alcoholic-beverage gross-receipts taxes, subject to the limitations that the taxes: (a) “Must conform in all respects with regard to the taxable or exempt status of items under chapters 57-39.2, 57-39.5, 57-39.6, and 57-40.2 and may not be imposed at multiple rates” (with stated exceptions for e.g. motor vehicles, manufactured homes, fuel, electricity/gas); (b) “May not be newly imposed or changed except to be effective on the first day of a calendar quarterly period after a minimum of ninety days' notice to the tax commissioner”; (c) “May not be limited to apply to less than the full value of the transaction or item as determined for state sales and use tax purposes” (except farm machinery gross-receipts tax); (d) Must be subject to collection by the Tax Commissioner under an agreement under NDCC §57-01-02.1 (except city lodging/restaurant tax) and administered by the Commissioner under Chapter 57-39.2, including reporting, refunds, penalty and interest. Source (bill text amending §§11-09.1-05 and 40-05.1-06, ndlegis.gov, search extract): https://ndlegis.gov/api/assembly/65-2017/regular/documents/17-1005-02000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Follow-up: re-verify the current subsection numbering on ndlegis.gov before final publication; the 2017 engrossment is the source of the quoted limitations, and later amendments may have renumbered them.

Consequence for cannabis: Because medical marijuana is taxable at state level, it is automatically taxable at local level wherever a general local tax is in force, at that jurisdiction's general rate. Because it is not exempt at state level, a city or county may not exempt it locally (the conformity rule forbids a local exemption for a state-taxable item, and forbids multiple rates). Conversely, a city with no local tax adds nothing. There is no “local cannabis tax” to opt into or out of — the only local question is whether the general local tax exists and at what rate and cap. The 2018 Legislative Council memorandum states the same point for recreational marijuana: “Sales tax on recreational marijuana sales also would apply at the local level if a local option tax is imposed by a city or county under home rule authority.” — https://ndlegis.gov/sites/default/files/resource/research-document/19.9352.01000.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. The same logic applies to medical sales. Collection: Local taxes are collected by the Tax Commissioner under §57-01-02.1 agreements, on the same return as state tax. The retailer does not file a separate city return. Local rates change only quarterly, on 90 days' notice — so rates are stable within a quarter but must be re-checked quarterly. The authoritative lookup is the Tax Commissioner's Local Taxes / Sales Tax Rate Lookup tool (tax.nd.gov); secondary rate tables go stale quickly (see the stale 6%/7%/7.25% figures in older secondary guides, now superseded by the April 2025 changes below). Local maximum tax (“cap”): Many city/county ordinances impose a maximum local tax per single sale/invoice, above which the purchaser may claim a refund from the Tax Commissioner. This matters for larger dispensary purchases (though the 30-day purchase limits keep most patient transactions well below the cap thresholds — see Part 1 for limits). The Tax Commissioner's official maximum-tax table (opened 30 October 2026) is the source for the city/county rates and caps in the table below.

Official local-tax table — city rates and caps (Tax Commissioner, opened 30 October 2026). The table gives the city rate/cap from the official maximum-tax page. County tax is additional where the county levies one. Combined rates in the right-hand column are derived by adding state 5% + city rate + county rate, and are labelled as derived — the Tax Commissioner's address-level lookup, not this table, is authoritative for any specific delivery address, because a city rate, a county rate and any special-district rate can combine differently by address, and because rates change quarterly.

Source for the April 2025 Bismarck/Fargo changes (official Tax Commissioner notice): https://www.tax.nd.gov/sites/www/files/documents/forms/business/sales-use/local-jurisdiction-rates/2025-jurisdiction/local-tax-changes-4-1-2025-revised.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30 (Bismarck 1.5% → 2%, max $37.50; Fargo 2% → 2.25%, max $56.25, both effective 1 April 2025). Worked example (illustrative, not a price claim): A $100 retail dispensary sale in Fargo on 30 October 2026 bears $5.00 state tax + $2.25 Fargo tax + $0.50 Cass County tax = $7.75 total tax, for a $107.75 total, before any cap (the cap is not reached at $100). The same $100 sale in Bismarck bears $5.00 + $2.00 + $1.00 = $8.00. A $3,000 single sale in Fargo would hit the Fargo cap ($56.25 = 2.25% × $2,500) and the Cass cap ($12.50 = 0.50% × $2,500), so local tax collected above those caps is refundable to the purchaser on application to the Tax Commissioner — but patient 30-day purchase limits (see Part 1) make a $3,000 single dispensary transaction unlikely in practice; the cap is noted for completeness, not as a typical patient outcome. Stale secondary figures to disregard: Older secondary guides quoting Fargo 7.5%, Bismarck 7% or 6%, Grand Forks 7.25% as a state+local total without the county add-on, or a statewide “average ~7%”, pre-date the April 2025 city increases and/or omit the county layer. They are superseded by the official table above for the cities listed. The often-quoted statewide ceiling of 8.75% (Grafton, Walsh County: 5% + 2.75% city + 1% Walsh County from 1 January 2026) is a secondary-summary figure (Walsh County 1% from 1 Jan 2026 IS on the official max-tax page, max $50/sale) — it is not a dispensary-city rate and is noted only to show the range.

Delivery / destination sourcing: North Dakota is a destination-based sales-tax state for most retail sales — the rate follows the delivery address, not the dispensary's address. For in-store dispensary pick-up the two coincide. For any lawful delivery (delivery rules are Part 1/Part 11 — note that HHS materials describe dispensary delivery to cardholders, while some secondary sources say delivery is not legal; that conflict is for Part 1 to resolve and is NOT resolved here), the delivery-address rate would apply. VERIFY the delivery-sourcing rule for dispensary deliveries against the Tax Commissioner's guideline before advising on a delivery transaction. No local tax on the licence fees themselves: Certification/application/card fees are paid to HHS, not to a retailer, and are not retail sales of tangible personal property; no state or local sales tax applies to the fee itself. (This is a straightforward application of §57-39.2-02.1 — the fee is not a “sale at retail” — and is stated as reasoning, not as a quoted Tax Commissioner ruling. VERIFY if a fee is ever bundled with a product sale.)

2.7 Income tax, §280E, and North Dakota conformity / decoupling

The North Dakota starting point. NDCC §57-38-01(13) defines “taxable income” for individuals, estates, trusts and corporations as “the taxable income as computed for an individual, estate, trust, or corporation for federal income tax purposes under the United States Internal Revenue Code of 1954, as amended, plus or minus the adjustments as may be provided by this chapter or other provisions of law.” NDCC §57-38-01(5) defines that Code as the Internal Revenue Code of 1986, as amended. NDCC §57-38-01.1 states the legislative intent: “adopting the federal definition of taxable income as the starting point for the computation of state income tax by all taxpayers and providing the necessary adjustments thereto.” Source (official ndlegis.gov Chapter 57-38 PDF, opened): https://ndlegis.gov/cencode/t57c38.pdf?20150904142200 — Accessed 2026-10-30. Caveat: The PDF served is an older compilation (2015 stamp in the URL). The definitions quoted were confirmed in the opened text, but the Run 1 Part 1 / tax researcher should re-confirm the current subsection numbering (notably whether §57-38-01(13) remains the “taxable income” definition after later amendments, including the 2023–2025 individual-rate reforms) on the current ndlegis.gov Code before final publication. The principle — federal starting point plus North Dakota adjustments — is stable and is also stated in §57-38-01.1.

What that meant while marijuana was Schedule I (before 28 April 2026). IRC §280E disallows deductions for ordinary and necessary business expenses (rent, payroll, marketing, etc. — cost of goods sold excepted) for a trade or business trafficking in a Schedule I or II controlled substance. Because North Dakota started from federal taxable income — i.e. income already computed after the §280E disallowance — the disallowance flowed into the North Dakota computation automatically, unless North Dakota provided an adjustment. Secondary tax analyses agree:

“Does Section 280E Apply to state individual income tax? Yes. North Dakota conforms to the Internal Revenue Code. The starting point is Federal Adjusted Gross Income. There is no provision that states Section 280E does not apply. Does Section 280E apply to state corporate income tax? Yes. North Dakota conforms to the Internal Revenue Code. The starting point is Federal taxable income. There is no provision that states Section 280E does not apply.” — AndreTaxCo, North Dakota cannabis §280E analysis (secondary, undated, accessed 2026-10-30): https://www.andretaxco.com/north-dakota-cannabis-280e — Accessed 2026-10-30.
“North Dakota starts both individual and corporate returns with federal taxable income. Under §280E, businesses dealing in cannabis may only deduct the cost of goods sold; all other business expenses are disallowed.” — Cannabis CPA Tax, North Dakota Cannabis & Hemp Tax Guide, updated 1 July 2025 (secondary): https://cannabiscpa.tax/north-dakota-cannabis-hemp-tax-guide-2025-edition/ — Accessed 2026-10-30.

No decoupling statute found. A search of Chapter 57-38 adjustments (§57-38-01.3 for corporations, and the individual-adjustment provisions) found no cannabis-specific addition or subtraction — no provision that adds back a §280E deduction, and no provision that allows a deduction disallowed federally. The only cannabis-related decoupling proposal located is historical and failed: HB 1430 (2015), §19-24-19 (PROPOSED, FAILED): The 2015 medical-marijuana bill (HB 1430, 64th Legislative Assembly) contained a provision that would have rendered §280E inapplicable for state purposes. It failed in the House on 18 February 2015 (yeas 26, nays 67). It is NOT law, and it pre-dates the enacted 2016/2017 programme entirely. The AndreTaxCo analysis cites it as: “Lawmakers have considered a provision that would render Section 280E inapplicable. See House Bill No. 1430, Section 19-24-19. However, current law suggests that Section 280E is applicable.” — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/64-2015/regular/bill-overview/bo1430.html (official bill overview: “Failed in House”) — Accessed 2026-10-30. No later decoupling bill was found in the 2023, 2025 or 2026 sessions checked via search. VERIFY: A full ndlegis.gov bill search for “280E” across all sessions should be run by the Run 2 researcher; the negative claim here rests on the searches performed on 30 October 2026, not on an exhaustive legislative-history review.

What the 28 April 2026 Schedule III change means for North Dakota — analysis, flagged VERIFY: 1. Federal level (given baseline): For a state-licensed medical operator, marijuana is no longer a Schedule I/II substance for §280E purposes, so the federal §280E disallowance is generally lifted for that operator's medical activity. For any non-medical (adult-use / unlicensed) activity, marijuana remains Schedule I and §280E remains fully applicable. 2. North Dakota level (reasoning, not a Tax Commissioner ruling): Because North Dakota's starting point is federal taxable income as computed, if the federal computation for a North Dakota medical licensee no longer includes a §280E disallowance, the North Dakota starting point for that licensee likewise no longer includes it — unless North Dakota enacts an adjustment to re-impose it. No such re-imposing adjustment was found. That is the straightforward conformity reading. 3. Why it is still VERIFY: (a) No Tax Commissioner guidance, ruling or FAQ on Schedule III / §280E flow-through for ND medical licensees was located on 30 October 2026; (b) the federal rule's exact scope (which entities/products count as “state-licensed medical” — e.g. a manufacturer selling only to dispensaries vs. a dispensary selling only to cardholders — and how mixed medical/non-medical activity is apportioned) is a federal question that North Dakota has not, on the sources found, addressed; (c) the tribal question in the federal baseline (“if any tribe operates a medical programme, mark as Verify whether it counts as 'state-licensed'”) applies with equal force here — a tribal programme, if any exists, cannot be assumed to flow through in the same way (see Part 10); (d) entity type matters — a pass-through entity's owners compute North Dakota tax on their individual returns from federal adjusted gross income, while a corporation computes from federal taxable income, and the adjustment schedules differ. No licensee should file on the basis of this paragraph alone; a North Dakota tax adviser and, ideally, a Tax Commissioner ruling should be obtained. The Part 2 conclusion is therefore: North Dakota does NOT decouple from federal §280E by statute; whether Schedule III relief flows through automatically is VERIFY pending Tax Commissioner guidance.

North Dakota income-tax rates (context only — secondary, VERIFY against current §§57-38-30 / 57-38-30.3): Secondary guides (July 2025) give corporate rates of 1.41% on the first $25,000, 3.55% on $25,001–$50,000, and 4.31% above $50,000 (plus a 3.5% water's-edge surtax where elected), and individual rates of 0%, 1.95% and 2.50% by income. These rates are NOT asserted as verified current law in this report, because the 2023–2025 legislature reformed individual rates and the current §§57-38-30 / 57-38-30.3 text was not opened in this run. They are included only so the reader can see the order of magnitude of the state income-tax cost that §280E conformity affects. Follow-up: open current §§57-38-30 and 57-38-30.3 on ndlegis.gov and replace this paragraph with verified brackets. Hemp and §280E: Hemp (as defined federally and in NDCC Chapter 4.1-18.1) is not a controlled substance and was never subject to §280E. A hemp business's ordinary expenses were always deductible federally and therefore always flowed through to North Dakota. The Schedule III change does not affect hemp. A business that handles both hemp and marijuana must keep the activities separate for §280E purposes — mixed-activity apportionment is VERIFY (no North Dakota guidance found).

Other state taxes that touch a cannabis/hemp business (ordinary, not cannabis-specific): Property tax: A dispensary/manufacturing facility's real property is subject to ordinary county property tax; no cannabis-specific property-tax rate or exemption was found. (No rate is quoted — property tax is mill-levy based and parcel-specific.) Employer taxes: Ordinary North Dakota unemployment-insurance and workers'-compensation obligations apply; no cannabis-specific employer tax was found. Motor-fuel, alcohol, tobacco taxes: Only if the business separately engages in a taxed activity (e.g. sells tobacco — see §2.5). No cannabis business pays alcohol tax on a cannabis product. No gross-receipts / privilege tax on compassion centres beyond sales tax and the certification fees in §2.4 was found. If a city attempted to impose a cannabis-specific privilege tax under home rule, the conformity limitation in §40-05.1-06 (taxes must conform to Chapters 57-39.2 etc. and may not be imposed at multiple rates) would be the first legal obstacle — but no such attempt was located, so this is noted as analysis, not as a decided case.

Rule applied throughout: Nothing in this section is current law. Each item gives the measure/bill number, what tax it would have imposed, and its final status, so that a reader cannot mistake a proposal for a rate.

Pattern worth noting: Every North Dakota adult-use proposal that reached voters imposed no cannabis excise beyond the ordinary 5% sales tax (2018, 2022, 2024); the only proposal with a true cannabis excise (HB 1501's 15% + 10%) was a legislative bill that died in the Senate. North Dakota voters have therefore never been asked to approve a cannabis excise tax, and the legislature has never enacted one. That is why the “no excise tax” headline in §2.1 is not an oversight — it is the consistent legislative and initiative record.

2.9 Revenue figures — what official data actually exists

A. Dispensary sales (the base on which sales tax is collected). The HHS Medical Marijuana Program Annual Report, Fiscal Year 2024 (data as of 30 June 2024) reports total dispensary sales. The secondary summary of that official report gives: FY2024 total dispensary sales: $22.4 million, up 3.8% on FY2023 (FY2023: $21.606 million, per the same secondary summary's FY2023 figure; FY2022: ~$20 million; 2020: $6.4 million — all as summarised from HHS annual reports). Source (secondary summary of the official FY2024 report, for the sales figure): https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/nd_cannmir.html — Accessed 2026-10-30; corroborated in narrative form by https://northdakotamonitor.com/2025/07/21/north-dakota-medical-marijuana-program-adding-edibles-making-eligibility-changes/ (“totaling $22.4 million last year”, 21 July 2025) — Accessed 2026-10-30. Source (official report itself, opened for pp. 1–8 in this run): https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Annual-Report-FY-2024-archive.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Gap: The report's “Total Dispensary Sales” table (p. 8) and the full “Expenses and Revenues” revenue lines were in the second half of the PDF, which could not be re-opened after a browser rate limit (see Method note). The $22.4m figure is therefore cited via the secondary summary of the same report, not as a directly extracted table cell, and the Run 1 Part 6 researcher should extract the p. 8 table directly and replace this citation. (Part 6, from the official report, gives the full FY2020–FY2024 series: $6,361,000 / $15,336,000 / $19,970,000 / $21,606,000 / $22,424,000 — reconciled figures used throughout this file.) Implied sales-tax yield (derived, NOT an official revenue figure — do not quote as Tax Commissioner data): At a blended combined rate of roughly 7–8% (state + local, varying by dispensary city — see §2.6), $22.4m of retail sales implies roughly $1.6–$1.8m of total sales/use tax collected on medical marijuana in FY2024, of which 5/7.5ths to 5/8ths (roughly $1.12m at the 5% state rate alone) would be state tax. This derivation is arithmetic only. The Tax Commissioner does not publish a marijuana-specific sales-tax collection total that was located in this run, and HHS reports dispensary sales, not tax collected. No official “medical marijuana tax revenue” total is asserted. Any figure presented as “North Dakota collected $X in marijuana taxes in FY2024” without a Tax Commissioner source should be treated as unverified.

No HHS or Tax Commissioner publication of hemp-product tax collections was located (hemp sales are not separately coded in sales-tax returns). Local tax is collected centrally and remitted to the city/county in aggregate; a dispensary's contribution is not separately reported. Part 3 — Local Rules

3.1 Who does what: state, county, city, township — and what each level CANNOT do

The state sets the programme; local government sets the place. That sentence summarises the whole of Part 3, and every detail below is an elaboration of it.

State (HHS Division of Medical Marijuana, under NDCC Chapter 19-24.1): Decides whether a compassion centre exists at all: registers no more than two manufacturing facilities and no more than eight dispensaries, unless HHS determines additional entities are necessary to increase patient access (NDCC §19-24.1-12, as summarised in the HHS FY2024 Annual Report). Decides who is registered, on merit criteria including suitability of the proposed location. Imposes the only statewide siting rule: a compassion centre may not be located within 1,000 feet of the property line of a pre-existing public or private school, and the applicant must prove local-zoning compliance (see §3.2). Does NOT zone land, issue building permits, or grant local business licences. A state registration certificate is not a zoning approval, and HHS will not register an applicant that has not first obtained local zoning approval.

Counties (board of county commissioners, NDCC Chapter 11-33 — County Zoning): NDCC §11-33-01: “For the purpose of promoting health, safety, morals, public convenience, general prosperity, and public welfare, the board of county commissioners of any county may regulate and restrict within the county, subject to section 11-33-20 and chapter 54-21.3, the location and the use of buildings and structures and the use, condition of use, or occupancy of lands for residence, recreation, and other purposes.” Source (2012 Code text via Justia, for the section wording; current text to be re-verified on ndlegis.gov): https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/2012/title-11/chapter-11-33/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. NDCC §11-33-20 is the key limit: “This chapter does not prevent townships from making regulations as provided in sections 58-03-11 through 58-03-15, but such townships may relinquish their powers, or any portion thereof, to enact zoning regulations to the county by resolution of the board of township supervisors. This chapter may not be construed to affect any property, real or personal, located within the zoning or subdivision authority of any city of this state, except that any city by resolution of its governing body may relinquish to the county its authority, or any portion thereof, to enact zoning regulations under chapter 40-47 or subdivision regulations under chapter 40-48, in which case the property is subject to this chapter.” In plain English: county zoning does not apply inside a city that exercises its own zoning, and does not override an organised township that exercises its own zoning, unless that city/township has relinquished its power to the county. County zoning is therefore primarily the zoning for unincorporated county land outside city zoning authority (including extraterritorial areas not covered by a city — see below) and outside organised-township zoning. A home-rule county (NDCC Chapter 11-09.1, §11-09.1-05(7)) may, if its charter so provides and it is implemented by ordinance, “provide for zoning, planning and subdivision of public or private property within the county limits but outside the zoning authority of any city or organized township” — i.e. even a home-rule county cannot zone over a city or an organised township. (Source: Attorney General letter to Dotzenrod, 6 April 1992, summarising §§11-09.1-05(7), 11-33, 40-47, 58-03 — labelled as an AG opinion, not law — https://attorneygeneral.nd.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/040692-Dotzenrod_0.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.) Practical consequence for cannabis/hemp: A manufacturing facility or hemp operation on unincorporated county land needs county zoning approval (typically a special/conditional-use permit in an agricultural or industrial district — see Ward County in §3.3). A dispensary inside a city needs city approval, not county approval, even though the county may also be the taxing jurisdiction. A facility in an organised township that zones needs township approval (and, if the township has relinquished zoning to the county, county approval instead).

Cities (governing body / planning commission, NDCC Chapter 40-47 — City Zoning; NDCC §40-05-02(13); home-rule cities NDCC Chapter 40-05.1, §40-05.1-06(11)): A city's zoning authority derives from NDCC §40-05-02(13) and Chapter 40-47 (AG Dotzenrod letter, above — opinion, not law). Cities zone within their corporate limits, and — critically for fringe sites — may extend zoning extraterritorially under NDCC §40-47-01.1: to unincorporated territory within ½ mile (cities under 5,000 population), 1 mile, 2 miles, or 4 miles depending on population tier (the 1997 amendment text gives: under 5,000 → 1 mile [as amended]; the tiered distances in the current statute must be re-verified — the 1978 case below confirms the 2-mile rule for cities of 25,000+). The leading case is Apple Creek Township v. City of Bismarck, 271 N.W.2d 583 (N.D. 1978), holding that Bismarck could exercise exclusive zoning control within 2 miles of its limits even over organised-township territory in that band. — Source: https://law.justia.com/cases/north-dakota/supreme-court/1978/9475-2.html — Accessed 2026-10-30. Practical consequence: A compassion centre or hemp facility just outside Fargo, Bismarck, Grand Forks or Minot may be governed by the city's extraterritorial zoning ordinance, not the county's or the township's, even though it sits outside the city limits. Always check the ETZ map, not just the city-limits map. Cities also exercise the ordinary municipal powers that affect any retail/manufacturing business: building permits, fire/life-safety inspection, business licensing (where the city requires a general business licence), and — for tobacco/vape retailers — local tobacco-retailer licensing/authority (see §3.1, tobacco paragraph below). A city may NOT, however, use zoning to ban a state-registered compassion centre outright in a way that conflicts with Chapter 19-24.1's scheme — no such ban was located, and the 2024 failed Measure 5's provision that it would “supersede local ordinances that otherwise would prohibit” adult-use activity confirms by contrast that, for medical activity, no supersession clause exists and local zoning remains a genuine gate. VERIFY (legal question, not a located case): Whether a city could lawfully zone compassion centres out of every district in the city (a de facto ban) has not been tested in a located North Dakota case in this run; do not assert that it can or cannot. What is certain is that no city checked in §3.3 has done so — every dispensary city has accommodated its dispensary through an existing use group or a special-use permit.

Townships (board of township supervisors, NDCC Chapter 58-03, §§58-03-11 through 58-03-15; organised under Chapter 58-02): An organised township derives zoning authority from NDCC §§58-03-01 and 58-03-11 through 58-03-15 (AG Dotzenrod letter, above — opinion). Township zoning, where exercised, takes precedence over a contrary county regulation (AG opinion: “the regulations or ordinances established by a township zoning commission or a city zoning commission, if they are within the zoning authority of the township or city and are in accordance with the statutes, take precedence over any contrary zoning regulation enacted by a county zoning commission”). Practical consequence: Hemp cultivation/processing on township land, and any compassion-centre site on township land outside city ETZ, must be checked against the township's zoning resolution first. Because townships share names across counties (e.g. there are multiple “Fairview”, “Lincoln” or “Riverside” townships in different counties), a township must always be cited with its county. No township-specific cannabis/hemp zoning resolution was verified in this run — not because townships have none, but because township resolutions are generally not published in a central, searchable code library. Any township claim in a later run must be sourced to that township's own resolution or minutes, with county stated. Unorganised territory (no organised township) falls to county zoning (Chapter 11-33) or city ETZ, as applicable.

What local government does NOT control: Licensing: No county, city or township licenses a compassion centre. Licensing/registration is exclusively HHS's. Local approval is a precondition to state registration, not a licence itself. (Ward County's 2018 minutes put it exactly: “The state dictates who can be issued licenses, but the cities and counties dictate location of facilities.” — https://www.co.ward.nd.us/AgendaCenter/ViewFile/Minutes/493?MOBILE=ON — Accessed 2026-10-30.) Patient/caregiver/product rules: Qualifying conditions, purchase limits, product forms, testing, labelling and advertising are state rules (Chapter 19-24.1 / NDAC 33-44-01). A city may not, by ordinance, change a purchase limit or authorise a product form the state prohibits. Tax rates: As shown in Part 2, a city/county may set its general local sales-tax rate (within home-rule limits), but may not set a cannabis-specific rate, exempt a state-taxable cannabis sale, or impose multiple rates. Hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shops — a different, split regime: There is NO state “CBD licence” and NO state “smoke-shop licence.” A hemp/CBD/vape retailer is, for local purposes, an ordinary retail business, plus: If it sells tobacco, electronic smoking devices or alternative nicotine products, it needs a state tobacco licence under NDCC Chapter 57-36 (Attorney General, Tobacco licensing: dealer/retail application fee $15; distributor/manufacturer/wholesaler $25 + $1,000 surety bond for a new application; licences expire 30 June each year — https://attorneygeneral.nd.gov/licensing-and-gaming/licensing/tobacco/ — Accessed 2026-10-30) and any local tobacco-retailer licence/authority the city requires (see Minot and Devils Lake in §3.3/§3.4). If it sells only CBD/hemp products and no tobacco/nicotine device, no state tobacco licence is required on that account — but it remains subject to general city business licensing, zoning (retail use group), signage and building rules, and to the state prohibition on delta-8/chemically derived cannabinoids (Chapter 4.1-18.1). A city may zone a “smoke/vape shop” as a distinct use (Dickinson does — see §3.3) and may restrict minors' entry to such a shop (Dickinson §22-57(f), $25 fee for a minor cited for entering/remaining — see §3.3), but it may not use a tobacco/vape ordinance to license medical marijuana — that is HHS's exclusive domain, and a dispensary is not a smoke shop. No city was found to require a CBD-specific licence. If a city claims to, that claim is VERIFY against its code — it was not located in this run.

No local opt-out / opt-in vote for medical marijuana: Unlike the 2022 and 2024 adult-use proposals (which would have empowered local jurisdictions to prohibit adult-use businesses — 2022 Measure 2 expressly so), the medical programme has no county/city opt-out referendum. A city or county cannot “vote out” the medical programme; it can only zone the location of a facility within its jurisdiction through generally applicable zoning. The 2024 Measure 5's supersession clause (it would have superseded local ordinances prohibiting adult-use purchase/sale/use/delivery/growing by 21+ adults) is PROPOSED/FAILED and does not affect medical zoning. — Source for 2024 Measure 5 analysis (official, Secretary of State): https://www.sos.nd.gov/sites/www/files/documents/elections/measures/analyses-2024-general-measure5.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

3.2 The statewide siting rules that every local rule sits under

NDCC §19-24.1-14 — compassion-centre application, local approval + school buffer (verbatim substance): For a compassion-centre registration application to be complete and eligible for review, the applicant must submit, among other items: “(d) The physical address of the proposed location of the proposed compassion center and: (1) Evidence of approval from local officials as to the proposed compassion center applicant's compliance with local zoning laws for the physical address to be used by the proposed compassion center; [and] (2) Evidence the physical address … is not located within 1,000 feet of a property line of a pre-existing public or private school” — paraphrased from the statute text; the “1,000 feet” and “evidence of approval from local officials” limbs are verbatim in substance. Source (2025 Code text, Justia mirror — re-verify subsection lettering on ndlegis.gov): https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-19/chapter-19-24-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. Corroboration (HHS presentation to the ND League of Cities, 7 March 2018): “When an applicant applies to become a registered manufacturing facility or a dispensary, the applicant is required to: Provide evidence of approval from local officials as to the applicant's compliance with local zoning laws for the physical address to be used; and Provide evidence the physical address of the proposed location is not located within 1,000 feet of a property line of a pre-existing public or private school.” — https://slidetodoc.com/north-dakota-department-of-health-division-of-medical/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. Corroboration (secondary legal analysis, Ohnstad Law, 12 Jan 2017, updated 30 July 2021, citing §19-24.1-14(1)(d)(1)): https://www.ohnstadlaw.com/blog/2017/01/impact-of-medical-marijuana-on-local-communities/ — Accessed 2026-10-30.

What “school” means — HHS interpretation (not in the statute): Chapter 19-24.1 “does not provide a specific definition of 'school.'” HHS interprets it to include: an entity on the Department of Public Instruction's “Approved Schools By City” or “Approved Schools By District” list; an entity on DPI's “Head Start Sites” list; and a public or private institution of higher education or career/technical education school. (HHS League of Cities presentation, above.) Note: Because the statute does not define “school,” a local ordinance that defines its own buffer (e.g. West Fargo's inclusion of daycare facilities) is adding to, not contradicting, the state rule — the applicant must satisfy both. Measurement — VERIFY: The statute says “within 1,000 feet of a property line of a pre-existing … school.” Whether the 1,000 feet is measured property-line to property-line, building to property-line, or by straight-line vs. pedestrian route is not stated in the extracts opened and is VERIFY against NDAC 33-44-01 and HHS application instructions. Do not assert a measurement method. “Pre-existing” — timing: The school must be pre-existing at the time of application. A school opening after a compassion centre is registered does not retroactively disqualify the site on the sources found — but whether HHS or a local ordinance treats a later school as a non-conforming-use trigger on renewal/relocation is VERIFY. No other statewide buffer: The state imposes NO buffer from churches, parks, residences, daycares, libraries or other dispensaries. Every such buffer in North Dakota is a local addition (West Fargo, Williston — see §3.3). A summary that lists a statewide “1,000 feet from churches” rule for North Dakota is conflating Williston's local rule with state law and is wrong. No statewide cap on the number of facilities per city/county, and no statewide moratorium: The caps are statewide (2 manufacturing / 8 dispensaries) and regional (see §3.5), not per-jurisdiction. No city or county moratorium (temporary ban pending ordinance) on compassion centres was located in this run, other than the general 2017–2018 period in which cities were drafting ordinances before applications opened — drafting is not a moratorium, and no moratorium ordinance text was found. If a moratorium is claimed for any jurisdiction, it is VERIFY against that jurisdiction's minutes/ordinances.

3.3 Official list? No — and jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction findings

Explicit finding required by the task: There is NO official statewide list of jurisdictions with medical-marijuana zoning, moratorium, cap or ban rules. Neither HHS, the ND League of Cities, nor the Tax Commissioner publishes such a list, and no such list was located on ndlegis.gov, hhs.nd.gov or any official county/city site searched on 30 October 2026. The HHS programme publishes the state list of registered compassion centres (see §3.5), not a list of local ordinances. The Western Planner / ND Planning Association roundtable (March 2019) is the closest contemporary compilation — it is a planners' discussion, not an official list, and it covers only the cities that chose to participate (Williston, Bismarck, and others summarised below). Roundtable source (secondary, planners' association, 27 Mar 2019): https://www.westernplanner.org/2019articles/2019/3/27/compassion-center-roundtable-city-planners-discuss-zoning-process-for-medical-marijuana-facilities — Accessed 2026-10-30.

What follows is therefore the required checked-jurisdiction table. For each jurisdiction, “Found” means a provision was located in an official or reliably reported source; “Not found” means a search of that jurisdiction's code/zoning/minutes on 30 October 2026 did not locate a cannabis-specific provision — it does NOT prove the jurisdiction has no provision (codes may be unsearchable, hosted on Municode/CivicPlus with poor indexing, or amended after the search). Each “Not found” is therefore a search result, not a legal conclusion, and is flagged accordingly. Dispensary-region cities are marked ◆.

Additional jurisdictions noted in passing (not in the required minimum list, but relevant to avoid gaps): McKenzie County: Cited in 2018 reporting as the model for the Stutsman County draft (zoning categories + security + distances from schools, churches, parks, residences). The McKenzie County ordinance text was not opened in this run. VERIFY if McKenzie County is cited in the final report — do not quote its distances from the Stutsman draft description. Watford City (McKenzie County): A 2020 Public Service Commission filing extract references a “Compassionate Care Medical Marijuana Manufacturing Center” use and NDCC 19-24 compliance in a Watford City context, but the extract is too fragmentary to support any Watford City ordinance claim. No Watford City finding is made. Rolette County: Its zoning resolution was located only as a general Chapter 11-33 resolution (no cannabis provision in the extract). No cannabis finding for Rolette County.

Caps and bans — summary: No jurisdiction checked was found to impose a numerical cap on compassion centres within its boundaries (beyond the state caps), and no jurisdiction checked was found to impose a ban (outright prohibition) on compassion centres, on hemp/CBD retail, or on smoke/vape shops. The restrictive tools actually used are: (1) special-use-permit requirements (Williston, Dickinson, Ward County), (2) use-group interpretation (Bismarck, Grand Forks, Fargo), (3) added buffers (West Fargo 1,250 ft incl. daycare/residential; Williston 1,000 ft church / 300 ft residential), and (4) operating conditions (Dickinson odor filtration + secure waste; Williston odor/HVAC plan). A “ban” claim for any North Dakota jurisdiction on the sources checked would be incorrect and must not be made without an ordinance text. Moratoriums — summary: No moratorium ordinance (temporary ban) was located for any checked jurisdiction. The 2017–2018 drafting period is sometimes misdescribed as a moratorium; it was not — cities were drafting ordinances in anticipation of applications, while the state application windows had not yet opened. VERIFY if a specific moratorium is alleged for a specific city/county after 2018.

Local permit / licence fees: Compassion-centre special-use-permit fees: No dollar figure for a Williston SUP, Dickinson SUP (including its annual review), or Ward County special-use application was located in the sources opened. Ordinary business-licence requirements (where a city requires one) apply to a dispensary/manufacturer as to any business; the fee, if any, is the general fee. VERIFY per city. Tobacco-retailer local fees (for smoke/vape shops that sell tobacco/nicotine — NOT for dispensaries): Minot: Local retail tobacco licence $200 annually (or $0 if the premises is licensed for retail alcohol sales on the same premises, or $100 on relicensing if the applicant qualifies for reduced monitoring under Minot Ord. §18-71(b)); an amending ordinance was proposed to require all holders to pay $200 annually (the “proposed” status of that amendment VERIFY — the extract is an ordinance proposal amending §18-67, not a confirmed adoption). Licence issued by the city clerk on application, police-chief approval, and fee payment; no licence while under suspension. — Source: https://minotnd.gov/AgendaCenter/ViewFile/Item/7186?fileID=22046 (Minot ordinance proposal, §18-67) — Accessed 2026-10-30. Devils Lake: Chapter 9.74 (Ord. 1040, 2026) grants retail authority to state-licensed tobacco retailers and imposes administrative penalties ($500 / $1,000 + suspension — see §3.3); no separate local tobacco-licence fee amount was stated in the extract opened. VERIFY the fee, if any, in the full Chapter 9.74 text. State tobacco licence fee (for reference): $15 (dealer/retail application), $25 + $1,000 surety bond (distributor/manufacturer/wholesaler, new application only), via the Attorney General — https://attorneygeneral.nd.gov/licensing-and-gaming/licensing/tobacco/ — Accessed 2026-10-30. This is a state fee, listed here only because local tobacco authority piggybacks on it (Devils Lake §9.74.030). Dickinson: A tobacco licence was approved as a routine item for a named smoke shop in Jan 2025 (Dickinson City Commission), confirming Dickinson licenses tobacco retailers locally, but the fee schedule was not extracted. VERIFY against Dickinson's licence-fee schedule; do not quote a Dickinson tobacco fee. No CBD-specific local fee was located for any checked jurisdiction.

3.5 Business concentration — ONLY what official sources say

This section does not estimate, rank or map clusters. It reports only HHS's own regional structure and HHS's own list of registered locations, verbatim in substance.

HHS's eight dispensary regions (official HHS Annual Report, FY2024):

“State law identifies the DHHS is to register no more than eight dispensaries unless the DHHS determines additional entities are necessary to increase access to usable marijuana by registered qualifying patients and registered designated caregivers. The DHHS established eight regions within the state where dispensaries would be located. Regions are comprised of a 50-mile radius from selected cities. The first dispensary opened in Fargo in March 2019. Additional dispensaries opened during the 2019 calendar year with the eighth, and final, dispensary opening in December 2019 in Dickinson.”

Source (official, opened): https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Annual-Report-FY-2024-archive.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30.

The eight region centres (HHS / Department of Health announcements, 2018, as reported): Fargo, Bismarck/Mandan, Grand Forks, Minot, Williston, Dickinson, Jamestown and Devils Lake — each region being a 50-mile radius around the centre city, within which the region's single dispensary may be located anywhere (not necessarily in the centre city itself). Sources: https://mjbizdaily.com/news/north-dakota-specifies-medical-marijuana-dispensaries-can-open/83304/ (12 Jan 2018) and https://us1033.com/north-dakota-department-of-health-announces-eight-regions-where-marijuana-dispensaries-can-be-set-up/ — Accessed 2026-10-30 (secondary reporting of the HHS announcement; the HHS region map itself was not re-opened in this run — follow-up: locate the HHS region map / region list on hhs.nd.gov and cite it directly).

Registered compassion centres as of the FY2024 Annual Report (official HHS list, search extract of the same PDF — see Method note on the second-half read): Manufacturing facilities (2, the statutory maximum): Grassroots Cannabis in Fargo (legal name GR Vending ND, LLC); Pure Dakota, LLC in Bismarck. Dispensaries (8, the statutory maximum): Curaleaf in Devils Lake; Curaleaf in Dickinson; Curaleaf in Jamestown; Curaleaf in Minot; Pure Dakota Health in Bismarck; Pure Dakota Health in Fargo; Pure Dakota Health in Williston; We-Mend (Strive Life) in Grand Forks. Source (search extract of the official HHS FY2024 report): https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Annual-Report-FY-2024-archive.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Operator name variant note (reconciled): The Grand Forks operator appears as “We-Mend (Strive Life)” in the FY2024 report and as “Strive Life” on the current HHS dispensary-locations page (Part 4, §4.2) — same Grand Forks dispensary, trade-name drift, not a ninth dispensary. See Part 4/5 trade-name note.

What official sources do NOT say (and this report therefore does not say): HHS does not publish a ranking of cities by dispensary count, sales, or patient volume, and this report does not create one. (For the record: on the FY2024 list, Fargo and Bismarck each host both a manufacturing facility and a dispensary, and the other six region centres each host one dispensary — but that is a reading of the list, not an HHS “concentration” finding, and it must not be presented as HHS's analysis. The list itself is the finding.) HHS does not publish the location of hemp growers/processors by city in a form that supports a concentration claim in Part 3; hemp location data belongs to Part 5 (source inventory) and Part 7 (licences), not to a Part 3 cluster ranking. No official source was located stating that dispensaries are “concentrated” in any region, that any region is underserved, or that any city has “the most” cannabis businesses. Any such claim in a secondary source is not adopted here. No new dispensary or manufacturing facility has been added beyond the 2 + 8 on the sources checked (the caps are filled; HHS may register more only if it determines additional entities are necessary for patient access, or to replace a revoked registration — no such determination was located on 30 October 2026). Whether any registration has changed hands, closed, or been replaced since 30 June 2024 (e.g. any Curaleaf / Pure Dakota / We-Mend rebranding or ownership change) is VERIFY against the current HHS dispensary/manufacturing list — the FY2024 list is the latest annual-report list extracted, not a live register.
