Part 4 — Licence Types

4.0 Governing law and the one umbrella term

North Dakota does not have separate “cultivator”, “processor”, “retailer”, “transporter” or “delivery” licence classes for medical marijuana. It has one umbrella registration — the “compassion center” — in exactly two operating forms:

“'Compassion center' means a manufacturing facility or dispensary.” — NDCC § 19-24.1-01(13)
“'Dispensary' means an entity registered by the department as a compassion center authorized to dispense usable marijuana to a registered qualifying patient and a registered designated caregiver.” — NDCC § 19-24.1-01(19)
“'Manufacturing facility' means an entity registered by the department as a compassion center authorized to produce and process and to sell usable marijuana to a dispensary.” — NDCC § 19-24.1-01(23)

Source: https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. The regulator is the North Dakota Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), Division of Medical Marijuana (“the department” in NDCC ch. 19-24.1, § 19-24.1-01(17)). Programme hub, opened 2026-10-30: https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm — public contact published there: 701-328-1311, 711 (TTY), 600 E. Boulevard Ave., Dept. 325, Bismarck, ND 58505-0250 (programme contact as published on the hub; no personal email reproduced here beyond the hub's public programme address convention — see Part 4 source report for the hub's published programme mailbox).

The rules are NDAC Article 33-44, Chapter 33-44-01, “Medical Marijuana” (effective 1 April 2018; amended 1 October 2019, 1 July 2022, 1 October 2026 for the definitions section; individual sections carry their own history lines cited below). Official PDF opened 2026-10-30: https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf

Correction to the brief: the brief cited “NDAC Article 75-13”. No such article was found for medical marijuana in the official NDAC PDF or on the HHS programme pages opened. The HHS out-of-state page itself cites “NDCC Chapter 19-24.1 and NDAC Chapter 33-44-01” (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/out-of-state, accessed 2026-10-30). Treat “75-13” as Verify / superseded citation — do not cite it without checking whether it was a pre-merger (Department of Health) numbering that has since been renumbered to 33-44. Note on the HHS “State Laws and Administrative Rules” page: the link from the programme hub (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/state-laws-and-administrative-rules) returned unrelated factsheet links (anthrax, bed bugs, etc.) when fetched on 2026-10-30, not the statute/rule texts. The statute and rule PDFs above were therefore opened directly from ndlegis.gov instead. Flagged as a website defect, not a legal gap.

4.1 Manufacturing facility (compassion center — manufacturing)

What it allows — verbatim operative text:

“The activities of a manufacturing facility are limited to producing and processing and to related activities, including acquiring, possessing, storing, transferring, and transporting marijuana and usable marijuana, for the sole purpose of selling usable marijuana to a dispensary.” — NDCC § 19-24.1-13(1)

Source: https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Consequences that follow from that sentence alone: A manufacturing facility may not sell directly to a patient or caregiver — only to a dispensary. There is no separate cultivation-only or processing-only statutory licence in NDCC ch. 19-24.1 itself. The two sub-categories below exist only in administrative rule (see §4.3). “Producing” means “planting, cultivating, growing, trimming, or harvesting … or the drying of the leaves or flowers” (NDCC § 19-24.1-01(40)); “processing” means “compounding or conversion of marijuana into a medical marijuana product” (§ 19-24.1-01(39)). Production must be in an “enclosed, locked facility” — “a closet, room, greenhouse, building, or other enclosed area equipped with locks or other security devices that permit access limited to individuals authorized under this chapter” (§ 19-24.1-01(20)). No home cultivation exists for anyone else: only a registered compassion center may produce/process/dispense (§ 19-24.1-12(1): “A person may not process or produce or dispense usable marijuana or otherwise act as a compassion center in this state unless the person is registered as a compassion center”).

Statutory cap: 2 — NDCC § 19-24.1-12(2)(a): “the department shall register no more than: a. Two compassion centers with the sole purpose of operating as a manufacturing facility”. Both slots are filled (see Part 5 counts). Additional-compassion-center exception (both types): § 19-24.1-12(4): “The department may register additional compassion centers if the department determines additional compassion centers are necessary to increase access to usable marijuana by registered qualifying patients and registered designated caregivers.” NDAC § 33-44-01-07 adds that for additional centres the department must also consider “the location of the proposed compassion center, including its proximity to previously approved compassion centers of the same category and whether the population of registered qualifying patients supports the need for an additional facility in the area” (effective 1 April 2018). Inventory / plant rule (rule, not statute): NDAC § 33-44-01-08(1): a manufacturing facility “shall grow an amount of marijuana sufficient to meet the qualifying patient population demands” and “may possess up to fifty plants for … department-authorized research and development” (separate restricted area; not for sale unless the department authorises in writing). There is no general statutory plant cap in NDCC ch. 19-24.1; the fee schedule instead prices plants above 1,000 (see §4.6). A dispensary “may not possess more than three thousand five hundred ounces [99.22 kilograms] of usable marijuana at any time, regardless of formulation” (NDAC § 33-44-01-08(3)).

4.2 Dispensary (compassion center — dispensary)

What it allows — verbatim operative text:

“The activities of a dispensary are limited to purchasing usable marijuana from a manufacturing facility, and related activities, including storing, delivering, transferring, and transporting usable marijuana, for the sole purpose of dispensing usable marijuana to a registered qualifying patient, directly or through the registered qualifying patient's registered designated caregiver. The activities of a dispensary include providing educational material and selling usable marijuana related supplies to a registered qualifying patient or a registered designated caregiver.” — NDCC § 19-24.1-13(2)

Source: https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. So a dispensary may buy only from a North Dakota manufacturing facility and sell only to registered qualifying patients / their registered designated caregivers. It may not buy from out of state, and (per the HHS out-of-state page) “State law has no protections for individuals in North Dakota with marijuana or marijuana products not purchased from an in-state registered dispensary” (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/out-of-state, accessed 2026-10-30). Statutory cap: 8 — NDCC § 19-24.1-12(2)(b): “Eight compassion centers with the sole purpose of operating as a dispensary.” All 8 are operating (see Part 5).

Regional placement — the 8 regions (department policy, not a statutory county list): HHS “established eight regions within the state where dispensaries would be located. Regions are comprised of a 50-mile radius from selected cities. The first dispensary opened in Fargo in March 2019. Additional dispensaries opened during the 2019 calendar year with the eighth, and final, dispensary opening in December 2019 in Dickinson.” — HHS Medical Marijuana Program Annual Report, Fiscal Year 2024, p. 2 (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Fiscal%20Year%202024.pdf, accessed 2026-10-30). The eight region centres, as announced by the Department of Health in 2018 and confirmed by the FY2024 report's dispensary list, are: Fargo, Bismarck/Mandan, Grand Forks, Minot, Williston, Dickinson, Jamestown, Devils Lake — each a 50-mile radius around the named city. Secondary-source confirmation (not law): MJBizDaily, 12 Jan 2018, “North Dakota specifies where state's medical marijuana dispensaries can open” (https://mjbizdaily.com/news/north-dakota-specifies-medical-marijuana-dispensaries-can-open/83304/) and 3 July 2018 phased-application report (https://mjbizdaily.com/news/north-dakota-begins-first-phase-of-accepting-medical-cannabis-dispensary-applications/99722/?utm_mediu_3). Verify note: the current HHS dispensary-locations page (opened 2026-10-30, see Part 5) lists one dispensary in each of Bismarck, Grand Forks, Devils Lake, Jamestown, Dickinson, Minot, Fargo and Williston — i.e. one per region, none in Mandan itself — consistent with the 50-mile-radius design. The statute itself does not name the eight cities; the regions are department-established policy under § 19-24.1-12. Do not cite the region list as statutory text.

Current dispensary roster (HHS dispensary-locations page, opened 2026-10-30 — public business addresses, reproduced because these are official public licence-holder listings, not personal data): https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/dispensary-locations

City (region centre)Dispensary (as listed by HHS)Address as listed

This roster is not a full licence-holder list for Part 5 purposes — it is the Part 4 regional-placement evidence. Manufacturing-facility addresses are not published on that page (see Part 5 limitation); the FY2024 report names them only by city/operator (Fargo and Bismarck, Part 5). Reconciled operator note: manufacturers are Grassroots Cannabis / GR Vending ND, LLC, Fargo + Pure Dakota, LLC, Bismarck; dispensaries are Pure Dakota Health Bismarck/Fargo/Williston, Curaleaf Devils Lake/Dickinson/Jamestown/Minot, Strive Life (FY2024: We-Mend / Strive Life) Grand Forks — note the Grand Forks operator name variant as reported.

4.3 Sub-categories created by rule only: production-only and processor-only manufacturing facilities

NDAC § 33-44-01-07.1 (effective 1 October 2022; general authority NDCC § 19-24.1-36) lets the department “use the compassion center application and selection process … to register a manufacturing facility as a specific category of a medical marijuana establishment”: 1. Production-only authorised manufacturing facility — “limited to producing and to related activities, including acquiring, possessing, storing, transferring, and transporting marijuana and the dried leaves or flowers of the plant of the genus cannabis in a combustible delivery form for the sole purpose of selling dried leaves or flowers … in a combustible delivery form to a dispensary.” 2. Medical marijuana product processor-only authorised manufacturing facility — “limited to producing and processing and to related activities, including acquiring, possessing, storing, transferring, and transporting marijuana and medical marijuana products for the sole purpose of selling medical marijuana products to a dispensary.” Source: https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Important: these are not separate statutory licence types and no separate statutory cap applies to them in NDCC ch. 19-24.1; a facility registered in one of these categories still counts as a manufacturing-facility compassion center for cap purposes. Whether either sub-category is currently in use by either of the two registered manufacturing facilities is Verify — the FY2024 report names only two full manufacturing facilities and does not break out production-only / processor-only registrations, and no HHS page found for this report lists a production-only or processor-only holder. Do not assume either sub-category is occupied.

4.4 Testing laboratory — NOT a compassion-center licence; it is a state contract

North Dakota has no licensed independent testing-lab licence class. Testing is by a laboratory contracted/selected by the department:

“The department may contract with a laboratory or laboratories to conduct random quality sampling testing of a compassion center's marijuana and usable marijuana. The department shall procure the laboratory testing services in accordance with North Dakota Century Code chapter 54-44.4. An awarded laboratory must be properly accredited as determined by the department.” — NDAC § 33-44-01-36 (effective 1 April 2018; amended 1 October 2019)
“The activities of a department-awarded laboratory include providing laboratory services and related activities, including acquiring, possessing, storing, transferring, and transporting marijuana, usable marijuana, and medical marijuana waste…” — NDAC § 33-44-01-37.
“A manufacturing facility shall have all usable marijuana tested … by a laboratory selected by the department as described in section 33-44-01-36. The manufacturing facility shall pay all costs of testing…” — NDAC § 33-44-01-40(1). “A manufacturing facility may not transfer usable marijuana to a dispensary until it is tested and passes compliance testing” (§ 33-44-01-40(2)).

Source: https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Laboratory agents get registry identification cards (NDAC § 33-44-01-38: issued “within thirty calendar days and at no cost”, expiring with the contract), and awarded laboratories are subject to random department inspection (§ 33-44-01-39). Historical holder (secondary sources only — current holder Verify): in May 2018 the Department of Health selected Keystone State Testing (Pennsylvania), operating in Fargo as Dakota State Testing, as the testing vendor (MJBizDaily, 22 May 2018: https://mjbizdaily.com/news/north-dakota-selects-medical-marijuana-manufacturers-lab-for-licensing/93763/; Cannabis Business Times, 22 May 2018: https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/us-states/north-dakota/news/15695747/north-dakota-chooses-medical-marijuana-laboratory-company). No current HHS page found for this report names the presently awarded laboratory or the contract term, and no open application/procurement notice was found. Current lab identity, contract dates and whether more than one lab is now awarded are all Verify. Do not present Dakota State Testing / Keystone as the current lab without re-confirmation — the 2018 award is 8 years old and procurement under NDCC ch. 54-44.4 would normally have been re-competed.

No other licence types exist in current law. In particular there is: no standalone transporter/courier licence (transport is a “related activity” of a manufacturing facility or dispensary under § 19-24.1-13); no standalone delivery licence (delivery is within dispensary activities, § 19-24.1-13(2)); no wholesaler/distributor licence; no research licence in ch. 19-24.1 (the only research carve-out is the 50-plant R&D allowance in NDAC § 33-44-01-08(1), §4.1 above); and no patient/caregiver cultivation licence (home grow is prohibited for all).

4.5 How licences were awarded — competitive, scored, phased; no lottery

Statute: NDCC § 19-24.1-12(3): “The department shall establish an open application period for the submission of compassion center applications. At the completion of the open application period, the department shall review each complete application using a competitive process established in accordance with rules adopted under this chapter and shall determine which applicants to register as compassion centers.” § 19-24.1-12(5): “If the department revokes or does not renew a compassion center registration certificate, the department may establish an open application period for the submission of compassion center applications.” Rule — NDAC § 33-44-01-06 (effective 1 April 2018; amended 1 October 2022): 1. The department announces the open application period (website, email, press release or other means) stating instructions, forms, deadline, criteria and score sheet, number and category of centres eligible, and contact information. 2. It may use a separate open application period for each category. 3. Each proposed centre must be a separate legal entity and submit a complete application. 4. A panel of at least 3 and no more than 5 members evaluates complete applications; members sign a conflict-of-interest form and a conflicted member may not participate. 5. The panel uses “an impartial and numerical scoring system”, must include the § 19-24.1-14(2) criteria, and each panel member scores every complete application; “The cumulative total of all the scores … is the final score. The final score will determine which applicants are eligible for registration.” 6. The department notifies the highest-scoring applicants in writing; on satisfaction of § 19-24.1-15 it issues the registration certificate. “A separate legal entity may possess only one compassion center registration certificate.” 7. If an eligible applicant fails § 19-24.1-15, the department may select the next-highest scorer or open a new period. There is no lottery in statute or rule. Any description of North Dakota awards as lottery-based is wrong for this programme.

Application contents (NDCC § 19-24.1-14(1), abridged — full list in the statute PDF): nonrefundable application fee (see §4.6); legal name, articles of incorporation/organisation and bylaws/operating agreement; evidence of Secretary of State registration and certificate of good standing; physical address plus (i) evidence of local officials' approval of compliance with local zoning laws and (ii) evidence the address is not within 1,000 feet of a property line of a pre-existing public or private school; for a manufacturing facility, a description of the enclosed, locked facility including steps to ensure production/processing is not visible from the street or public areas; name/address/date of birth of each principal officer/board member (or member-manager/manager/governor) and consent to a criminal-history record check under NDCC § 12-60-24; each such person's relevant experience (medicine, pharmaceuticals, natural treatments, botany, food science, food safety, production, processing, business experience); security/safety measures; container design/security features; a complete operations manual; a plan for affordable access for patients with limited financial resources; lists of all persons/entities with direct/indirect management/policy authority and all ownership interests (including land/building owners); and identity of any creditor holding a security interest in the premises. Review criteria (NDCC § 19-24.1-14(2)): suitability of location (including local zoning compliance and geographic convenience for patients statewide); character and relevant experience of principal officers/board members (or managers/governors); operations/services plan (staffing/training, sufficient capital, ability to provide adequate supply); sufficiency of recordkeeping plans; safety/security/diversion-prevention plans (location, security devices); affordable-access plan; safe/accurate packaging and labelling plan; and testing plans.

Historical award timeline (secondary sources, for context only — not law): manufacturing-facility applications opened spring 2018 (19 applicants reported); Pure Dakota (Bismarck) and GR Vending ND, LLC d/b/a Grassroots Cannabis (Fargo) selected May 2018 (MJBizDaily, 22 May 2018, above). Dispensary applications were phased: Bismarck and Fargo from 10 July 2018; Grand Forks and Williston from autumn 2018; Devils Lake, Dickinson, Jamestown and Minot from January 2019 (application period closed 26 February 2019 for the last four, per the Western Planner roundtable summary of the programme). All eight dispensaries were open by December 2019 (FY2024 report, §4.2 above). No new open application period has been announced since — see §4.8.

4.6 Fees — statute ceilings vs. rule amounts vs. the 1 November 2026 step-up (read carefully)

There are three layers and they do not all say the same number. This is the single most error-prone area in secondary summaries, so all three are set out:

Layer 1 — Statutory ceilings (NDCC §§ 19-24.1-14(1)(a), 19-24.1-15(1)(a), 19-24.1-16(2)(a)): application fee “not to exceed five thousand dollars”; certification fee “not to exceed ninety thousand dollars for a dispensary and one hundred ten thousand dollars for a manufacturing facility”; renewal fee the same ceilings. These are maximums, not the amounts actually charged in every year.

Layer 2 — Rule amounts actually charged (NDAC §§ 33-44-01-07.2, -07.3, -07.4):

FeeRule amountHistory
Application — manufacturing facility$3,000, nonrefundableNDAC § 33-44-01-07.2(1); effective 1 Oct 2022, amended 1 Oct 2023
Application — dispensary$2,000, nonrefundableNDAC § 33-44-01-07.2(2); same history
Certification — manufacturing facility, through 31 Oct 2026$75,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(1)(a)
Certification — dispensary, through 31 Oct 2026$60,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(1)(b)
Certification — production-only manufacturing facility, through 31 Oct 2026$40,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(1)(c)
Certification — processor-only manufacturing facility, through 31 Oct 2026$20,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(1)(d)
Certification — manufacturing facility, from 1 Nov 2026$110,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(2)(a)
Certification — dispensary, from 1 Nov 2026$90,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(2)(b)
Certification — production-only, from 1 Nov 2026$70,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(2)(c)
Certification — processor-only, from 1 Nov 2026$50,000NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3(2)(d)
Additional certification fee$5,000 for every 500 plants in excess of 1,000 plants a manufacturing facility possessesNDAC § 33-44-01-07.4; effective 1 Oct 2023
Compassion-center agent registry card application/renewalup to $200 (statute, NDCC § 19-24.1-18(2)(g)); rule amount $200 (NDAC § 33-44-01-03.2(3))NDAC § 33-44-01-03.2, amended 1 Oct 2025
Failure-to-notify fee (centre)$150NDAC § 33-44-01-03; NDCC § 19-24.1-20(1) ceiling $150

Source for all rule amounts: https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Secondary confirmation of the 1 Nov 2026 step-up (The Marijuana Herald, Sept 2026: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/north-dakota-to-sharply-increase-medical-marijuana-business-fees-with-some-rising-150/) matches the rule text exactly, including the 150% processor-only rise ($20,000 → $50,000). Note the step-up restores the full-manufacturing and dispensary fees to their statutory ceilings; the pre-November 2026 discounts ($75,000/$60,000) were the 2023 reductions, not the statutory rates. Any source quoting “$90,000/$110,000” for a pre-November 2026 certification, or “$60,000/$75,000” for a post-1-November-2026 certification, is using the wrong layer for that date. Reconciliation note: Part 2, §2.4 reported a secondary $5,000 payable per 500 plants (statutory ceiling $7,500) figure as unverified; Part 4, from the NDAC text opened, gives $5,000 per 500 plants over 1,000 (NDAC §33-44-01-07.4). The $5,000 NDAC figure is the one to use; the the superseded 2019 figure is flagged as superseded/conflicting in the Gaps Table and must not be used in revenue calculations. Part 2's instruction to “confirm or delete” the the superseded 2019 figure is therefore resolved in favour of $5,000 on the NDAC text, subject to a final live NDAC re-check for the 1 Nov 2026 schedule.

Layer 3 — Financial assurance / security bond (NDCC § 19-24.1-15(1)(b) + NDAC § 33-44-01-06(10)): an applicant eligible for registration must submit “a financial assurance or security bond to ensure the protection of the public health and safety and the environment in the event of abandonment, default, or other inability or unwillingness to meet the requirements of this chapter”. The department determines amount and acceptable evidence, subject to caps: no more than $100,000 for a dispensary and no more than $1,000,000 for a manufacturing facility (NDAC § 33-44-01-06(10)). The exact bond amount actually required of each current holder is Verify — it is set by the department case-by-case within those caps and is not published in the sources opened. Secondary surety sources quoting “$1M” as the bond are quoting the manufacturing cap, not a confirmed assessed amount. Renewal fees: NDCC § 19-24.1-16(2)(a) caps renewal at the same $90,000/$110,000 ceilings; the renewal fee actually charged follows the NDAC § 33-44-01-07.3 certification schedule in force at renewal (see table above). The renewal fee “shall [be] refund[ed] if the department rejects the renewal application” (§ 19-24.1-16(2)(a)) — unlike the nonrefundable application fee. Patient/caregiver/agent fees are not licence fees and are excluded from Part 4 except for the agent card above: patient application $40 (NDAC § 33-44-01-03.2(1)–(2)); agent card $200 (above).

4.7 Requirements, ownership caps, transfer and change rules

Entity and good-standing requirements: separate legal entity per centre (NDAC § 33-44-01-06(4)); Secretary of State registration + certificate of good standing (NDCC § 19-24.1-14(1)(c)); one registration certificate per legal entity (NDAC § 33-44-01-06(9)). Ownership caps — verbatim (NDCC § 19-24.1-13(3)):

“An individual or organization may not hold an ownership interest in: a. More than one manufacturing facility. b. More than four dispensaries. c. More than one dispensary within a twenty-mile [32.19 kilometer] radius of another dispensary.”

“Ownership interest” = “an aggregate ownership interest of five percent or more in a compassion center, unless the interest is solely a security, lien, or encumbrance, or an individual who will be participating in the direction, control, or management of the compassion center” (NDCC § 19-24.1-01(34)). “Member” (for LLC/LLP/partnership centres) = 10% or more (§ 19-24.1-01(30)). Anti-exclusivity rule (NDCC § 19-24.1-13(4)): “An agreement may not be entered between a manufacturing facility and dispensary whereby a dispensary agrees to limit purchases or sales of usable marijuana to one manufacturing facility.” This is why the current market structure (Pure Dakota supplying its own 3 dispensaries plus Curaleaf/Strive buying from both manufacturers) is a commercial pattern, not a lawful exclusivity requirement — Verify the actual supply pattern from primary sources before asserting it; it is not stated in the FY2024 report. No vertical-integration mandate or ban in statute: the statute neither requires a manufacturer to own dispensaries nor forbids common ownership up to the caps above. In practice the FY2024 roster shows Pure Dakota operating both a manufacturing facility (Bismarck) and 3 dispensaries, and Curaleaf operating 4 dispensaries (the maximum) with no North Dakota manufacturing facility — both within the caps. Whether Grassroots/Curaleaf's Fargo manufacturing facility and its dispensaries share ownership is Verify from corporate records (see Part 5); do not infer it from brand names alone.

Agent eligibility (NDCC § 19-24.1-18): every compassion-center agent (principal officer, board member, member, manager, governor, employee, volunteer or agent, § 19-24.1-01(14)) must be 21 or older, hold a registry identification card, consent to a criminal-history record check under NDCC § 12-60-24 (initial + biennial on renewal, and at any time the department requires), and is prohibited if “convicted of a drug-related misdemeanor offense within the five-year period before the date of application or a felony offense” (§ 19-24.1-18(3)(c)). Agent cards expire 1 year after issuance or on termination of the centre's certificate, whichever is first; renewal application no less than 45 days before expiry (§ 19-24.1-18(10)). The department pays the criminal-history check fees for agents (§ 19-24.1-18(3)(a)) — contrast hemp, where the applicant pays (Part 5 note). Transfer — certificates are NONTRANSFERABLE, but amendment is possible with prior approval (NDCC § 19-24.1-17):

“1. Upon application of a compassion center to the department, a registration certificate of a compassion center may be amended to authorize a change in the authorized physical location of the compassion center, or to amend the ownership or organizational structure of the compassion center with the registration certificate. A compassion center shall provide the department written notice of any change described under this section at least sixty calendar days before the proposed effective date of the change.
2. A registration certificate authorizing the operation of a compassion center is void by a change in ownership, substantial corporate change, change in location, or discontinued operation, without prior approval of the department. The department may adopt rules allowing for certain types of changes in ownership without the need for prior written approval from the department.
3. The department shall authorize the use of additional structures located within five hundred feet [152.40 meters] of the location described in the original application, unless [it] makes an affirmative finding the use … would jeopardize public health or safety or would result in the cannabis business being within one thousand feet [304.80 meters] of a property line of a pre-existing public or private school.”

In plain English: you cannot sell/assign the certificate itself to a new owner as a standalone asset; a change of ownership or location must be applied for and approved in advance (60 days' notice), and an unapproved change voids the certificate. Whether the department has adopted rules allowing any ownership changes without prior approval under § 19-24.1-17(2), last sentence, is Verify — no such rule was located in NDAC ch. 33-44-01 as opened. Renewal (NDCC § 19-24.1-16): certificate “expires two years after issuance”. Renewal application “at any time beginning ninety calendar days before” expiry and “a minimum of sixty calendar days before” expiry “to avoid suspension”. The department “shall approve” within 60 days of submission if: renewal fee paid; complete application; the centre has “at no time” been suspended for violation; inspections raise no serious concerns; and the centre continues to meet all chapter/rule requirements. If renewal is not granted, the centre “shall dispose all marijuana and usable marijuana in accordance with rules” (§ 19-24.1-16(3)). Suspension/revocation (NDCC § 19-24.1-19): the department may suspend or revoke a card or certificate for material misstatement or for violation of the chapter/rules; suspension “may not be for a period longer than six months”; during suspension a manufacturing facility may continue to produce/process/possess but may not transfer or sell, and a dispensary may continue to possess but may not purchase, dispense, or transfer; sale/transfer to an unauthorised person requires revocation. Appeal within 30 days to Burleigh County district court. No compassion-center suspension or revocation was identified in the sources opened for this report — see Part 5 on the absence of a published enforcement list. Other standing requirements (for completeness, not full operations rules — those are Part 11): local zoning compliance + 1,000-foot school buffer (§ 19-24.1-14(1)(d)); enclosed, locked facility (§ 19-24.1-01(20)); operations manual (§ 19-24.1-14(1)(j)); security/safety measures (§ 19-24.1-14(1)(h)); “The department of commerce may not certify a compassion center as a primary sector business” (§ 19-24.1-12(6)) — i.e. no primary-sector tax-incentive certification.

4.8 Are new licences open now? — NO (as of 30 October 2026, on the evidence available)

  • Both statutory caps are full: 2/2 manufacturing facilities, 8/8 dispensaries (FY2024 report + current HHS dispensary-locations page, §§4.1–4.2, Part 5).
  • A new open application period can arise only (a) if the department determines additional centres are necessary for patient access (§ 19-24.1-12(4)), or (b) if a certificate is revoked or not renewed (§ 19-24.1-12(5)).
  • No open application period announcement was found on the HHS programme hub (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm, opened 2026-10-30 — no application-period notice appears on the hub page text), in the FY2024 report, or in any 2025–2026 HHS release located for this report.
  • Caveat: NDAC § 33-44-01-06(1) allows announcement “using the department's website, electronic mail, press release, or any other means” — so absence from the hub page alone does not prove no period is open; it proves no period was found in the official sources opened. The correct live check is the HHS hub + a direct enquiry to the programme before advising any applicant. State this as: no open period found; Verify live before acting.
  • The practical entry route while caps are full is therefore (i) acquisition/amendment of an existing centre's ownership or location with prior department approval under § 19-24.1-17 (certificate itself nontransferable — see §4.7), or (ii) waiting for a department determination under § 19-24.1-12(4)/(5). Both routes require department approval; neither is an open application.

4.9 Social equity provisions — explicit absence

There is no social-equity programme, set-aside, priority, fee waiver/reduction, or dedicated funding for medical-marijuana licensing in NDCC ch. 19-24.1 or NDAC ch. 33-44-01 as opened. The scoring criteria in § 19-24.1-14(2) and NDAC § 33-44-01-06 contain no equity criterion; the only affordability-related criterion is the applicant's “plan for making usable marijuana available on an affordable basis to registered qualifying patients with limited financial resources” (§ 19-24.1-14(1)(k), (2)(f)) — a patient-affordability plan, not an applicant-equity preference. Secondary confirmation (not law): CannBus North Dakota Legal Summary Q3 2026 states “North Dakota does not offer a state social equity program for medical cannabis licensing. There are no state-level licensing priorities, set-asides, fee waivers/reductions, or dedicated funding for applicants from communities disproportionately affected by cannabis prohibition” (https://www.cannbus.org/lsum/nd_lsum.html, accessed via search 2026-10-30). The same summary notes no state grant/loan/fee-waiver programme for medical cannabis businesses (https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/nd_cannmir.html). Treat the secondary confirmation as consistent with, but not a substitute for, the statute/rule text above. Related absence: no veteran, small-business, minority-owned or in-state-residency preference appears in the application criteria either. Residency is not a compassion-center criterion in § 19-24.1-14 (contrast patient/caregiver rules, which are outside Part 4). If a future bill adds equity provisions, it will be a new enactment, not an interpretation of current law.

4.10 Estimated startup costs — NON-OFFICIAL estimates only, marked as such

No official North Dakota startup-cost estimate exists in any official source opened. The state publishes fees (above) but “The market — not the state — will dictate what the drug costs, and patients are not allowed to grow their own” (contemporaneous AP reporting of the programme design, 2017 — secondary only). Do not present any total below as official. Official, quantifiable entry costs (from §4.6, for a full manufacturing facility or a dispensary, 2-year certificate cycle from 1 Nov 2026): Application (one-off, nonrefundable): $3,000 (manufacturing) / $2,000 (dispensary) — rule amounts; statute ceiling $5,000. Certification (per 2-year cycle): $110,000 (manufacturing) / $90,000 (dispensary); plus $5,000 per 500 plants over 1,000 for a manufacturer. Bond/financial assurance: department-determined, capped at $1,000,000 (manufacturing) / $100,000 (dispensary) — the bond premium/collateral cost is not the cap; actual cost Verify per applicant. Agent cards: $200 per agent per year (each agent, annually). Testing costs: borne by the manufacturing facility (NDAC § 33-44-01-40(1)) — rates are in the department–laboratory contract, not published in the sources opened: Verify.

Non-official, third-party estimate (mark as such in any reuse): Cannabis Training University's state-by-state dispensary-cost guide (accessed via search 2026-10-30: https://cannabistraininguniversity.com/cannabis-industry/how-much-does-it-cost-to-open-a-dispensary/) gives a North Dakota dispensary startup range of $500,000–$1 million, citing a $5,000 application fee and $90,000 licence fee. Problems with that figure for reuse: (a) it uses the statutory-ceiling application fee ($5,000), not the rule amount ($2,000); (b) it is a generic national-template range, not a North Dakota build-out costing; (c) it predates or ignores the 1 Nov 2026 fee step-up context and gives no date, build-out, inventory, security-system, payroll or rent breakdown for North Dakota. Use it only as an order-of-magnitude illustration with those caveats, or not at all. No non-official manufacturing-facility startup estimate specific to North Dakota was found that is worth quoting; national cultivation build-outs vary by orders of magnitude with facility size and are not transferable without a stated facility size. Bottom line for Part 4: the only defensible North Dakota startup-cost statement is the fee + bond-cap + agent-card arithmetic above, plus “all other costs (premises, build-out, security, inventory, staffing, testing, insurance) are applicant-specific and unpublished.”

4.11 PROPOSED adult-use licence types — FAILED measures only (not law)

Nothing in this section is current law. All items are PROPOSED and DEFEATED unless stated otherwise. Do not cite them as licence types available in North Dakota.

Initiated Measure 5 (2024) — DEFEATED 5 November 2024 (PROPOSED): Would have required HHS (or another agency designated by the legislature) to establish marijuana regulations, including production/distribution rules, by 1 October 2025. Would have allowed licensing of up to 7 cultivation/manufacturing facilities and up to 18 marijuana retailers (dispensaries) — the measure's own text used “manufacturing facility” and “dispensary” terminology carried over from the medical programme, with the same ownership caps (no more than 1 manufacturing facility, no more than 4 dispensaries, no more than 1 dispensary within 20 miles of another) and the same fee ceilings ($5,000 application; $90,000 dispensary / $110,000 manufacturing certification), plus a proposed $7,500 fee per 500 plants over 1,000 (total plants capped at 10,000 in the MPP summary). Sources (secondary summaries of the measure text, not the measure as law): Ballotpedia, “North Dakota Initiated Measure 5, Marijuana Legalization Initiative (2024)” — status Defeated (https://ballotpedia.org/North_Dakota_Initiated_Measure_5,_Marijuana_Legalization_Initiative_(2024), accessed via search 2026-10-30); Marijuana Policy Project summary of Measure 5 (https://www.mpp.org//assets/pdf/states/north-dakota/summary-of-measure-5-north-dakotas-2024-cannabis-initiative.pdf?v=1789789419). Official result to be confirmed by the Part 9 researcher from the Secretary of State canvass; secondary tallies report ~52.5% No / ~47.5% Yes. Do not quote a final percentage in Part 4 as official until that canvass is opened. Earlier adult-use measures (PROPOSED, DEFEATED — licence detail Verify for Parts 9/1): 2018 Measure 3 and 2022 Measure 2 were also defeated. Their proposed licence structures are not detailed here because no official measure text was opened for this Part 4/5 report; the Part 1/9 researcher must open the Secretary of State measure texts before describing them. Do not carry forward the 2024 caps (7/18) to 2018 or 2022 — each measure had its own text. Pending adult-use legislation / 2026 initiative (PROPOSED, status Verify): no adult-use licence bill or certified 2026 initiative was confirmed from an official source (ndlegis.gov / Secretary of State) for this report. A secondary market report (CannBus Q3 2026) states that as of May 2026 no adult-use measure had been certified for the November 2026 ballot, but that is unconfirmed by an official source and is flagged Verify, not adopted. North Dakota's legislature meets biennially (regular sessions in odd-numbered years; the 69th Legislative Assembly sat in 2025, next regular session 2027) — so any 2026 adult-use change would have to come by initiative, not by a 2026 regular-session bill, unless a special session is called. That session point must be confirmed by the Part 1 researcher from ndlegis.gov. Hemp licences are NOT adult-use licences: NDDA hemp grower/processor licences (NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1) are agricultural licences, not cannabis-retail licences, and are covered as data sources in Part 5 and in full in Part 7. Do not list them as medical or adult-use licence types.

Part 5 — Licence Data Sources

Do NOT try to list every licence holder here — this part inventories sources instead. Reading rule: “Downloadable file” means a file the source itself offers for download (PDF/XLSX/CSV). A web page that merely displays a list is recorded as “interactive page / web list — no download button found”, even if a browser can print/save it. Where a page was opened and no export button was visible in the fetched text, that is stated; a button rendered only by JavaScript that the text fetch cannot see is flagged Verify (live check needed) rather than asserted absent. No source below is a full licence-holder register with addresses, licence numbers and expiry dates for medical marijuana — North Dakota does not publish one in the sources opened. That absence is itself the headline finding of Part 5.

5.1 HHS Dispensary Locations page — the ONLY current public dispensary list

  • URL: https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/dispensary-locations
  • Holder type: registered dispensaries only. Manufacturing facilities are not listed here (no manufacturing-facility locations page was found on the hub).
  • Format: HTML web list, grouped by city, with dispensary name, street address, public phone, operator website link and store hours. Opened 2026-10-30; full roster reproduced in Part 4, §4.2 above (8 entries).
  • How to access: open the URL directly (linked as “Dispensary Locations” from https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm). No login, no fee.
  • Download/export button: none found in the fetched page. It is an interactive web page with no download option, no CSV/XLSX, no PDF version linked from the page text. (Live JavaScript check: Verify — the text fetch cannot rule out a print function, but there is no stated export.)
  • Update frequency as stated: none stated on the page. No “last updated” stamp was visible in the fetched text.
  • Latest data date: undated on the page itself. Cross-check: the 8 names/cities match the FY2024 annual report roster exactly (see §5.2), and the page was live on 2026-10-30. Any change of operator, address or hours after the last HHS edit would appear here first — but without a date stamp, a reader cannot tell from the page alone when it was last edited. Verify currency by phone/email to the programme before relying on hours.
  • Fields available: city, dispensary trade name, street address, public phone, operator website, store hours (incl. Mountain Time note for Dickinson). Not available: licence/registration-certificate number, certificate issue/expiry date, legal entity name (vs. trade name), ownership, manufacturing-facility counterpart, or patient counts per dispensary.
  • Limitations: (1) dispensaries only; (2) no manufacturing facilities; (3) no licence numbers or dates; (4) no archive/version history — a superseded roster cannot be recovered from this page; (5) hours are operator-supplied and may lag reality.

5.2 HHS Medical Marijuana Annual Reports — the ONLY source that names manufacturing facilities and gives counts over time

  • What they are: reports formerly required by NDCC § 19-24.1-39 (“Report to legislative management”), which required the department to report: (1) registry-card applications and renewals; (2) numbers of registered qualifying patients, designated caregivers and compassion-center agents; (3) nature of debilitating conditions; (4) cards revoked; (5) health-care providers completing certifications; (6) number of compassion centers; (7) expenses and revenues; (8) statistical data with no individual identification. (FY2024 report, p. 3, citing § 19-24.1-39.)
  • CRITICAL — the annual-report requirement has been repealed: NDCC §§ 19-24.1-38 (Advisory board) and 19-24.1-39 (Report to legislative management) are both marked “Repealed by S.L. 2025, ch. 479, § 39” in the current NDCC PDF (https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf, accessed 2026-10-30). HHS's own 2025 legislative summary confirms: “Senate Bill No. 2308 will eliminate the Medical Marijuana Advisory Board as well as the annual report requirements for the program” (effective 1 August 2025) (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Summary%20of%20Bills%20Passed%202025.pdf, accessed 2026-10-30). Consequence: FY2024 may be the last annual report ever published. No FY2025 annual report was found for this report — Verify whether HHS will publish any successor statistical release, and do not promise a future annual report in Part 6.
  • Format: PDF, one file per fiscal year. Interactive dashboard? No. These are static PDFs.
  • Exact direct download links + file names found (all under the HHS “DOH Legacy/MM” documents path):
    • FY2024: https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Fiscal%20Year%202024.pdf — file name as served: Fiscal Year 2024.pdf
    • FY2024 archived copy (same content, “archived” watermark): https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Annual-Report-FY-2024-archive.pdf — file name: Annual-Report-FY-2024-archive.pdf
    • FY2022 (revised 16 Jan 2023): https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Annual-Report-FY-2022-Revised-archive.pdf — file name: Annual-Report-FY-2022-Revised-archive.pdf
    • The hub's “Archived” link (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm, link 8) is the route to older years; individual FY2019, FY2020, FY2021, FY2023 PDF URLs were not opened for this report — the Part 6 researcher should harvest them from the Archived page rather than guessing URLs. Do not construct URLs by pattern.
  • How to access/download: open any URL above directly; no login, no fee. The PDFs are the download.
  • Update frequency as stated: annual (fiscal year, 1 July–30 June), now discontinued per the repeal above.
  • Latest data date: 30 June 2024 (FY2024 report: “Data is reported as of June 30, 2024”).
  • Fields available (FY2024, with the counts the Part 5 commission asked for): Manufacturing facilities (names + cities): Grassroots Cannabis in Fargo (legal name GR Vending ND, LLC); Pure Dakota, LLC in Bismarck. Total: 2. Dispensaries (names + cities): Curaleaf in Devils Lake, Dickinson, Jamestown, Minot (4); Pure Dakota Health in Bismarck, Fargo, Williston (3); We-Mend (Strive Life) in Grand Forks (1). Total: 8. Registry identification cards as of 30 June 2024: qualifying patients 9,934; designated caregivers 164; agents 483. (FY2024 report, p. 4–5 table; note the report's own caveat that one individual may hold multiple cards, so card counts ≠ distinct individuals; minor patients <40 in each of the five years shown.) Cards revoked in FY2024: 4, all qualifying patients (FY2022: 16; FY2023: 7 — FY2024 report, p. 6). Compassion-center counts, expenses/revenues, dispensary sales totals, patients by county and by age, and conditions selected (anxiety disorder 6,318 and PTSD 1,653 of 15,787 conditions selected for 9,934 patients — patients may have multiple conditions) are in the same PDF; sales/patient detail belongs to Part 6, not Part 5, and is not repeated here beyond the counts above. Not available in the reports: licence/certificate numbers, certificate issue/expiry dates, street addresses of manufacturing facilities, ownership percentages, agent names, lab identity, or enforcement actions against centres (see §5.9).
  • Limitations: (1) annual snapshot only — no quarterly series in the reports opened (the commission asked for quarterly reports; no quarterly HHS medical-marijuana report was found — Unavailable); (2) series ends at FY2024 due to repeal; (3) city-level only for facilities; (4) trade names used for dispensaries in some years (“We-Mend (Strive Life)”) vs. current hub name (“Strive Life”) — reconcile trade-name changes before merging series; (5) older reports sit under an “Archived” hub link whose full URL list was not harvested here.

5.3 HHS programme hub live counter — “Cards Issued”

  • URL: https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm (opened 2026-10-30)
  • Holder type: registry identification cards (the hub does not break the counter into patients vs. caregivers vs. agents on the hub page text).
  • Format: web counter, not a list and not downloadable. No export button.
  • Latest data date / figure: “Cards Issued: 10,982 — As of October 8, 2026” (hub page text, opened 2026-10-30).
  • Update frequency as stated: none stated on the hub page.
  • Limitations / reconciliation warning: 10,982 (8 Oct 2026, all cards?) vs. FY2024's 9,934 patient cards + 164 caregiver cards + 483 agent cards = 10,581 cards at 30 June 2024. The hub figure is not labelled by card type in the fetched text, so it cannot be directly compared to the FY2024 patient-only figure (9,934) without knowing its composition. Do not present 10,982 as “patients” — present it as “cards issued (composition unstated on the hub page) as of 8 October 2026”. A secondary market report (CannBus Q3 2026, data as of 30 Sept 2026) cites “10,710 active medical marijuana cards” as of 8 Sept 2026 from the programme page — a different date and a different figure, consistent with a moving counter, not a conflict. Both secondary figures are superseded for citation purposes by the hub figure opened directly (10,982 at 8 Oct 2026). See also Part 6, §6.2, which cites the CannBus snapshot as 10,710 (8 Sept 2026) with the same “cards, not patients” caveat.

5.4 NDDA hemp licence sources — application system, NOT a public holder list

  • Programme page URL: https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp (opened 2026-10-30)
  • Holder types: hemp growers and hemp processors (NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1). Retailers/sellers of hemp products need no NDDA licence (programme page: “The Hemp Program does not require any licenses for selling seeds. Seed dealers will need to contact the North Dakota State Seed Department.”).
  • Format: HTML information page + forms + a login portal. No public searchable list of licensed growers/processors was found on the NDDA hemp page or anywhere else on ndda.nd.gov for this report.
  • How to apply (from the programme page, for source identification): submit Criminal History Record Check Request (SFN 60688) + 2 sets of fingerprints + Hemp License Application (SFN 58476) and/or Hemp Processor License Application (SFN 61613) + $40.00 non-refundable background-check payment + the licence fee (grower: $100 / 1 lot, $200 / 2 lots, $300 / 3 lots, $350 / 4+ lots; processor: $200), mailed to NDDA Plant Industries Division, 600 E Boulevard Ave, Dept 602, Bismarck, ND 58505-0020; or apply online via the Online Licensing & Exchange Portal (OLE) — the programme page links “Apply For a Hemp License Online” to ole.ndda.nd.gov (full portal URL as returned in the NDDA page's link list; the older secondary source gives https://ole.ndda.nd.gov/login?next=hempprojects/ — Verify the current OLE hemp path live, as portal paths change).
  • Statute (for Part 7 cross-reference): NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1. Key licence provisions as summarised in the 2019/2025 Justia republications (secondary republication of the statute — the Part 7 researcher must open the official ndlegis.gov chapter PDF before quoting): § 4.1-18.1-02 — any person desiring to grow or process hemp shall apply to the agriculture commissioner; licence must be obtained before purchasing/obtaining hemp material for planting/propagation; initial licensure requires state + nationwide criminal-history check (NDCC § 12-60-24); denial for a controlled-substance felony in the last 10 years; licence expires December 31; § 4.1-18.1-03 — fee not to exceed $350 per producer/processor. The NDDA page's lot-based grower fees ($100–$350) sit within that $350 ceiling. The official ndlegis.gov chapter PDF URL follows the pattern of the medical chapter but was not opened for this report — do not cite a guessed URL; the Part 7 researcher must open it from ndlegis.gov's code index.
  • Downloadable files: the SFN forms are downloads from the NDDA page (SFN 60688, SFN 58476, SFN 61613 — linked as form numbers on the page; exact file URLs were not harvested in the text fetch and are not guessed here). There is no downloadable list of current hemp licensees from NDDA in the sources opened. Research summaries from 2016–2020 (NDDA Industrial Hemp Pilot Program Research Summaries, linked from the NDDA hemp page for 2016, 2017, 2018, 2019, 2020) are historical programme summaries, not current licence lists, and their exact file URLs were not harvested — follow the NDDA page's links.
  • Update frequency / latest data date: none stated for a holder list, because no holder list is published. Licence year ends 31 December each year (statute), so any count is inherently annual.
  • Limitations: (1) no public holder list; (2) the programme page still carries stale pilot-programme language (“Industrial Hemp Research Pilot Program”, federal CSA warning citing 7 U.S.C. § 5940, the 2014 Farm Bill pilot authority) alongside current licensing instructions — the page has not been fully updated for the post-2018-Farm-Bill USDA-approved state plan, so its framing language must not be quoted as current law; (3) the $40 background-check figure on the NDDA page vs. $40.00 in several secondary sources is a conflict to flag: the NDDA page opened 2026-10-30 says “$40.00”; secondary sources (e.g. northdakotacannabis.org) say $40.00. The NDDA page is the more authoritative and more recent source opened, so $40.00 is adopted here and $40.00 is listed as the superseded/conflicting figure in the research notes for Run 2.
  • Counts (official first, then dated secondary — counts belong to Part 6 in full, but the Part 5 commission asked for totals where official figures exist, so the position is stated here): no current official NDDA count of licensed hemp growers/processors was found in the sources opened. The NDDA page gives no count. USDA NASS gives acreage, not licence counts (2024: 850 acres grown in open areas, 760 harvested — NASS ND hemp release, 17 Apr 2025: https://www.nass.usda.gov/Statistics_by_State/North_Dakota/Publications/Crop_Releases/Hemp_Release/2025/ND-hemp2504.pdf). Dated secondary counts (Vote Hemp 2020: 81 licensed farmers, ~5,000 licensed acres in 2020; a 2023 secondary summary: 8 licensed processors) are not adopted as current totals and must not be carried into Part 6 as current figures without a fresh official source. Total hemp licences, current year: Unavailable from official sources opened — Verify by direct NDDA enquiry / public-records request.

5.5 USDA hemp records for North Dakota

  • USDA-approved plan list — URL: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review (AMS page, “Updated February 17, 2026” per search result). Entry: “North Dakota | ND Approved Plan (pdf)*” — the asterisk means approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021. This confirms North Dakota runs its own USDA-approved state plan, not the USDA direct-licensing programme — so USDA does not issue North Dakota producer licences, and a USDA producer-licence list will not contain North Dakota state licensees.
  • North Dakota State Hemp Plan (PDF, downloadable): https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/NorthDakotaDepartmentofAgricultureHempPlan.pdf — file name: NorthDakotaDepartmentofAgricultureHempPlan.pdf. Key plan facts (from the plan text as returned by search): NDDA oversees hemp production/processing; NDDA began issuing producer licences in 2007 (DEA permits then unobtainable); pilot programme with active producers since 2015; production authorised by NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1; licence numbers in USDA-prescribed format, expiring annually 31 December; producers report crop acreage to the local Farm Service Agency office; NDDA reports each producer (full name, licence number, address, phone, email if available) to USDA monthly (first of each month, USDA-approved format). That monthly NDDA→USDA report is a federal record, not a public list — it is not published as a downloadable file in the sources opened.
  • USDA Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP) Public Search Tool — URL (as given in a state-agency page returned by search): https://hemp.ams.usda.gov/s/PublicSearchTool (Kansas Dept. of Agriculture hemp search page, https://portal.kda.ks.gov/Hemp/HempSearch/LicenseSearch, describes it as “a search tool that allows users access to basic information on licensed hemp producers across the country”). AMS's own HeMP page: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/hemp-emanagement-platform-hemp. AMS FAQ: “Information on licensees in your area can be obtained through the Hemp Public Search Tool” (https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/frequently-asked-questions-faq).
    • Holder type: licensed hemp producers nationally, as reported by their licensing authority (state/tribal/USDA). For North Dakota, entries (if any) would reflect NDDA-reported producers.
    • Format: interactive search tool. No bulk download stated in the sources opened. Whether the tool exposes North Dakota producers and which fields it shows for them was not verified by a live search in this report (the tool requires interactive use and was not run as a browser task). Verify by a live Public Search Tool query filtered to North Dakota before citing any count from it.
    • Limitation: the tool is a verification aid (“examiners can use this tool to verify a borrower's license is valid” — CSBS hemp JobAid, May 2026), not a statistical release; it is not a substitute for NDDA's own (unpublished) list, and its coverage lag vs. NDDA's monthly reporting is unstated.
  • USDA AMS hemp programme contacts page (for NDDA contact verification): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-producers/grower-contact-list — North Dakota entry points to https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp (current as of March 2025 per that page). Do not reproduce individual programme-staff phone/email shown there in the final combined report (personal contact details rule); the NDDA programme page itself is the citable source.

5.6 North Dakota Secretary of State — FirstStop business entity search

  • URL (business search): https://firststop.sos.nd.gov/search/business (URL as given verbatim in multiple search results, including a U.S. government SkillBridge verification document: https://publicdev.skillbridge.mil/static/docs/Business_Entity_Verification_Documents_2026.pdf).
  • Holder type: business entities, not cannabis licences. FirstStop records that a company/LLC/partnership/trade name exists, its type, SOS Control ID, status (Active/Inactive), standing (Good Standing / Not Good Standing) and initial filing date. It does not record whether the entity holds a medical-marijuana registration certificate or a hemp licence, and a FirstStop hit for e.g. “GR Vending ND, LLC” or “Pure Dakota, LLC” proves entity registration only — never cite a FirstStop result as proof of a cannabis licence.
  • Format: interactive search (HTML). Search by business name or SOS Control ID (10-digit, zero-padded, e.g. format shown in secondary guides); Advanced options: Contains / Starts with, Active-entities-only filter.
  • How to access: free, no account required for basic search (per SOS guides returned by search). Filing, name reservation, annual reports and paid document/certificate orders run through the same FirstStop portal with login.
  • Download/export button: no bulk download from the search itself in the sources opened. Secondary guides note the SOS sells business-database lists (active records per entity type; monthly all-records subscription) — pricing quoted in secondary guides ($40 per entity type for active records; $1,440/month for all business + trademark records) is unverified from an official SOS source for this report — Verify before quoting. A single entity's detail page / certificate of good standing can be ordered, but that is a per-entity filing, not a licence list.
  • Update frequency as stated: not stated in the sources opened; records reflect filings as processed (a filing made hours/days earlier may not yet appear, per secondary guides — treat as a currency caveat, not an official statement).
  • Latest data date: live search (no snapshot date).
  • Fields available (per secondary guides, to be confirmed live): business name, entity type, SOS Control ID, status, standing, initial filing date; detail page adds registered-agent and filing history. Not available: cannabis/hemp licence status, licence number, or programme standing.
  • Use in this research: FirstStop is the only way to verify the legal-entity existence and good standing that NDCC § 19-24.1-14(1)(c) requires of a compassion-center applicant, and to check the legal name behind a trade name (e.g. GR Vending ND, LLC d/b/a Grassroots Cannabis, per the FY2024 report). It is a corroborating source, never a licence source.
  • Limitations: (1) no cannabis-licence field; (2) entity status ≠ licence status — an entity can be Active/Good Standing while its compassion-center certificate is suspended, revoked or expired, and vice versa; (3) trade names and fictitious partnership names appear in the same result list as companies, so name searches need disambiguation; (4) the SOS itself notes filings list incorporators/organisers, “but these individuals may not necessarily be the owners” (per secondary guide quoting SOS guidance) — do not use FirstStop to infer ownership percentages for Part 4 caps.

5.7 Local (county / city / township) permit records

  • Central list? NO — Unavailable as a single source. No statewide list of local medical-marijuana or hemp/CBD/smoke-shop permits, zoning approvals, moratoriums or caps was found in any official source opened. Local approval exists as a condition of the state application (NDCC § 19-24.1-14(1)(d)(1): “Evidence of approval from local officials as to the proposed compassion center applicant's compliance with local zoning laws”), but the state does not publish the local approvals themselves.
  • How local records would be found (jurisdiction by jurisdiction — the Part 3 researcher must do this): city council / county commission meeting minutes and zoning files for each facility city (Bismarck, Fargo, Grand Forks, Minot, Williston, Dickinson, Jamestown, Devils Lake for medical facilities), plus city business-licence / tobacco-retailer lists for hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shops where a city licenses them. Formats vary (PDF minutes, HTML agendas, paper zoning files); no download standard exists.
  • What was checked for this report: the HHS dispensary-locations page (§5.1) and FY2024 report (§5.2) give facility cities but no local permit numbers; no city or county permit database was opened. No local permit count is asserted.
  • Limitation: because local records are decentralised and (for townships in particular) often paper-only, absence of a local record in an online search is not evidence that no local approval exists. Flag every local-permit claim in Part 3 as jurisdiction-sourced or Verify.

5.8 Tribal licence records (if public)

  • USDA plan list position: the AMS state/tribal plan list (https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review, updated 17 Feb 2026) shows North Dakota's state plan as approved; the search excerpt also shows a “Sisseton-Wahpeton O…” tribal-plan entry (truncated in the search result — Verify the full tribal-plan table live: whether Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate holds a USDA-approved tribal hemp plan, and whether any other North Dakota tribe does, must be confirmed from the AMS table itself, not from the truncated snippet).
  • Turtle Mountain Band of Chippewa Indians — tribal hemp code (public, web-readable): Title 51 of the Turtle Mountain Tribal Code establishes hemp production and processing licensing, including § 51.04.030 (Hemp Processing License application contents), § 51.05.010 (licence identifiers in USDA-prescribed format; annual renewal; licences not assignable or transferable), § 51.04.050 (fees set by the Commission), § 51.09.010 (processing licensee may process/manufacture/package/store hemp, buy from a producer licensed in any jurisdiction, and market/sell on or off the reservation; may not accept hemp over 0.3% THC). Source for the code text as indexed: National Indian Law Library (NILL), e.g. https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.04.030 and https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.05.010/ (accessed via search 2026-10-30; the tribe's own law site is law.tmchippewa.com — the NILL page itself warns to contact the tribe for the official version).
    • No Turtle Mountain hemp licence-holder list was found. The code is the licensing framework, not a register. Holder counts: Unavailable.
    • A tribal hemp business example returned by search (Pride of Dakota manufacturers directory: http://www.prideofdakota.nd.gov/manufacturers/ — “Chippewa Harvest LLC … owned by the Turtle Mountain Band of Chippewa Indians … industrial hemp business … hemp hearts, hemp protein powder, hemp oil”) is a marketing directory entry, not a licence record — do not cite it as a licence.
  • Other four tribes (Standing Rock Sioux Tribe, Spirit Lake Nation, MHA Nation / Three Affiliated Tribes, Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate): no public tribal cannabis or hemp licence list was found for any of them for this report. Whether each has a tribal hemp plan, tribal cannabis ordinance, or tribal regulator is a Part 10 question requiring official tribal-government sources; nothing is asserted here. In particular, no tribal medical-marijuana licence list exists in the state sources opened, and whether any tribal medical programme would count as “state-licensed” for the federal Schedule III / 280E baseline is Verify (see scope note).
  • Format summary for tribal sources: web-readable code text (no download button identified for a holder list); USDA plan list is a web table with plan PDFs where approved. No CSV/XLSX tribal holder list found.

5.9 Enforcement actions — no published list; only revocation counts in the annual reports

  • No standalone HHS enforcement-action list, press-release index, or disciplinary database for compassion centers was found in the sources opened. The HHS hub (https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm) has no enforcement link in its fetched text; the FY2024 report's table of contents has no enforcement section.
  • What does exist (counts only, no names): annual-report revocation counts. FY2024: 4 registry identification cards revoked, all qualifying patients (FY2024 report, p. 6). FY2022: 16; FY2023: 7 (same report). No compassion-center certificate revocation or suspension is reported in the FY2024 report, and no centre-level enforcement action was identified in any source opened. That is absence of a published action, not proof that none occurred — centre-level actions, if any, would appear (if at all) in department orders or court records, not in the statistical report, and NDCC § 19-24.1-37 makes application/renewal information confidential except for statistical disclosure “in a manner such that an individual or compassion center is not identified” (§ 19-24.1-37(2)(g)).
  • Statutory enforcement hooks (for the Part 11 researcher): suspension/revocation under NDCC § 19-24.1-19 (see Part 4); failure-to-notify fee up to $150 (§ 19-24.1-20(1)); plan-of-correction process under NDAC §§ 33-44-01-31/32. None of these creates a public list.
  • Hemp enforcement: NDDA hemp violations (NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1, e.g. negligent violations, hot hemp) are likewise not published as a holder-level list in the sources opened. Verify by NDDA enquiry if Part 11 needs case-level enforcement.
  • Format / download: the only downloadable enforcement-adjacent files are the annual-report PDFs in §5.2. No separate enforcement CSV/PDF found.

5.10 Seed-to-sale / registration system (internal, not a public data source)

  • System: BioTrackTHC — named on the HHS out-of-state page (“Applications will be completed and submitted online via the BioTrackTHC registration system found on the program website under the Medical Marijuana Registration Portal”, https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm/out-of-state) and in 2018 reporting as the Florida-based vendor selected to implement the monitoring system (Technology Networks, 6 Mar 2018, republishing ND Department of Health material: https://www.technologynetworks.com/applied-sciences/news/north-dakota-accepting-proposals-from-cannabis-testing-labs-298234).
  • Holder types in the system: patients, caregivers, agents, compassion centers, laboratories (inventory control, dispensing limits, testing — NDAC §§ 33-44-01-19/20 inventory control; § 33-44-01-33 data reporting to the prescription-drug monitoring programme).
  • Format / access: login portal for registered users (https://mmregistration.health.nd.gov/, linked from https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm). No public search, no public dashboard, no public download. The “limit meter” and 30-day purchase history are patient-account features, not public data.
  • Limitation: BioTrackTHC data reaches the public only as aggregated in the (now discontinued) annual reports (§5.2) and the hub counter (§5.3). Do not describe BioTrackTHC as a public licence database.

Part 6 — Market Data

Method note: Official sources first (ND HHS Division of Medical Marijuana, ND Department of Agriculture, USDA NASS/AMS). Secondary sources are used ONLY for non-law facts (market figures, events, banking platforms) and are labelled SECONDARY with publication date. No personal phone numbers or emails are reproduced. North Dakota places are distinguished from same-name places elsewhere (e.g. Williston, ND, not Williston, VT/FL; Dickinson, ND, not Dickinson, TX; Jamestown, ND, not Jamestown, NY). Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026; adult-use remains Schedule I; federal hemp dates 12 November / 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law exists.

Headline for Part 6: North Dakota's legal cannabis market is medical-only, small, and flat: $22.424 million in state FY2024 across just 8 dispensaries, with patient growth slowing sharply. Hemp is likewise small in planted terms in recent NASS data (850 acres grown in the open in 2024) — far below the 3,000+ licensed acres of the 2017 pilot era — and ND-specific hemp value / by-purpose breakdowns are not published in the NASS state releases opened.

6.1 Medical dispensary sales by year (OFFICIAL — HHS annual report + legislative testimony)

Source: ND HHS, Medical Marijuana Program Annual Report, Fiscal Year 2024 (data as of 30 June 2024; sales from the programme's state traceability and enforcement monitoring system, rounded to nearest thousand; FY2019 sales not provided because only two dispensaries were open that year) — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Annual-Report-FY-2024-archive.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. Corroborated / extended by: ND HHS presentation to House Appropriations, Human Resources Division, 13 January 2025 (HB 1012 testimony) — http://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/69-2025/testimony/HAPPHUM-1012-20250113-28445-F-WILKE_DIRK_D.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30, which repeats FY2020–FY2024 and adds a FY2025 figure labelled “(6 months)”.

FY2024$22,424,000+3.8%
FY2025, first 6 months only (as labelled in Jan 2025 testimony; exact months not stated in the slide — VERIFY whether July–December 2024)$11,197,000n/a — half-year only; do NOT annualise as an official figure. For scale only: doubling it would give ~$22.4m, i.e. essentially flat vs FY2024, but that is arithmetic, not an official total.
  • No official full-year FY2025 or FY2026 sales total was found in any source opened. The HHS summary of 2025 legislative changes states SB 2308 “will eliminate the Medical Marijuana Advisory Board as well as the annual report requirements for the program,” effective 1 August 2025 — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Summary-of-Bills-Passed-2025-Legislative-Session-archive.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. That is the likely reason no FY2025 annual report was located; flag as a follow-up to confirm with HHS whether any FY2025/FY2026 sales release exists outside the annual-report series.
  • Context (SECONDARY, dated): North Dakota Monitor, 21 July 2025, “North Dakota medical marijuana program adding edibles, making eligibility changes” — https://northdakotamonitor.com/2025/07/21/north-dakota-medical-marijuana-program-adding-edibles-making-eligibility-changes/ — Accessed 2026-10-30 — repeats the same trajectory (“by 2020, $6.4 million… tripled over the next two years, rising to $20 million in 2022, but have recently stabilized, totaling $22.4 million last year”).
  • Forecast (SECONDARY, old, do not use as current): MJBizDaily's 2024 Factbook forecast cited in secondary coverage put ND's 2024 medical market at $25 million — above the official $22.424m actually reported. MJBizDaily's original 2016-era forecast was $10–20m eventual annual sales. Both are superseded by official actuals above.

6.2 Patient, caregiver and agent counts (OFFICIAL)

Source: FY2024 annual report (above), registry data as of 30 June each year. The report warns: card numbers are not headcounts of individuals — one person may hold more than one caregiver card and one agent may serve multiple facilities.

As of 30 JuneQualifying-patient cardsDesignated-caregiver cardsAgent cards
20203,233109410
