20239,596196457
20249,934164483
  • FY2024 patient growth was only +338 cards (+3.5%) vs +1,638 in FY2023; the report itself notes growth “was significantly less compared to prior fiscal years.”
  • Minor patients (under 19): fewer than 40 in each of FY2020–FY2024.
  • FY2024 patient mix: top conditions selected (a provider may select more than one, so totals exceed patient count; 15,787 conditions selected for 9,934 patients): anxiety disorder 6,318; PTSD 1,653; severe debilitating pain 1,576; spinal stenosis or chronic back pain 1,499; fibromyalgia 1,406; neuropathy and migraine figures are garbled in the PDF text extraction and are NOT asserted here (VERIFY from the PDF chart).
  • County of residence, 30 June 2024 (official): Cass 24%, Burleigh 14%, Ward 12%, Grand Forks 10%, Morton 5%, all other counties combined 35%. The top three counties are ~50% of patients.
  • Age, 30 June 2024 (official): over 50% of patients are aged 30–49.
  • Revocations FY2024: 4 registry cards, all qualifying patients (vs 16 in FY2022, 7 in FY2023).
  • Most recent count (OFFICIAL programme page, different metric — do not conflate): ND HHS Medical Marijuana page — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/mm — Accessed 2026-10-30 — displays “Cards Issued: 10,982 — As of October 8, 2026.” This is a cards-issued counter on the programme page, not a 30-June patient-card table, and the page does not break it into patients / caregivers / agents in the text opened. Do NOT present 10,982 as “10,982 patients.”
  • Conflicting / intermediate secondary figure: CannBus Q3 2026 market report (SECONDARY, data as of 30 September 2026) — https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/nd_cannmir.html — Accessed 2026-10-30 — cites “Registry Cards Issued (Sep 8, 2026): 10,710” from the same HHS programme page. The 10,710 (8 Sept) → 10,982 (8 Oct) movement is consistent with ~272 net cards in a month, but because the metric is “cards issued” on a live page, the two snapshots should be cited with their dates, not averaged.
  • Derived ratios (calculated, not official): FY2024 sales per patient card = $22,424,000 ÷ 9,934 ≈ $2,257/year; sales per dispensary = $22,424,000 ÷ 8 ≈ $2.80 million/year. These are arithmetic only.

6.3 Product mix (OFFICIAL — only split published)

The FY2024 annual report publishes only one product split: share of total sales that were dried leaves and flowers vs everything else combined. No official breakdown by concentrate / tincture / topical / capsule / patch, and no official unit or gram volumes, were found in the report opened.

Fiscal yearDried leaves & flowers share of salesAll other products combined (implied)
FY202067%33%
FY202172%28%
FY202269%31%
FY202366%34%
FY202462%38%
FY2025 (6 months, Jan 2025 testimony)61%39%
  • Trend: flower share has fallen every year since FY2021 (72% → 61%), i.e. non-flower products are slowly gaining share, but flower remains a clear majority.
  • Edibles caveat: cannabinoid edibles were NOT in the programme during FY2024 (the FY2024 glossary expressly excludes a cannabinoid edible product from “usable marijuana”). Low-dose edible lozenges were authorised by HB 1203 effective 1 August 2025 (see Part 1 / Part 9 of the full research); any post-August-2025 product-mix shift is UNAVAILABLE — no official post-edibles sales split was found. Do not assume edibles changed the mix.
  • VERIFY: whether HHS's traceability system holds a finer product-category split that is disclosed on request or in interim-committee presentations; it is not in the FY2024 annual report.

6.4 Dispensary and manufacturing-facility counts (OFFICIAL)

  • FY2024 annual report: 2 registered manufacturing facilities (Grassroots Cannabis / GR Vending ND, LLC, Fargo; Pure Dakota, LLC, Bismarck) and 8 registered dispensaries (Curaleaf — Devils Lake, Dickinson, Jamestown, Minot; Pure Dakota Health — Bismarck, Fargo, Williston; We-Mend / Strive Life — Grand Forks). First dispensary opened Fargo, March 2019; eighth and final opened Dickinson, December 2019. Statutory caps are 2 and 8 unless HHS finds more are needed for patient access.
  • No official source opened reports any change to the 2 / 8 count through 2026; the HHS programme page (accessed 2026-10-30) still links a dispensary-locations list. VERIFY the live dispensary list at the time of Run 2 compilation, as operator branding (Curaleaf / Pure Dakota / Strive Life) may have changed without the count changing.

6.5 Official price data

  • No official North Dakota price series exists in any source opened. HHS annual reports publish total sales dollars and a flower share, but no price per gram, per ounce, or per product, and no price index. The State Tax Commissioner publishes no cannabis price data found. Do not invent a price.
  • What is known officially: the state does not regulate dispensary prices (secondary reporting of the 2017 law notes “whatever the market says. The state won't regulate pricing” — KCJB/iHeart, 18 April 2017, SECONDARY).
  • Secondary price claims (labelled, unverified against any official source — use only with this caveat):
    • NorthDakotaCannabis.org, “How Much Does Medical Marijuana Cost in North Dakota?” (page updated 21 September 2026 per search listing) — https://NorthDakotaCannabis.org/mmj-cost — accessed via search 2026-10-30 — claims average $384/oz high-quality, $330/oz medium-quality in ND. Methodology is not stated on the page excerpt; treat as an unverified aggregator figure, NOT an official price.
    • World Population Review, “Marijuana Cost by State 2026” (published 28 October 2026) — https://worldpopulationreview.com/state-rankings/marijuana-cost-by-state — accessed via search 2026-10-30 — likewise ranks ND highest at $384/oz average. Same caveat: secondary aggregator, no stated ND sampling method.
    • A commercial telehealth/marketing page (NDMedCard.com, accessed via search 2026-10-30) — https://www.ndmedcard.com/why-get-a-card.html — quotes ND dispensary “standard pricing” of $52 per eighth (3.5 g) and $90 per quarter (7 g), tax included, with 30–40% promotional discounts. This is marketing material, not a price survey; do not use as a market price.
  • Conclusion for Part 6: official price data = NONE FOUND. Any price quoted in the final HTML must carry the secondary/unverified label above.

6.6 Hemp acreage — NASS official series (planted/grown vs harvested; NOT licensed acres)

Critical distinction: USDA NASS “grown in open areas” = planted/grown acres estimated from its survey; it is NOT NDDA licensed acres. Licensed acres (below, §6.7) were much larger in the pilot era and are a different measure. Never mix the two series in one table.

Source for every row: USDA NASS, North Dakota Field Office news releases (Fargo, ND): 2022 crop year, released 19 April 2023 — https://data.nass.usda.gov/Statistics_by_State/North_Dakota/Publications/Crop_Releases/Hemp_Release/2023/ND-hemp2304.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. 2023 crop year, released 17 April 2024 — https://data.nass.usda.gov/Statistics_by_State/North_Dakota/Publications/Crop_Releases/Hemp_Release/2024/ND-hemp2404.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30. 2024 crop year, released 17 April 2025 — https://www.nass.usda.gov/Statistics_by_State/North_Dakota/Publications/Crop_Releases/Hemp_Release/2025/ND-hemp2504.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30 (opened directly; text confirmed).

  • By purpose (fibre / grain / CBD-floral / seed) for ND: NOT PUBLISHED in the state releases opened. The state releases give only the all-utilisations total. NASS suppresses state-level by-purpose detail where disclosure rules apply (small respondent counts). Do NOT allocate ND's 850 acres across purposes, and do NOT substitute national shares for ND shares.

A secondary industry ranking (ASIGA, undated page accessed via search 2026-10-30 — https://asiga.org/) lists ND 8th among US hemp-production states with the descriptor “Grain + Fiber” — SECONDARY, no ND acreage/value attached, use only as a directional descriptor, not a figure.
  • 2022 Census of Agriculture (OFFICIAL, but extraction garbled — VERIFY, do not quote precisely): NASS's 2022 Census hemp highlights for ND exist — https://www.nass.usda.gov/Publications/AgCensus/2022/Online_Resources/Hemp/hpsdv1.pdf — accessed via search 2026-10-30 — but the text extraction is too garbled (farm counts, acres and sales values interleaved) to assert any census figure safely here. The census is a different instrument from the annual Hemp Production and Disposition Inquiry and must not be averaged with it.

NDDA does not publish a current annual licence-count table on its hemp page opened (https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp — Accessed 2026-10-30; page gives forms/fees and links to 2016–2020 pilot-program research summaries, but no 2021–2025 licence/acreage table). The historical licence figures below are therefore SECONDARY and dated; the NDDA pilot summaries themselves should be opened for Run 2 / Part 7 verification.

Possible causes (unverified): licensed vs approved vs planted acres, or different cut-off dates. The NDDA 2020 pilot research summary is the tie-breaker source to open.
  • Licensed vs planted gap (interpretation, not a figure): licensed acres in 2017 (~3,100) vs NASS grown acres in the 2020s (740–850) show the pilot-era licensed footprint collapsing to a much smaller actually-planted footprint. That comparison is valid directionally (licensed ≠ planted) but the years differ; do not present it as a single-year utilisation rate.
  • NDDA pilot summaries (OFFICIAL, to open for Part 7): NDDA hemp page links 2016, 2017, 2018, 2019 and 2020 Industrial Hemp Pilot Program Research Summaries. They were not text-extracted in this Part 6 pass; they are the authoritative source for pilot-era acreage and grower counts.

6.8 Hemp value and product-market figures

  • ND-specific hemp production value: NOT PUBLISHED in sources opened. NASS state releases for ND give acres only — no ND yield, production, price or value. The National Hemp Report's state value table suppresses small states. Do NOT estimate an ND hemp value by applying national $/acre to ND acres.
  • National context (OFFICIAL, for scale only — not ND figures): USDA NASS National Hemp Report, released 17 April 2025 (2024 crop year): US total hemp value (open + under protection) $445 million, up ~40% vs 2023; open-field value $417 million on 45,294 planted / 32,694 harvested acres. By use (open field, 2024): floral $386m on 11,827 harvested acres; fibre $11.2m on 18,855 acres; grain $2.62m on 4,863 acres; seed balance. Source via NASS briefing/report — https://www.nass.usda.gov/Newsroom/Executive_Briefings/2025/04-17-2025.pdf — and secondary reporting of the same report (Marijuana Moment, 17 April 2025; Hemp Gazette, April 2025), accessed 2026-10-30. The 2025 national report (released 16 April 2026) puts US 2025 value at $739 million — http://www.nass.usda.gov/Publications/Todays_Reports/reports/hempan26.pdf — accessed via search 2026-10-30. Neither report's ND state value is asserted here.
  • ND hemp product market (SECONDARY, dated, qualitative only): the 2017-era ND profile (MJBizDaily state highlight, via Medible Review republication, undated page accessed 2026-10-30 — https://mediblereview.com/hemp-state-highlight-north-dakota-growing-lots-of-hemp-but-market-potential-is-limited/) reported seed for food/oil at ~$0.50/lb, no reliable fibre-stalk price (no fibre processor then), and no flower price (CBD extraction then barred). These prices are pre-2019-law and must NOT be presented as current ND prices. No current ND hemp commodity price series was found.
  • ND hemp businesses named in secondary sources (market colour, not a census): Healthy Oilseeds, LLC (Carrington, ND — Foster County) — seed/oil/protein processing, cold-press expelling, milling, sifting, bottling; described in secondary sources as ND's long-standing seed processor and exporter. No revenue, volume or market-share figure for any ND hemp firm was found in an official source; none is asserted.

6.11 Cross-checks and reconciliation for the full Run 1 document

  • Medical sales FY2020–FY2024 and patient cards FY2020–FY2024 in this Part match the FY2024 annual report exactly; the Jan 2025 testimony repeats the same FY2020–FY2024 sales and flower shares and adds only the FY2025 6-month row.
  • Dispensary count (8) and manufacturing count (2) are consistent across the FY2024 report, the Jan 2025 testimony context, and secondary Q3 2026 coverage.
  • The HHS programme-page counter (10,982 cards, 8 Oct 2026) and the CannBus-cited snapshot (10,710 cards, 8 Sept 2026) differ because of date, not necessarily error — cite both with dates and the “cards, not patients” caveat.
  • Hemp: NASS grown acres (850 in 2024) must never be presented alongside NDDA licensed acres (3,100 in 2017; ~3,400 or ~5,000 in 2020, disputed) as if they were one series. The correct narrative is: large licensed footprint in the pilot era → much smaller actually-grown footprint in NASS's 2022–2024 survey, with the 2020 licensed-acre figure itself disputed.
  • Tax-revenue cross-check (for Part 2, not Part 6): at 5% state sales tax, FY2024 sales of $22.424m would imply ~$1.12m state sales tax before local taxes and before any timing/exemption adjustments — this is arithmetic, NOT a Tax Commissioner figure, and no official cannabis sales-tax receipt total was found in this pass. Do not present it as official revenue.

Correction 1 — Plant surcharge: $5,000 payable / $7,500 ceiling adopted; Run 1 $5,000 figure confirmed as payable (not downgraded) Verify. NDCC §19-24.1-24, as amended by SB 2210 (2019, enrolled text): “For every five hundred plants in excess of one thousand plants a manufacturing facility possesses, the manufacturing facility shall pay the department an additional certification fee of not to exceed seven thousand five hundred (NDAC: five thousand payable) dollars. This fee is due at the time of increase and again at renewal of the compassion center registration certificate under section 19-24.1-16.” Source: https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/66-2019/documents/19-0890-04000.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. The Run 1 compiler’s $5,000 figure from NDAC §33-44-01-07.4 is downgraded to Verify: it may reflect a different NDAC fee layer (application/certification schedule) or a misreading, and must be re-checked against a directly opened current NDAC §33-44-01-07.4 before either figure is quoted as the current NDAC amount. Do not carry the compiler’s “$5,000 adopted, the superseded 2019 figure superseded” line into the combined file.

Correction 2 — Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate (SWO) medical programme exists. This corrects the incompleteness of Run 1 Part 5’s “other tribes not found” / “other tribes’ licence records not found” for SWO. SWO adopted a Medical Cannabis Code, ch. 81, on 2 Nov 2021 (Motion No. 11), amended to Dec 2024, with a Medical Cannabis Commission and tribal retail. SWO is still NOT an ND state-licensed programme, and First Light is still NOT an ND dispensary (see Part 10). Whether tribal medical counts as “state-licensed” for federal Schedule III / 280E remains Verify.

Correction 3 — MHA Nation medical decriminalization + October 2026 survey added. MHA Nation (Three Affiliated Tribes, Fort Berthold) has decriminalized medical marijuana — not legalized it — so no dispensary may operate on the reservation on that basis; the decriminalization resolution number and date were not located and remain Verify. MHA’s official website reports an Advisory Survey to tribal members 18+ (return by 27 October 2026) asking “Do you support legalization of medical cannabis for MHA Nation?” — a survey, not a law, licence, or programme; its results were not located and remain Verify. Correction 4 — SB 2294 nonresident pathway partly closed. Run 1’s enrolled-section gap is partly closed from HHS Nonresident Adult Patient Application Instructions (Mar 2026) and the engrossed bill: eligibility is a resident of one of the 49 other states, DC, or a US territory holding a home-state medical marijuana card; applicants must be 19+; the home-state ID and home-state medical card must remain valid for the full 60 days of the ND nonresident card or the application will not be approved; fee $40, online only; processing 2–4 weeks after a complete application. The enrolled Century Code section number remains Verify (the fiscal note cites §19-24.1-03.1 among amended sections, but that is not a confirmed codification of the new nonresident section). Critically: this does NOT recognise an out-of-state card at the counter by itself, and does NOT authorise bringing out-of-state product into North Dakota. See Parts 10 and 12.

Multiple secondary guides state $40.00. Adopt $40.00 as the NDDA figure; $40.00 is treated as a stale secondary figure, not a current NDDA fee, and should not be quoted as the NDDA background-check payment.

Correction 9 — Patient delivery stays Verify. NDAC §§33-44-01-29/30 create a full transport/manifest regime for compassion centres and laboratories, but no NDAC text located in Run 2 expressly authorises or prohibits dispensary delivery to patients (as opposed to centre-to-centre/lab transport). A secondary guide states delivery is legal under NDAC 33-44-01; other secondary sources say delivery is illegal. The conflict is NOT RESOLVED. Describe §§29/30 as the transport regime, not as a patient-delivery authorisation.

Correction 10 — 0.1% ND hemp limit rejection reaffirmed. A secondary CBD guide again claims North Dakota applies a 0.1% delta-9 limit. Run 1 rejected this as unsupported, and Run 2 found no statute, NDAC provision, or NDDA page stating 0.1%. Do not adopt 0.1%. The ND hemp standard is total THC (THCa × 0.877 + delta-9) capped at the federal 0.3% ceiling in practice.

Federal baseline used as given (not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity and remains fully applicable to adult-use; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law. Hemp is not a controlled substance and was never subject to §280E, so the Schedule III change does not affect hemp. The 3 Nov 2026 election had not occurred on 30 Oct 2026, so no 2026 cannabis result is reported.

Part 7 — HEMP LICENCES

7.1 Answer in one paragraph

North Dakota has exactly two hemp licence types under NDCC Chapter 4.1-18.1, both issued by the Agriculture Commissioner through the North Dakota Department of Agriculture (NDDA): a hemp grower licence and a hemp processor licence. There is no hemp handler, transporter, distributor, retailer, or finished-product licence in Chapter 4.1-18.1, and no hemp retail licence is required to sell lawful hemp/CBD products. North Dakota runs its own USDA-approved state plan — it does not use the USDA direct-licensing programme for producers on state land. That confirms Run 1, Part 5. Tribal hemp on reservation land is separate (see §7.8): the Standing Rock Sioux Tribe and the Turtle Mountain Band of Chippewa Indians have their own USDA-approved tribal plans.

7.2 Governing law

  • Statute: NDCC Chapter 4.1-18.1 — Hemp, as amended by 2019 HB 1349 / HB 1113 (2018 Farm Bill alignment; hemp/CBD removed from controlled-substances schedules), 2021 HB 1045 (THC redefined to include delta-7, delta-8, delta-10 isomers; licensee isomerisation/conversion banned), and 2023 SB 2096 (rewrote definitions, created §4.1-18.1-01.1 administrative-rules authority and nine new sections, and expressly excluded delta-8 / chemically derived cannabinoids / hemp-extract inhalants / commissioner-disapproved psychotropic products from "hemp commodity or product").
  • Source: Run 1, Part 1 §1.2.4; NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1 (2025 text, search extract) — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-4-1/chapter-4-1-18-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract — direct re-open Verify].
  • Commissioner THC authority: NDCC §4.1-18.1-04.1: "The commissioner shall determine the total tetrahydrocannabinol concentration under this chapter up to an amount not to exceed the federal Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 [Pub. L. 115-334] and federal domestic hemp production program regulations under title 7, Code of Federal Regulations, part 990." In practice NDDA applies 0.3% total THC (Run 1, Part 1).
  • Source: NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1 (2025 text, search extract) — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-4-1/chapter-4-1-18-1/ — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract — direct re-open Verify].
  • Administrative rules: NDCC §4.1-18.1-01.1 authorises the commissioner to adopt rules governing sale, distribution, testing, labelling, and regulation of hemp and hemp commodities/products and prohibited substances/products. Run 1 cited NDAC Article 7-20 (Hemp Products) from secondary sources for product labelling/testing; the full NDAC 7-20 text was not directly opened in Run 1 or Run 2 — Verify before quoting any 7-20 section. Do not confuse NDAC 7-20 with NDAC 74-03-16 (seed certification, §7.7 below), which is a different article.
  • Federal framework: 7 CFR Part 990, Domestic Hemp Production Program (final rule effective 22 March 2021). State-plan requirements are at 7 CFR 990.3(a), including land legal description / geospatial location, sampling by a sampling agent within 30 days before anticipated harvest, and FSA acreage reporting.
  • Source: 7 CFR Part 990 (eCFR) — https://www.ecfr.gov/api/renderer/v1/content/enhanced/2024-04-23/title-7?subtitle=B&chapter=IX&part=990 — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract].

7.3 Own USDA-approved state plan — confirmed, with the history that matters

  • Current status: OWN USDA-APPROVED STATE PLAN, not USDA direct licensing. The USDA AMS "List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans" (updated 17 February 2026) lists: "| North Dakota | ND Approved Plan (pdf) |" where "" means "Plan approved under the final rule which took effect March 22, 2021." Once approved, a plan remains in effect unless withdrawn by the State or revoked by USDA.
  • Source: USDA AMS, List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans — https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract — direct re-open Verify; this confirms the Run 1, Part 5 statement of the same list date].
  • Plan document: North Dakota State Hemp Plan (PDF), NDDA.
  • Direct plan PDF link (from AMS search extract): https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/NorthDakotaDepartmentofAgricultureHempPlan.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract — direct re-open Verify].
  • Approval-date history — do not overstate. North Dakota's first plan submission was not approved under the interim final rule: in January 2020 USDA listed North Dakota as "Pending Resubmission," and February 2020 reporting records that North Dakota's plan "was rejected and sent back" / "was not approved, because of inconsistencies with the interim final rule," so North Dakota continued its 2014 Farm Bill pilot programme for the 2020 season. The plan now shown as "ND Approved Plan (pdf)*" is therefore a later, final-rule approval. The exact later approval date was not located in the sources opened for this run — Verify from the AMS plan PDF or AMS correspondence; do not cite a 2020 approval date for the state plan. (The March 2020 approval dates sometimes seen in secondary material belong to tribal plans, e.g. Standing Rock, §7.8 — not the state plan.)
  • Sources: USDA plan-status table as of 29 Jan 2020 (search extract); Red River Farm Network, 4 Feb 2020 — https://www.rrfn.com/2020/02/04/states-working-with-usda-on-hemp-regs/ — Accessed 2026-10-30; Hemp Industry Daily, 3 Feb 2020 — https://hempindustrydaily.com/montana-north-dakota-keeping-old-rules-for-2020-hemp-production-season/ — Accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Consequence: Because North Dakota has an approved state plan, a producer growing hemp in North Dakota (outside tribal jurisdiction) must be licensed by NDDA, not by USDA. USDA direct licences apply only where the state/tribe has no approved plan. A USDA licence is therefore not an alternative route for a North Dakota grower on state land — Verify only for a grower whose land is in a jurisdiction with no approved plan, which is not the North Dakota state-land case.
  • Source: USDA AMS, Information for Hemp Growers — https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-for-hemp-growers — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract].

7.4 The two licence types

FieldHemp Grower LicenceHemp Processor Licence
Statutory basisNDCC §4.1-18.1-02: "Any person desiring to grow or process hemp shall apply to the agriculture commissioner for a license on a form prescribed by the commissioner. A license must be obtained before a person purchases or obtains hemp material for planting or propagation."Same section, same sentence — grow and process are the two verbs in one licensure section, issued as two separate NDDA licence types.
What it allowsGrow/produce hemp (field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure), by lot (see §7.6). A grower licence does not by itself authorise processing into a commodity/product; a person who both grows and processes needs both licences (NDDA issues them independently — Run 1, Part 5).Process hemp into an allowed hemp commodity/product: fibre via retting/other processing for textiles, rope, paper, hempcrete or other building/fibre materials; cured flower not treated with extracted cannabinoids; hemp seed processed so it is incapable of germination and suitable for human consumption; hemp seed pressed/processed into oil; CBD and CBG products (broad spectrum, full spectrum, isolate) with total THC not exceeding the commissioner's amount; and any hemp commodity/product approved in writing by the commissioner. It does not authorise making inhalants, delta-8 / chemically derived cannabinoids, or commissioner-disapproved psychotropic products (§4.1-18.1-01(5), §4.1-18.1-04.3).
NDDA application formHemp License Application, SFN 58476 (form dated 8-2021 in the extract).Hemp Processor License Application, SFN 61613.
Licence fee (NDDA page, adopted)By number of lots: 1 lot $100.00; 2 lots $200.00; 3 lots $300.00; 4+ lots $350.00. A "lot" is "a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure containing the same variety or strain of hemp throughout."Flat $200.
Statutory fee capNDCC §4.1-18.1-03: "The commissioner shall assess each producer and processor a fee not to exceed three hundred fifty dollars." Fees are deposited in the commissioner's operating fund, appropriated on a continuing basis to enforce the chapter. The NDDA schedule sits within that cap.Same $350 cap; $200 actual fee.
Background-check payment$40.00 adopted (NDDA page). See §7.5 for the $40.00 vs $40.00 resolution.Same $40.00 payment, same forms (SFN 60688 + 2 sets of fingerprints).
Testing/inspection feeUp to $125 to inspect, sample, and test hemp — NDCC §4.1-18.1-04.2(1). "The provisions of chapter 54-44.4 do not apply to hemp testing under this section." Whether the $125 is charged per lot, per sample, or per inspection in current practice — Verify on the NDDA page / current fee schedule.Same testing-fee section applies to hemp under the chapter; processor-specific sampling practice — Verify.
Term / renewalExpires 31 December of the licence year (NDCC §4.1-18.1-02(5): "A license issued under this chapter expires December thirty-first."). Annual renewal is therefore required; secondary sources state licences "expire every December 31st and require annual renewal." The step-by-step OLE renewal procedure and any renewal deadline/grace period are not stated in the statute extract — Verify on NDDA/OLE before advising an applicant.Same 31 December expiry (same subsection). Same renewal-procedure Verify.
CapsNo cap found in Chapter 4.1-18.1 on the number of grower or processor licences, on acreage, or on lots. Contrast the medical programme's hard caps (2 manufacturing facilities / 8 dispensaries, Run 1 Part 4). A secondary source also states "There is currently no limit on the number of hemp licenses issued in North Dakota" — treat the no-cap point as a negative search of the chapter, Verify against the full current chapter text.Same — no cap found.
TransferabilityNot stated in the statute extract — Verify. Do not assume a hemp licence is transferable, assignable, or saleable with land/business. NDDA/OLE transfer or change-of-ownership procedure, if any, was not located.Same — Verify.
Info linkNDDA Hemp page — https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract — direct re-open Verify]. Online application via NDDA Online Licensing & Exchange (OLE) portal, linked from that page.Same NDDA Hemp page and OLE portal.

7.5 Application packet and requirements (both licence types)

The NDDA hemp page lists the following documents to begin the process (search extract of the official page, corroborated by Run 1, Part 5):

  1. Criminal History Record Check Request, SFN 60688;
  2. 2 sets of fingerprints;
  3. Hemp License Application (SFN 58476) for growers, or Hemp Processor License Application (SFN 61613) for processors;
  4. $40.00 non-refundable check or money order made out to the North Dakota Department of Agriculture as payment for the background check;
  5. Appropriate hemp licence fee (table in §7.4).

Statutory application contents (NDCC §4.1-18.1-02):

  • Name and address of the applicant, and the legal description of the land area to be used to produce or process hemp (§4.1-18.1-02(1)).
  • The applicant is responsible for anyone working under the applicant's licence for all sections of the chapter.
  • The commissioner shall require each applicant for initial licensure to submit to a statewide and nationwide criminal history record check. The nationwide check is conducted in the manner provided in NDCC §12-60-24. All costs of the background check are the applicant's responsibility. Criminal-history records provided to the commissioner are confidential.
  • The commissioner shall deny licensure to any person convicted of a felony relating to a controlled substance under state or federal law in the last ten years (the 10-year felony bar; this matches 7 CFR 990).
  • "If the applicant has completed the application process to the satisfaction of the commissioner, the commissioner shall issue the license."
  • An application "may be submitted to the commissioner anytime before the purchase of hemp seed or viable propagation material" — i.e. there is no fixed annual application window in the statute; the practical constraint is that the licence must be issued before obtaining seeds, clones, or other propagative material.

Denial / revocation grounds (NDCC §4.1-18.1-04, search extract): the commissioner may deny or revoke a licence of a person who (a) repeatedly violates the chapter, (b) provides false or misleading information in connection with any required application, or (c) has been convicted of a felony as described in §4.1-18.1-02 since the most recent criminal-history background check. Full §4.1-18.1-04 text — Verify on direct re-open.

Background-check fee conflict — RESOLVED as instructed. Run 1, Part 5 flagged "$40.00 on NDDA page (vs $40.00 in secondary sources — NDDA adopted)." Run 2 confirms the same split: the NDDA hemp page extract states $40.00; multiple secondary guides (NorthDakotaCannabis.org, PaymentCloud, Fortuna Hemp) state $40.00. Adopt $40.00 as the NDDA figure; $40.00 is treated as a stale secondary figure, not a current NDDA fee. The $40.00 figure should not be quoted as the NDDA background-check payment. (If NDDA has since changed the payment, that change was not located — re-check the NDDA page live before advising an applicant.)

Other fees that are NOT hemp licence fees — do not conflate: the medical programme's agent-card, application, and certification fees (Run 1, Part 4) are a different programme; the USDA direct-licence fee (where USDA licences) is not an NDDA fee; and the historic pilot-era $5/acre / $150-minimum fee in the repealed Chapter 4-41 text is not current law — do not quote it.

7.6 Lot, FSA, and reporting — the compliance spine (cross-reference Part 11)

  • Lot definition (NDDA + plan): a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure containing the same variety or strain of hemp throughout. Licence fees are calculated on total lots across all hemp fields (grower form, SFN 58476).
  • FSA acreage reporting (plan + 7 CFR 990): "Licensed producers are required to submit their hemp crop acreage to the local Farm Service Agency (FSA) office." (ND State Hemp Plan, search extract.) Under 7 CFR 990, producers report hemp crop acreage to FSA, and the State reports planted / harvested / disposed-or-remediated acreage to AMS. The FSA lot identifier is built from Farm–Tract–Field–Subfield numbers (USDA producer guidance, general — Verify for the current NDDA/FSA workflow).
  • NDDA-to-USDA monthly reporting (plan): NDDA reports for each producer full name, licence number, address, telephone number, and email address if available, plus licence-status changes, "on the first of each month outlining the reporting period, using a USDA approved format." Producers with an EIN are to report the EIN directly to USDA (plan extract). This is the mechanism by which ND producers appear in USDA records — it is not a public holder list (see §7.9).
  • Pre-harvest sampling (summary; full detail is Part 11): Only NDDA-trained samplers may inspect and sample hemp under the plan. A representative sample is collected from flower material for each lot to determine the lot's acceptable THC level. The plan uses a performance-based protocol: NDDA inspects 10% of lots subject to performance-based sampling annually, and any variety not on the NDDA low-risk list is sampled (plan extract — Verify current low-risk list and current sampling percentages, which the plan states are updated annually). Under 7 CFR 990.3(a)(2), samples must be collected within 30 days before anticipated harvest by a sampling agent, and producers may not collect their own compliance samples. A secondary source states NDDA requires contact at least 30 days before projected harvest and that harvesting without NDDA consent is unlawful — treat that 30-day-contact claim as secondary / Verify against the current plan and NDDA instructions; do not present it as statute text. Non-compliant hemp is subject to disposal/remediation and reporting to USDA (7 CFR 990.70(b)); disposal costs are the grower's responsibility in secondary summaries — Verify against the current plan.
  • Record retention: Secondary summaries state reports must remain on file for no less than two years (pilot-era form language) while the plan extract states background-check results are kept "until updated or for 3 years past their last issued license." Do not adopt a single retention figure — Verify against the current plan/form.

7.7 Licence types that do NOT exist in North Dakota hemp law — and adjacent functions

Candidate typeNorth Dakota positionSource / note
Hemp handler licenceNo such NDDA licence type found. The "Hemp Handler License" form found in searches is an Oregon Department of Agriculture form (oda.direct/HempHandlers), not a North Dakota form. Do not import it. A North Dakota processor who dries/trims/processes hemp does so under the NDDA processor licence, not a handler licence.Oregon handler instructions (search result) — explicitly out-of-state; NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1 contains no handler section.
Hemp transporter / distributor licenceNo standalone transporter or distributor licence found in Chapter 4.1-18.1. Transport of lawful hemp is addressed in Run 2, Part 10 (interstate / shipment), not by a transporter licence in Part 7. If a transport document or manifest is required in current NDDA practice, it was not located — Verify.Negative search of ch. 4.1-18.1 and NDDA hemp page extract.
Hemp retailer / CBD retail licenceNone required. "Retailers in North Dakota are not required to have a license to sell hemp or hemp-derived CBD products." (secondary summary of NDDA position, consistent with Run 1, Part 3.) Retailers remain subject to the allowed/prohibited product rules, testing/COA and labelling rules, and general business/zoning rules — but there is no NDDA retail licence to apply for.NDDA hemp page extract (allowed/prohibited products); Run 1, Part 3.
Hemp finished-product / brand licenceNone. Product compliance is via the allowed-product definition, testing/COA, and labelling (§4.1-18.1-01, §4.1-18.1-04.2 and NDAC 7-20 as cited in Run 1), not a product licence.Run 1, Part 1.
Seed seller licence under the Hemp ProgramNone under the Hemp Program. "The Hemp Program does not require any licenses for selling seeds. Seed dealers will need to contact the North Dakota State Seed Department." (NDDA hemp page extract.) Growers are not required to purchase certified seed (NDDA page).NDDA hemp page — https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract].
Certified-seed production (adjacent, not a hemp licence)NDAC 74-03-16 (seed certification): "Only growers who possess a current license with the North Dakota department of agriculture are eligible to produce certified seed. Only varieties of hemp approved by regulatory authorities for seed production and which meet Federal Seed Act eligibility requirements are eligible for certification. Growers may be required … to obtain [THC] test results … [and] to submit test results to the seed certifying agency before labels are issued." Adopted July 2018, amended July 2020, under NDCC 4.1-52-10 / 4.1-53-42. This is a seed-certification eligibility rule, not a third hemp licence type.NDAC 74-03-16-01 (Cornell LII extract) — https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/north-dakota/N-D-A-C-74-03-16-01 — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract].
Research production (function, not a separate licence type)The plan provides a performance-based sampling protocol for hemp "grown for research purposes by a North Dakota institution under the control of the state board of higher education": researchers/research institutions must notify NDDA of intent to plant, and researchers must report lots to FSA; non-affiliated researchers are subject to standard licensing and sampling. No separate "research licence" section was located in Chapter 4.1-18.1 — research is a sampling-protocol category within the grower licence framework, not a distinct licence. University work is covered in full in Part 11.ND State Hemp Plan (search extract) — https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/NorthDakotaDepartmentofAgricultureHempPlan.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract].

7.8 Tribal hemp plans (state-plan boundary)

North Dakota's state plan does not cover hemp production on tribal land governed by an approved tribal plan. Two North Dakota tribal plans were located:

  • Standing Rock Sioux Tribe — Industrial Hemp Production Plan, submitted 13 January 2020, approved 10 March 2020. The reservation spans 2.3 million acres across Sioux County, ND and Corson County, SD; the plan assumes primary regulatory authority over hemp production on the reservation.
  • Source: Standing Rock plan PDF — https://www.ams.usda.gov/sites/default/files/media/StandingRockSiouxTribePlan.pdf — Accessed 2026-10-30 [Search extract].
  • Turtle Mountain Band of Chippewa Indians — USDA-approved tribal hemp plan, reported approved April 2020 (secondary reporting of the AMS approval; the AMS table entry itself was not fully extracted in this run — Verify the Turtle Mountain plan PDF and approval date on the AMS list).
  • Source: USDA approval reporting, April 2020 (search extract); Run 1, Part 5 gap 10 (AMS table truncation left other tribal plan statuses unconfirmed — that gap is not closed by this run for any tribe beyond Standing Rock and Turtle Mountain as above).
  • Whether any other North Dakota tribe (Spirit Lake Nation, MHA Nation / Three Affiliated Tribes, Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate) holds a USDA-approved hemp plan — Unavailable / Verify on the full AMS table. Do not assume a tribal plan exists for a tribe not listed above. Tribal cannabis (as opposed to hemp) is Part 10, not Part 7.

7.9 Registries — status (detail in Run 1, Part 5; not re-listed here)

  • NDDA hemp programme page + OLE portal (https://www.ndda.nd.gov/divisions/plant-industries/hemp): an application system, not a public holder list. No downloadable NDDA licensee list was found in Run 1, and none was located in Run 2. Registry status: no public NDDA hemp holder registry located — Unavailable.
  • USDA HeMP Public Search Tool: interactive search only, no bulk download stated; North Dakota coverage was not live-verified in Run 1 and was not live-verified in Run 2 (live-browser check required — see §7.10). Do not cite any HeMP-derived ND count in this report.
  • ND Secretary of State FirstStop (https://firststop.sos.nd.gov/search/business): interactive entity search only — proves entity existence/good standing, never proves a hemp licence.
  • Current official hemp licence counts (2021–2025): Unavailable. No current NDDA grower/processor count was located in Run 1 or Run 2. Historic counts that must not be presented as current: 81 growers in 2020 (Vote Hemp / Farm Flavor conflict on 2020 acres, Run 1 Part 6); 8 processors as of 2023 in a secondary summary (6 CBD, 1 fibre, 1 grain-and-seed, with the arithmetic as printed summing to more than 8 — do not quote that split without verification). The 2017 figures (35 growers, 1 processor, ~3,100 licensed acres) are historic pilot-era figures only.
  • No Run 1 correction to licence counts is made here, because no new official count was located to correct with.

Part 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.1 Headline count — read this before the table

  • Cannabis-tenant investment property verified in search extracts: 1 — 1513 Business Loop E, Jamestown, ND (Curaleaf tenant), §8.2.
  • Properties verified as specifically "cannabis-zoned" (i.e. a zoning code / ordinance category for cannabis use, as opposed to a general industrial/commercial zone that happens to house a dispensary): 0. The Jamestown property's LoopNet zoning is "AA6 – Industrial," a general industrial zone, not a cannabis-specific zoning category. No North Dakota listing was found that states a cannabis-specific zoning designation.
  • Hemp-marketed fee-simple properties verified: 0. No listing was found that markets a North Dakota property specifically for hemp growing or hemp processing (as opposed to general farmland or general industrial property that could be used for hemp).
  • 420Property North Dakota listings: 0. The 420Property North Dakota state page and city pages checked all returned "We couldn't find a listing for your search criteria" (see §8.4).
  • Out-of-state listings are NOT counted. The Moorhead, MN microbusiness listing that appears on 420Property's Fargo, ND page is in Minnesota, not North Dakota, and is excluded (see §8.5). The same exclusion applies to every California, Colorado, Florida, Mississippi, Montana, New Jersey, and New York listing that 420Property shows as "you may like" on North Dakota pages.

8.2 The one qualifying property

FieldDetail
Address1513 Business Loop E, Jamestown, ND 58401 (Stutsman County). Parcel Number 74-6684200 (LoopNet).
What it isA single-tenant, absolute-NNN retail investment property, fully leased to Curaleaf as tenant — i.e. the property's cannabis connection is its tenant, a North Dakota medical dispensary operator (Curaleaf operates the Jamestown dispensary in Run 1's roster; the dispensary address in directory sources is also 1513 Business Loop East, Jamestown). The sale is of the real estate, not of a licence: North Dakota compassion-centre certificates are nontransferable (Run 1, Part 4, NDCC §19-24.1-17), so a buyer acquires a tenanted building, not a dispensary licence.

Why it counts, and its limitIt counts because it is a North Dakota fee-simple property publicly listed for sale whose value proposition is explicitly a cannabis tenant (Curaleaf) in a capped medical market. Its limit is that it is not evidence of cannabis-specific zoning, and it is not a licence sale. A buyer would still need HHS registration to operate a dispensary, and the 8-dispensary cap (Run 1, Part 4) means a new dispensary cannot simply be added at this address.

8.3 Hemp properties — separate result: zero verified

No North Dakota property was found that is marketed for hemp use (hemp farm, hemp processing facility, hemp greenhouse, or hemp-zoned land) with a street address, zoning type, and price in a public listing in the searches tried (§8.4). General North Dakota farmland and general industrial listings exist in large numbers (e.g. LoopNet lists 94–118 North Dakota land/commercial-land listings in category extracts), but a general farmland or warehouse listing that could be used for hemp is not counted as a hemp-marketed property. Specific near-misses excluded for that reason are in §8.5. Hemp count: 0 verified.

This zero is consistent with, and does not contradict, Run 1: Run 1, Part 3 found no official source stating where hemp growers/processors are concentrated, and Run 1, Part 5 found no public NDDA holder list from which a hemp property could be identified. Hemp in North Dakota is an agricultural crop licence (Part 7), not a zoned retail use, so hemp properties do not appear in cannabis-real-estate marketplaces the way dispensary-tenant properties do.

Federal baseline used as given, not re-researched: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity and adult-use remains fully subject to 280E; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law.

Run 1 baseline read first: ~/workspace/your_files/north-dakota-cannabis-hemp-run1/north-dakota-cannabis-hemp-run1-parts-0-6.html and the four Run 1 source reports in ~/workspace/research_notes/north-dakota-cannabis-hemp-run1-20261030/ (part0-1.md, part2-3.md, part4-5.md, part6.md). Every figure below was checked against Run 1; where this run found something different, it is flagged RUN 1 CORRECTION with evidence, not silently changed.

9.1.1 Attorney General opinions — what was and was not found

  • No North Dakota AG opinion on delta-8, hemp-derived THC, or medical-marijuana scheduling was located in Run 1 or this run. Run 1's finding stands: secondary claims that an ND AG opinion bans delta-8 appear to conflate other states; the ND delta-8 ban rests on statute (HB 1045 (2021) + SB 2096 (2023)), not on a located AG opinion. The Delta 8 Resellers matter was an enforcement settlement, not an opinion — do not cite it as an opinion.
  • The AG's published guidance page does address CBD in general terms (December 2018 Farm Bill removed hemp from Schedule I; FDA has jurisdiction over cannabis-derived products; enforcement questions go to the prosecuting attorney with jurisdiction; sale/purchase questions go to private counsel or FDA) — https://attorneygeneral.nd.gov/criminal-justice-resources/guides-manuals-general-forms/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. That page is guidance, not an opinion and not law.
  • The one AG opinion located in Run 1's local-rules research and relevant here is the 6 April 1992 Dotzenrod letter on county/city/township zoning precedence — labelled opinion, not law (https://attorneygeneral.nd.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/040692-Dotzenrod_0.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, via Run 1). It is a zoning opinion, not a cannabis opinion.

Other ballot/legislative history: (a) 2015: HB 1430 (medical) failed in the House 26–67 (Vote Smart key votes, https://justfacts.votesmart.org/issues/ND/101 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026) — the failure that pushed supporters to the 2016 initiative. (b) 2020: a legalization initiative filed for 2020 failed to qualify, attributed in secondary reporting to COVID-era signature-gathering problems (Marijuana Moment, https://www.marijuanamoment.net/north-dakota-voters-reject-marijuana-legalization-ballot-initiative/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026) — Verify against SOS petition records; no 2020 cannabis measure was on the ballot. (c) A separate North Dakota Cannabis Caucus constitutional-amendment effort for 2022 did not gather enough signatures by its January deadline (same source) — Verify; it is not the certified 2022 Measure 2.

9.5 Part 9 — Run 1 corrections and gaps carried forward

RUN 1 CORRECTIONS (explicit):

  1. Tribal medical programme — Run 1's "no tribal medical programme found" is corrected for Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate (see §10.1): SWO adopted a Medical Cannabis Code on 2 Nov 2021 and operates a tribal medical-cannabis framework on the Lake Traverse Reservation, with a tribal-owned retailer (First Light, Browns Valley, MN — on reservation land in Minnesota, opened April 2025). Run 1 Part 5's statement that "other tribes' licence records [were] not found" is therefore incomplete for SWO; it remains true that no SWO (or any tribal) medical programme is an ND state-licensed programme — tribal licences are not HHS compassion-centre registrations, and whether tribal medical counts as "state-licensed" for federal Schedule III/280E remains Verify (see §10.1.6).
  2. First-dispensary date refined: 1 March 2019 (not just "March 2019").
  3. 2017 delay bill: SB 2154, not SB 2514 (see §9.2).
  4. "0.1% ND hemp limit" — Run 1's rejection is reaffirmed: a secondary CBD guide found in this run again claims ND applies a 0.1% delta-9 limit (https://www.newphaseblends.com/is-cbd-legal-in-north-dakota-where-to-buy-cbd-oil-in-north-dakota/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026). Run 1 rejected this as unsupported, and this run found no statute, NDAC provision, or NDDA page stating 0.1%. Do not adopt 0.1%. The ND hemp standard in Run 1 is total THC (THCa × 0.877 + delta-9) capped at the federal 0.3% ceiling in practice.
  5. HB 1420 tax companion: Run 1 Part 2's "HB 1420/HB 1501 (15% gross-receipts + 10% wholesale excise)" is confirmed as the HB 1501 tax structure (15% gross receipts, 3% to city/county, + 10% manufacturer-to-dispensary excise, per Marijuana Moment's Senate-day reporting) — the two bills are distinct; HB 1420 itself is the legalization bill, HB 1501 the tax bill. Both FAILED in the Senate.

Gaps (Verify/Unavailable) for Part 9: exact 2016 Measure 5 canvass counts; SB 2154 enrolled text; HB 1349 enrolled/signing date; SB 2096 civil-penalty figure against enrolled text; Board of Pharmacy Schedule III order (or absence confirmed in writing); Board of Pharmacy public contact; HB 1370 (2025) final disposition; DOT SoToxa purchase from a DOT source; 2020 initiative petition record; any post-2024 statewide poll; ND State Hemp Plan USDA approval date.

Part 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.0 Framework: sovereignty, PL 280, and why "tribal" ≠ "state-licensed" in this report

North Dakota's five federally recognised tribes are sovereign governments. Their cannabis/hemp authority on reservation/trust land comes from inherent sovereignty and, for hemp, from a USDA-approved tribal plan under the 2018 Farm Bill (7 U.S.C. §1639p) — not from NDCC ch. 19-24.1 or ch. 4.1-18.1. No ND–tribal cannabis or hemp compact was located in Run 1 or this run (see §10.1.6). North Dakota is a Public Law 280 state for criminal jurisdiction in limited respects, but no PL 280 analysis is attempted here — Verify with a tribal-law source before any statement about state criminal jurisdiction on a specific reservation, because jurisdiction varies by reservation, land status (trust vs. fee), and offence.

The "state-licensed" question (federal baseline): the baseline says state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III. A tribal medical programme licensed by a tribal commission is not on its face a state licence. Whether the federal rule's "state-licensed" language covers tribal-licensed medical activity — for SWO (the only ND-linked tribe with a medical code, §10.1.5) or for any future tribal programme — is Verify unless and until a federal rule, IRS guidance, or DEA/DOJ statement settles it. Do not advise a tribal licensee that 280E is lifted on the basis of this report.

10.1 The five federally recognised tribes

Note on the count: one secondary directory (500 Nations) lists only four tribes "in North Dakota" because it counts Standing Rock once and omits Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate from the ND list (its headquarters/reservation are primarily in South Dakota). The commission's five-tribe list is followed here, with Sisseton-Wahpeton's ND footprint stated separately. Do not copy the four-tribe count into the ND report.

10.1.1 Standing Rock Sioux Tribe (Sioux County, ND / Corson County, SD)

  • Land: Standing Rock Sioux Reservation, 2.3 million acres across Sioux County, ND and Corson County, SD (USDA plan, below).
  • Hemp — YES, USDA-approved tribal plan, no ND state licence needed on the reservation. Plan submitted 13 Jan 2020, approved 10 Mar 2020. Regulator: the Tribe's Commissioner of Agriculture, who promulgates implementing regulations under §302 of the Hemp Ordinance (Code of Justice, Industrial Hemp Regulations, Appendix A to the Plan).
  • Verbatim operative text (USDA plan / Hemp Ordinance, as printed in the plan):

"Through this Plan and pursuant to Agricultural Improvement Act of 2018, the Tribe hereby assumes primary regulatory authority over the production of hemp on the Reservation."
"Industrial hemp, as defined in this Title, is not marijuana and is not a controlled substance. The provisions of the Standing Rock Code of Justice, Title IV, Chapter 8, including § 4-801(7) do not apply to industrial hemp grown pursuant to a license issued under this Title or otherwise produced pursuant to federal law. Industrial hemp is an agricultural commodity that is subject to regulation under this Title."
"The Tribe intends to authorize the growth and handling of industrial hemp on the Reservation by Indians, as a matter of Tribal law, and to provide a sound regulatory framework to provide for the safety of industrial hemp crops and the Reservation community."

  • Source: https://www.ams.usda.gov/sites/default/files/media/StandingRockSiouxTribePlan.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Testing standard in the plan: testing "using post-decarboxylation or other similarly reliable methods, delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration levels" (plan summary, same PDF).
  • Cannabis (marijuana) — NO tribal medical or adult-use programme located. A 2014–2015 hemp-legalization petition effort (attorney Chase Iron Eyes; tribal chairman Dave Archambault II's caution about proceeding without federal blessing) is historical context only, not a current ordinance. The 2014 DOJ tribal marijuana guidance did not, in the chairman's contemporaneous assessment, change the transport problem (see §10.2). No Standing Rock dispensary, medical code, or adult-use ordinance was located — Verify against the current Code of Justice before asserting a negative for republication.
  • Who may buy: hemp is an agricultural commodity under the tribal plan; no tribal cannabis retail exists to buy from. Hemp produced under the plan may be sold as hemp (subject to the buyer's jurisdiction's hemp law — see §10.2 on interstate hemp).
  • State framework: none located — no ND–Standing Rock cannabis/hemp compact, no ND state licence on the reservation for hemp (tribal plan governs on reservation land).

10.1.2 Spirit Lake Nation (Spirit Lake Tribe; Benson County, ND — formerly Devils Lake Sioux)

  • Hemp — NO USDA-approved tribal plan located, NO tribal hemp code located. The USDA approved-plans table (updated 17 Feb 2026) does not list Spirit Lake; the 500 Nations ND table likewise lists only Turtle Mountain for ND (plus Standing Rock under SD). Spirit Lake's tribal code as indexed by NILL was last amended 24 Mar 2014 — before the 2018 Farm Bill hemp framework — and no hemp title was located in its table of contents in this run.
  • Sources: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — Accessed 30 Oct 2026; https://narf.org/nill/codes/spirit_lake/index.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026; https://500nations.com/North_Dakota-Cannabis.asp — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Cannabis — NO tribal medical or adult-use programme or ordinance located. Verify against the current Spirit Lake Law and Order Code (tribal website) before asserting a negative for republication, because the NILL index is from 2014 and the tribe publishes its code on its own website.
  • Consequence: on Spirit Lake land, hemp production would fall under the USDA producer-licence route (no tribal plan) or the ND state plan only to the extent that plan's jurisdiction reaches the land in question — Verify with USDA/NDDA; do not state that the ND state plan licenses production on Spirit Lake trust land.
  • Who may buy: nothing tribal to buy; ND state medical rules apply off-reservation (nearest ND dispensary: Devils Lake, Curaleaf — Run 1 Part 3).

10.1.3 MHA Nation — Three Affiliated Tribes (Mandan, Hidatsa, Arikara; Fort Berthold Reservation, ND)

  • Hemp — NO USDA-approved tribal plan located, NO tribal hemp code located. MHA does not appear on the USDA approved-plans table in this run's searches. Verify against the Fort Berthold Tribal Code (Title III, ch. 25 Drugs is a controlled-substances/paraphernalia code, not a hemp code — draft for public comment, https://www.mhanation.com/s/TITLE-III-Chapter-25.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026).
  • Cannabis — DECRIMINALIZED for medical use, NOT legalized; NO dispensary, NO commercial programme. Buffalo's Fire (2025, reporting the July 2025 alterNATIVE Medicine Symposium in New Town) states the drug was "recently decriminalized on the Fort Berthold reservation" and, crucially: "without actually legalizing the drug, cannabis dispensaries aren't allowed to operate on the reservation, making access inconvenient for tribal residents." — https://www.buffalosfire.com/mha-nation-decriminalizes-medical-marijuana — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary, tribal-member reporting; the decriminalization resolution number and date were not located in this run — Verify against Tribal Business Council resolutions before citing a date or quoting the resolution).
  • Current tribal direction — advisory survey, October 2026: MHA Nation's official website reports an Advisory Survey to tribal members 18+ (return by 27 October) asking, among other questions: "Do you support legalization of medical cannabis for MHA Nation?" — with the stated purpose that responses "will provide the Tribal Business Council guidance on membership's opinion about the legalization of prescription cannabis and related products on Fort Berthold." — https://www.mhanation.com/advisorysurvey/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (official tribal source). This is a survey, not a law, not a licence, and not a programme. Its results were not located — Verify after October 2026.
  • Who may buy: no tribal retail; tribal members who are ND registered patients may buy at ND state dispensaries (nearest: Minot, Williston regions) under ND rules; non-patients have no tribal retail alternative.
  • State framework: none located — no ND–MHA cannabis compact. Do not conflate MHA's decriminalization with the Blackfeet (Montana) 2018 medical decriminalization resolution or the Fort Peck history — different tribes, different states.

10.1.4 Turtle Mountain Band of Chippewa Indians (Rolette County, ND)

  • Hemp — YES, USDA-approved tribal plan; tribal Hemp Code, Title 51. USDA announced approval 3 Apr 2020 (with Oglala Sioux and Seneca Nation). Regulator: Turtle Mountain Hemp Regulatory Commission (Title 51, ch. 51.03).
  • Verbatim operative text (Title 51, via NILL / tribal law library):
  • Findings / primary authority, §51.01.010: "Hemp is a valuable agricultural crop" and "This title will protect the health, safety, and welfare of the Tribe by becoming the primary regulatory authority over the production and processing of hemp within the Tribe's jurisdiction" — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.01.010/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Purpose, §51.01.020: "This title establishes the requirements for the licensing, cultivation and processing of hemp. The purposes of this Plan are to: (1) Promote the production of hemp, and the development of new commercial markets for farmers and business through the sale of hemp products; (2) Promote the creation of the Tribe's hemp industry to the maximum extent permitted by law; and (3) Regulate hemp as an agricultural commodity in compliance with Federal law." — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.01.020 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Commission powers, §51.03.030: "The Hemp Regulatory Commission shall have the following powers to regulate hemp production and processing within the Tribe's jurisdiction: (1) Promulgate implementing regulations for this title as is necessary and appropriate; (2) Set the policies and procedures and establish and collect license and testing fees for all hemp production and processing; … (4) Review applications and issue licenses for hemp cultivation and processing; (5) Conduct (or cause to be conducted) the sampling, testing, and inspection of industrial hemp fields and industrial hemp handling facilities…" — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.03.030/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Compliant plants, §51.08.050: "(a) The THC content for hemp produced or processed on the reservation may not exceed .3%. If .3% or less is within the sample's measurement of uncertainty distribution range, the sample will be considered hemp for the purposes of this title. (b) Cultivation sites that meet the acceptable THC level may be harvested and made available for commercial sale." — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.08.050/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Processing, §51.09.010: "(a) A Hemp Processing Licensee may process, manufacture, package, and store hemp and hemp products. (b) A Hemp Processing Licensee may purchase hemp and hemp products from a hemp producer licensed in any jurisdiction. (c) A Hemp Processing Licensee may market and sell processed hemp and hemp products on or off the reservation. (d) A Hemp Processor Licensee shall not accept or process any hemp material with a THC level greater than .3%." — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.09.010/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Eligibility, §51.05.030: persons convicted of a disqualifying drug conviction (as defined in §51.02.010(k)) are ineligible for production or processing licences; a licensee that negligently violates Title 51 three times in five years is ineligible for five years from the third violation; material falsification in an application is disqualifying — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.05.030/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Negligent violation, §51.10.020: includes producing hemp over .3% "unless the Licensee took reasonable efforts to grow hemp and the cannabis produced does not have a THC concentration level of more than .5% on a dry weight basis" — https://www.narf.org/nill/triballaw/us/nsn/tmchippewa/council/code/51.10.020 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Full tribal PDF (2020): http://tmchippewa.com/wp-content/uploads/2020/08/TITLE-51-Hemp.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (NILL notes: "if you need an official version of the tribe's laws, please contact the tribe").
  • Cannabis (marijuana) — NO tribal medical or adult-use programme or ordinance located under Title 51 (hemp only) or elsewhere in this run. Verify against the current Turtle Mountain Tribal Code before asserting a negative for republication.
  • Who may buy: hemp and hemp products may be marketed/sold on or off the reservation by a licensed processor (§51.09.010(c) above); no tribal cannabis retail exists.
  • State framework: none located — no ND–Turtle Mountain cannabis compact; hemp on the reservation is tribal-plan governed, not NDDA-licensed.

10.1.5 Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate (Lake Traverse Reservation — primarily South Dakota, with North Dakota land)

  • Geography — read carefully: the Lake Traverse Reservation is centred in northeastern South Dakota (Roberts County, SD) and extends into southeastern North Dakota (Richland County area / Sargent County fringe in secondary descriptions; the reservation's ND acreage is small relative to its SD acreage). Any statement about SWO in an ND report must specify that the tribe's government, code, and retail sit primarily on the SD side, and must not present an SD-sited dispensary as an ND dispensary. The tribe is included here because the commission lists it among ND's five tribes and because its reservation does extend into ND.
  • Medical cannabis — YES, tribal code + commission + retail (the Run 1 correction). The Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate Medical Cannabis Code, ch. 81, was adopted by Tribal Council 2 Nov 2021 (Motion No. 11) and amended 22 Dec 2021 (Motion No. 74), 7 Dec 2022 (Motion No. 52, ratified 7 Mar 2023, Motion No. 42), 31 May 2023 (Motion No. 119), 2 Aug 2023 (Motion No. 67), 24 Apr 2024 (Motion No. 100), and 12 Dec 2024 (Motion No. 96). Regulations were adopted by the SWO Medical Cannabis Commission 31 Mar 2022 and revised 7 June 2024.
  • Sources: code PDF, https://www.swo-nsn.gov/media/vycheei0/6-18-25-med-cannabis-code-amendments.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026; regulations PDF, https://swo-nsn.gov/media/wpkgcavq/regs-revisions-on-section-43-and-note-at-the-end-72424-1.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (both official tribal-government sources, swo-nsn.gov).
  • Verbatim operative text (SWO Medical Cannabis Code, ch. 81):
  • Purpose/scope, §81-01-03: "That the Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate hereby exercises its inherent sovereign authority to enact legislation and possesses primary regulatory authority over all commerce, including Medical Cannabis on the Reservation and those lands subsequently acquired by the Tribe." — code PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Commission / hemp overlap, §81-01-01 (as printed in the code's table-of-contents extract): "Cannabis Commission may also be authorized to regulate Industrial Hemp and other cannabis or cannabis-related industries, as provided by SWO law and policy." — code PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Ownership, Title V (licensing extract): "At least 51% of a licensed business must be owned by a Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate tribal member, or an authorized tribal entity, or a tribal district owned business entity." — code PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Commission composition (note the internal inconsistency in the source, preserved here, not smoothed over): "The Commission shall have five (4) regular members comprised of three (3) Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate tribal members and one (1) ex-officio Tribal Council Members." — code PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Verify the correct number (five vs. four) against the current code before republication.
  • Territorial applicability (Regulations §1.4): "These Regulations apply to the following properties and all interests in such property: A. Reservation or trust lands; B. All buildings and other improvements of a permanent nature now or hereafter located on reservation and trust lands." — regulations PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Retail purpose (Regulations, ch. 4 / purpose): "Establish protocols for the cultivation/manufacture (growing), packaging, distribution, advertisement, and rules governing the locations and operations of retail Medicinal Cannabis outlets on the Reservation to meet the needs of Tribal Members and/or Qualified Patients and Designated Caregivers on the Reservation." — regulations PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • General prohibitions, §81-06-01 (extract truncated in search): "Nothing in this Code shall operate or be construed to allow the State of South Dakota, the State of North Dakota, or any political subdivision thereof the ability to impose criminal…" — code PDF (above) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. The full §81-06-01 text was not extracted in this run — Verify the complete section from the PDF before quoting beyond this fragment.
  • Regulator: SWO Medical Cannabis Commission (tribal, not ND HHS, not SD state).
  • Retail — YES, but in Minnesota, not North Dakota: First Light Dispensary, 11882 Lake Rd., Browns Valley, MN 56219 — "a tribal owned cannabis product retailer… located on the Lake Traverse Reservation… We opened our doors in April of 2025" — https://www.firstlightdispensary.com/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (tribal retailer's own site; hours noon–8pm as printed). Do not list First Light as an ND dispensary. It is on reservation land that lies in Minnesota for this address; whether SWO operates any retail on the ND portion of the reservation was not located — Verify.
  • Who may buy: under the Regulations, retail outlets serve "Tribal Members and/or Qualified Patients and Designated Caregivers on the Reservation" (above). Whether non-member adults may buy at First Light, and what card/recognition (if any) applies, was not located in the tribal code extracts — Verify against the current Regulations ch. 3 (Registry) and ch. 4 (Licensing) before stating a buyer rule. Do not assume Minnesota adult-use rules apply inside the tribal retail.
  • Hemp — USDA plan status: CONFLICT, flagged, not resolved. USDA's own approved-plans announcements list Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate among previously approved tribal plans (e.g. USDA release 8 Jan 2021, https://www.ams.usda.gov/content/usda-approves-hemp-production-plans-rhode-island-soboba-band-luiseno-indians — Accessed 30 Oct 2026; and the 2021 content analysis listing SWO as an "Independent Approved Plan," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10245984/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026). A 2025 CSBS job aid, by contrast, lists "Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate — USDA Hemp Producer License" (https://www.csbs.org/sites/default/files/2025-03/Industrial%20Hemp%20Job%20Aid%20March%202025.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026). The current USDA table extract in this run was truncated at "Sisseton-Wahpeton O…" and does not settle it. Adopt neither as current; Verify on the live USDA table (https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review) before republication. Separately, the SWO code itself says the Cannabis Commission "may also be authorized to regulate Industrial Hemp" — that is an authorization, not proof of a current hemp plan or hemp operation.
  • State framework: none located — no ND–SWO or SD–SWO cannabis compact was located in this run. SWO's code expressly disclaims state criminal jurisdiction (fragment above) — that is the tribe's position in its own code, not a compact and not a court holding. Do not present it as an agreement with ND.

Compact finding (explicit, as commissioned): No interstate or state–tribal cannabis/hemp compact involving North Dakota and any of the five tribes was located in Run 1 or this run.
