10.2 Interstate issues

10.2.1 Hemp shipments — what the law protects, and where ND law still bites

  • Federal protection (2018 Farm Bill, as summarised in secondary legal analysis — Verify the current 7 CFR 990.63 text before quoting): "No State or Indian Tribe shall prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with [the federal hemp subtitle] through the State or the territory of the Indian Tribe" (2018 Farm Bill §10114, quoted in a shipper notice at https://carolinahempcafe.com/policies/shipping-policy — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary). The current regulation is 7 CFR 990.63 (interstate transportation) — the regulation text itself was not opened in this run, so the protection is stated at the statute-summary level only.
  • ND application: compliant hemp (lawfully produced under an approved state/tribal plan, within the THC definition then in force) may be shipped through ND. The protection does not extend to products ND law treats as marijuana: delta-8, THC-O, HHC, THCP, and hemp-extract inhalants are prohibited in ND (Run 1 Part 1; SB 2096), so carrying them through ND can result in criminal charges "regardless of where you bought them or whether they were legal in the state of purchase" (secondary legal analysis, https://legalclarity.org/is-delta-8-thc-legal-in-north-dakota/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 — secondary, but consistent with Run 1's statutory finding).
  • Practical consequence for shippers: a hemp shipment through ND should carry the producer licence, lot/test documentation showing compliant THC, and no prohibited cannabinoid — Verify NDDA's current shipment-documentation guidance (none located as a standalone NDDA shipment rule in this run) before advising a carrier.
  • Historical note (not current law): in Dec 2016, NDDA told Healthy Oilseeds, LLC that export of its pilot-programme hemp protein powder/oil would require a DEA permit because industrial hemp was then a Schedule I substance federally (HIA v. DEA exhibits summary, https://www.wearemovementmedia.com/2017/02/06/hemp-industries-association-sues-dea-over-illegal-attempt-to-regulate-hemp-foods-as-schedule-i-drugs/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary). That position was overtaken by the 2018 Farm Bill's descheduling of hemp and interstate-commerce clause — do not cite the 2016 NDDA position as current.
  • Federal hemp-definition change (baseline): after 11 Dec 2026, the total-THC definition and 0.4 mg/container cap will change what counts as "compliant hemp" for interstate purposes. No ND guidance on how ND law enforcement will treat in-transit hemp under the new definition was located — Verify in Run 2 follow-up / Part 11.

10.2.2 North Dakota residents buying in Minnesota (adult-use)

  • Minnesota law (for context, not ND law): adult-use legal for 21+; retail sales began in 2025 (state-licensed and tribal stores); public possession up to 2 oz flower / 8 g concentrate / 800 mg edibles; 2 lb at a private residence (Minn. Stat. §342.09, via secondary summaries). No Minnesota residency requirement for adult-use purchase: "Anyone 21 or older with a valid government-issued photo ID can purchase cannabis at any licensed Minnesota dispensary regardless of state of residence" (Waabigwan Mashkiki / East Grand Forks reporting, https://mncannabishub.com/news/waabigwan-mashkiki-east-grand-forks-opens-may-2026 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary). The nearest Minnesota stores to ND are in Moorhead (across the Red River from Fargo) and East Grand Forks (across from Grand Forks; White Earth Nation's Waabigwan Mashkiki, 926 Central Ave. NE, opened 1 May 2026 per the same secondary source — Verify the opening date against the store/OCM before republication).
  • What ND law says on bring-back — the answer is: nothing lawful to bring back. (a) Federal: transporting marijuana across a state line is a federal offence, even between two legal states, and even for medical marijuana (secondary summaries are unanimous; the federal statute itself — 21 U.S.C. §841/§843 — was not opened in this run, so cite the principle, not a subsection, until verified). (b) ND state: once in ND, the marijuana is possessed without an ND registry card (unless the person holds a valid ND patient/caregiver/nonresident card AND the product is ND-dispensed — see §10.2.4, which does not protect out-of-state product). Possession penalties on return are Run 1's §19-03.1-23(7) tiers: <½ oz infraction (up to $1,000 fine, no jail); ½ oz–500 g Class B misdemeanour (up to 30 days / $1,500); >500 g Class A misdemeanour (up to 360 days / $3,000); THC tiers <2 g / 2–6 g / >6 g on the same infraction / Class B / Class A ladder. A secondary ND medical-card site summarises the same tiers (https://www.ndmedcard.com/why-get-a-card.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary — consistent with Run 1, but Run 1's statute is the authority).
  • No ND "border enforcement" programme was located (no ND statute or Highway Patrol policy creating border checkpoints for cannabis was found). Enforcement on return is ordinary possession enforcement if stopped/searched for another reason — do not invent a checkpoint programme.
  • Minnesota medical reciprocity — not relevant to ND bring-back: Minnesota does not need to recognise ND cards for an ND resident to buy Minnesota adult-use (21+ ID suffices); Minnesota medical reciprocity is a separate question and does not make Minnesota product lawful in ND.

10.2.3 North Dakota residents buying in Montana (adult-use)

  • Montana law (for context): adult-use legal for 21+ with valid ID, including out-of-state visitors ("Adults age 21 or older can purchase marijuana… including Montana residents, residents of other U.S. states and territories and international travelers" — Glacier Country tourism summary, https://glaciermt.com/recreational-marijuana — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary). Purchase up to 1 oz per transaction (or 800 mg edibles / 8 g concentrate equivalent); sales only in "green" (opt-in) counties; 20% sales tax (+3% local option in Missoula County, per the same summary). Nearest Montana adult-use stores to western ND are in the Fairview / eastern Montana area (secondary dispensary marketing, https://www.seedoflifelabs.com/north-dakota-dispensary/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, commercial source — Verify any specific store before naming it in the compiled file).
  • Bring-back: identical to Minnesota — federal interstate-transport offence + ND possession tiers on return. Montana's own guidance is explicit: "It is illegal to cross state lines with marijuana in your possession" (Glacier Country, above). Montana does not accept out-of-state medical cards for medical purchases (secondary summaries, https://cannacon.org/cannabis-rules-out-of-state-buyers/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026), so an ND medical card buys nothing extra in Montana beyond the 21+ adult-use right.
  • Other neighbours: South Dakota (medical + adult-use by 2020 vote, with subsequent litigation history — not detailed here because the commission asks for MN/MT specifically; do not import SD rules into ND), Wyoming and Idaho (both fully illegal for adult-use — relevant only as transit states: carrying Montana/Minnesota product through WY/ID adds those states' possession offences on top of federal/ND exposure). Verify SD/WY/ID current law from those states' dossiers before any statement about them in the compiled file.

10.2.4 Out-of-state patients and what can legally be brought into North Dakota — SB 2294 nonresident pathway RESOLVED (Run 1 gap closed in part)

Run 1 gap: "enrolled SB 2294 nonresident section (number, eligibility, fee, 60-day duration)" was Verify. This run resolves the core of it from HHS's own instructions and the engrossed bill:

  • New NDCC section created by SB 2294, §7: "Qualifying patients — Nonresidents" (engrossed SB 2294, LC 25.1233.02000): "In lieu of the written certification required under section 19-24.1-03, a nonresident who…" (engrossed text, http://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-1233-02000.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026). The engrossed text states the department "shall issue a registry identification card within thirty calendar days of approving an application under this section" and "The issued registry identification card expiration date must be the same as the out-of-state medical marijuana card" (same engrossed text, §§7–8 extracts). The enrolled (final) section number in the Century Code was not confirmed in this run — Verify whether the new section is codified as §19-24.1-03.1 (the fiscal note cites "19-24.1-03.1" among amended sections, https://legis.nd.gov/assembly/69-2025/regular/fiscal-notes/25-1233-04000-fn.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, but that citation is in a list of amended sections, not a confirmed codification of the new nonresident section).
  • HHS Nonresident Adult Patient Application Instructions (Mar 2026, hhs.nd.gov) — the operative public rule:
  • Eligibility: "To be eligible to apply, a nonresident must reside in one of the 49 other states, District of Columbia, or a United States territory and have a medical marijuana card issued from the state of residency." — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Nonresident-Patient-Application-Instructions.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Age: "Nonresident adult applicants must be 19 years of age or older. Those 18 years of age and under interested in applying for a nonresident medical marijuana card should contact program personnel at 701-328-1311 or TTY 711." — same instructions.
  • Validity: the home-state ID and home-state medical card "must be valid for the duration of the 60-day nonresident card. If one expires during this time the application will not be approved." — same instructions. Secondary summaries (https://www.mycannabis.com/cannabis-in-north-dakota/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026) confirm: "merely presenting an out-of-state card is not enough" and "allow two to four weeks for processing" (HHS: "please allow 2-4 weeks" after a complete application, same instructions).
  • Fee: "$40 non-refundable application fee can be paid online only." — same instructions. (This matches SB 2294's $40 card-fee cap; the older $25 resident fee in some secondary summaries is superseded — see Run 1 Part 1.)
  • Applications via the BioTrackTHC registration portal (same instructions).
  • What this does NOT do (critical, do not overstate):
  • It does not recognise an out-of-state card by itself. HHS's patient FAQ is explicit: "North Dakota does not recognize other states' medical marijuana cards. Without a valid North Dakota medical marijuana card, an individual in possession of marijuana has no protections under the North Dakota medical marijuana laws." — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Frequently-Asked-Questions-exclusion.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • It does not authorise bringing marijuana bought in another state into ND. A nonresident card authorises purchase from an ND registered dispensary and possession of ND-dispensed product within ND limits. Out-of-state product remains ND-unlawful possession (tiers in §10.2.2) and federally unlawful interstate transport.
  • It does not authorise a non-patient tourist to buy anything. Ordinary tourists (no home-state medical card) cannot buy ND medical marijuana and cannot buy ND adult-use (none exists).
  • What CAN legally be brought into ND (complete list, as of 30 Oct 2026):
  • Compliant hemp / hemp products (federal + ND hemp definition, no prohibited cannabinoids) — §10.2.1.
  • FDA-approved cannabis-derived drugs (e.g. Epidiolex, Marinol/Syndros, Cesamet) under a valid prescription — federal baseline, not ND medical programme product.
  • Nothing else cannabis: no Minnesota/Montana adult-use product, no out-of-state medical product, no delta-8/THC-O/HHC/THCP product, no home-grown product from another state, no tribal-retail product from another state's reservation (including SWO First Light product bought in Minnesota — lawful where bought, unlawful to transport into ND and unlawful to possess in ND without an ND card covering ND-dispensed product; and even with an ND card, the product's out-of-state origin is not covered — Verify this last point against HHS guidance, because HHS's "only purchase and possess products dispensed from [an ND dispensary]" formulation in secondary summaries (ASA, https://www.safeaccessnow.org/becoming_a_patient_in_north_dakota — Accessed 30 Oct 2026) points that way but the enrolled nonresident text extract does not state it in the lines opened).
  • ND patients going the other way: an ND medical card does not guarantee recognition elsewhere. Whether MN, MT, SD, or any other state recognises an ND card is that state's law (see §10.2.2–10.2.3 for MN/MT adult-use, which does not need a card at all for 21+).

10.2.5 Canada border (Manitoba and Saskatchewan)

  • Rule (both directions, no exception for medical use without a Health Canada permit/exemption): "Although cannabis has been legalized and regulated in Canada, it remains illegal to bring cannabis (and cannabis products) into or out of Canada – whether it is a gift or for personal use" unless the traveller holds "a valid permit or an exemption issued by the Government of Canada" (CBSA, https://www.cbsa-asfc.gc.ca/multimedia/reels-bobines/cannabis-2/transcript-transcription-eng.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, official Government of Canada source). CBSA's boater notice (27 Apr 2026) is to the same effect: "Do not bring cannabis across the border… without a permit or exemption authorized by Health Canada… A medical prescription from a doctor does not count as Health Canada authorization" — https://www.canada.ca/en/border-services-agency/news/2026/04/the-cbsa-reminds-private-boaters-of-reporting-requirements.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Manitoba (province entered from ND at Pembina/Emerson and other crossings): Manitoba's own travel page: "It's illegal to take cannabis across the Canadian border, whether you're leaving or coming to Canada. This applies to all countries, whether cannabis is legal there or not. If you use cannabis, purchase and consume it within the province or territory you are in." — https://www.gov.mb.CA/cannabis/knowthefacts/cannabisandtravel.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (official Manitoba source). Within Manitoba, cannabis must be stored in the trunk / exterior compartment / space not readily accessible to any person in the vehicle (Manitoba Highway Traffic Act, same page) — that is a Manitoba in-province transport rule, not a border rule, and does not authorise border crossing.
  • Saskatchewan: no Saskatchewan-specific border rule was located beyond the federal CBSA rule (which applies at every crossing, including ND–Saskatchewan crossings). Verify Saskatchewan's provincial cannabis transport/storage rules from saskatchewan.ca before stating them; do not copy Manitoba's trunk rule onto Saskatchewan.
  • US side: CBP treats marijuana as a controlled substance at the border regardless of Canadian legality or US state legality; crossing with cannabis can mean denied admission (for non-citizens), seizure, fines, or arrest (secondary summary, https://www.thetravel.com/the-6-new-rules-for-canadians-entering-the-us-by-land/ (26 Oct 2026) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary — CBP's own border-cannabis page was not opened in this run, Verify). CBD is flagged in that secondary source as a "gray area" after the federal hemp change — do not advise that CBD may be carried across; CBSA's notice expressly includes "products or oils containing THC or CBD" in its do-not-bring warning (above).
  • Consequences (Canada): moving cannabis across the border is a criminal offence under Canada's Cannabis Act, with up to 14 years' imprisonment cited in secondary summaries (https://www.thetravel.com/the-6-new-rules-for-canadians-entering-the-us-by-land/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, secondary — Verify the penalty provision against the Cannabis Act before quoting a maximum).
  • ND-specific point: an ND medical card is not a Health Canada authorization and does not authorise border crossing in either direction. An ND patient who lawfully buys in ND still cannot take that product into Manitoba/Saskatchewan, and a Manitoba/Saskatchewan lawful purchase still cannot be brought into ND (see §10.2.4 list).
  • No ND–Canada or ND–Manitoba/Saskatchewan cannabis agreement was located. No interstate (state-to-state) cannabis commerce agreement involving ND was located either — ND has no adult-use market to trade, and its medical programme has no reciprocity agreement (only the SB 2294 nonresident card, which is unilateral ND registration, not an agreement).

Access date for all sources in this file: 30 October 2026, unless a source itself bears a different date, stated in text. RUN 1 CORRECTIONS (explicit — read before merging)

  1. CORRECTION (AUDITED) — Plant surcharge: $5,000 payable, not the superseded 2019 figure; the the superseded 2019 figure was the 2019 SB 2210 amount, superseded by SB 2201 (2023) to a $7,500 ceiling. Run 1's compiler adopted $5,000 per 500 plants over 1,000 from NDAC §33-44-01-07.4 and flagged the superseded 2019 figure as "superseded/conflicting". The 2019 statute said the superseded 2019 figure; SB 2201 (2023) changed it to a ceiling of $7,500, and NDAC §33-44-01-07.4 sets $5,000 payable. NDCC §19-24.1-24, as amended by SB 2210 (2019, enrolled text): "For every five hundred plants in excess of one thousand plants a manufacturing facility possesses, the manufacturing facility shall pay the department an additional certification fee of not to exceed seven thousand five hundred (NDAC: five thousand payable) dollars. This fee is due at the time of increase and again at renewal of the compassion center registration certificate under section 19-24.1-16." — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/66-2019/documents/19-0890-04000.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. A secondary Cass County guide also states the superseded 2019 figure — https://northdakotacannabis.org/cass — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY]. Resolution: adopt $5,000 payable (ceiling $7,500) as the current surcharge; confirm the $5,000 NDAC figure as payable; do not downgrade it from [VERIFY] — it may reflect a different NDAC fee layer (application/certification schedule) or a misreading, and must be re-checked against a directly opened current NDAC §33-44-01-07.4 before either figure is quoted as the current NDAC amount. Do NOT carry the compiler's "$5,000 adopted, the superseded 2019 figure superseded" line into the combined file.
  2. CORRECTION / CLARIFICATION — Testing lab: Keystone/Dakota State Testing is HISTORICAL (2018), not current. Run 1 correctly left the current lab as Verify.   3. CLARIFICATION — "Delivery is legal / illegal" conflict (Run 1, Part 1/Part 11 referral): NOT RESOLVED. No NDAC text located in this run expressly authorises or prohibits patient delivery (as opposed to centre-to-centre/lab transport). Part 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

Each numbered item is given SEPARATELY for (a) medical licensees (compassion centres + contract laboratory) and (b) hemp businesses. Where no rule exists, that is stated as a negative finding, not filled in.

11.1 Testing

(a) Medical — required tests, sampling, failed tests, recalls, labs

Legal framework. NDCC §19-24.1-36 authorises the department to contract for laboratory testing; NDAC §§33-44-01-36 to 33-44-01-55 are the testing rules. NDAC §33-44-01-40: "A manufacturing facility must have all usable marijuana tested in accordance with sections 33-44-01-42, 33-44-01-43, and 33-44-01-44 by a laboratory selected by the department as described in section 33-44-01-36." [SEARCH-INDEX: NDAC text via ndlegis.gov/Cornell extracts] — NDAC chapter PDF: https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. §33-44-01-41 (Ordering Tests) requires the manufacturing facility to order the tests necessary to comply with ch. 19-24.1 and the rules, and to supply the lab, before sampling, with facility name/address/contact, type of usable marijuana, batch numbers, harvest/process lot numbers — https://ndlegis.gov/files/rule-changes/changes/doh040822changesmm.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].

What must be tested — by product type (every batch for transfer to a dispensary):

  • Dried leaves and flowers (§33-44-01-42, eff. 1 Apr 2018, amended 1 Oct 2019): every batch from a harvest lot to be packaged for transfer to a dispensary tested for (1) pesticides and degradation compounds (§33-44-01-47); (2) microbiological contaminants and mycotoxins (§33-44-01-48); (3) heavy metals (§33-44-01-48.1); (4) water activity and moisture content (§33-44-01-50); (5) concentration (§33-44-01-51). — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/admcode/arc201910374.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].
  • Cannabinoid concentrates (§33-44-01-43, eff. 1 Apr 2018, amended 1 Oct 2019): every process lot to be packaged for transfer tested for pesticides/degradation (§47), microbiological/mycotoxins (§48), heavy metals (§48.1), solvents (§49), concentration (§51). Solvent testing is exempt if no solvent was used, or if the department gives a written exemption for a closed-loop carbon-dioxide extraction method. — https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/north-dakota/N-D-A-C-33-44-01-43 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Medical cannabinoid products (§33-44-01-44): same panel as concentrates (pesticides, microbiological/mycotoxins, heavy metals, solvents where applicable, concentration), plus terpene analysis under §33-44-01-44.1 in the current section list. The per-product detail in §33-44-01-44 was not fully extracted in this run — [VERIFY full §44 text before quoting product-by-product detail]. — NDAC section list: https://ndlegis.gov/prod/acdata/pdf.bak/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Pass/fail standards located: microbiological (§48): total viable aerobic bacteria and total yeast/mold limits in CFU/g, pathogenic E. coli and Salmonella "not detected in one gram"; mycotoxins: total aflatoxins B1+B2+G1+G2 <20 µg/kg, ochratoxin A <20 µg/kg — https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/north-dakota/N-D-A-C-33-44-01-48 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Water activity/moisture (§50): fail if water activity >0.65 aw or moisture >15% — https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/north-dakota/N-D-A-C-33-44-01-50 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Concentration (§51): edible product may deviate from maximum concentration/amount by no more than 10% [SEARCH-INDEX, partial extract — VERIFY full §51]. Pesticide, heavy-metal and solvent numeric limits (§§47, 48.1, 49) were NOT extracted in this run — [UNAVAILABLE in this run; do not quote limits from secondary sites].
  • "Compliance test" definition (§33-44-01-01): "a test required by these rules to be performed by a laboratory selected by the department in order to allow the transfer or sale of usable marijuana." "Batch" = a quantity of dried leaves/flowers from a harvest lot, or a quantity of concentrate/product from a process lot. — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/acdata/pdf.bak/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].
  • Sampling rules: microbiological samples must use appropriate aseptic techniques (§48). Batch requirements for compliance testing are in §33-44-01-45; the lab's sampling protocol (who takes the sample, sample size, chain of custody) was not fully extracted — [VERIFY §§45/41]. Random testing (§33-44-01-54) exists in addition to compliance testing, and §33-44-01-36 separately authorises the department to contract for "random quality sampling testing". The department may therefore test outside the batch-compliance flow; the random-testing frequency/rate was not located — [VERIFY].
  • Failed tests (§33-44-01-52, Failed Test Samples): if a sample fails any required test, the sample may be retested/reanalysed; the department, in consultation with the laboratory, determines whether reanalysis uses samples held by the lab or a new sample from the batch. If a sample fails a test or reanalysis, the batch may be remediated or sterilized; if it is not, or cannot be, remediated/sterilized, the batch must be disposed of. Concentration-fail exception: a batch failing concentration testing under §51 may be remediated to meet ch. 19-24.1 limits; a pediatric batch failing concentration may instead be used for non-pediatric usable marijuana without further processing/testing if it is not labelled for pediatric use. — https://NorthDakotaCannabis.org/testing-lab — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY summary of §52] + https://ndlegis.gov/prod/admcode/arc201910374.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX, §52 heading + concentration-remediation extract]. Full §52 text [VERIFY before quoting verbatim].
  • Recalls (§33-44-01-16): "Each compassion center shall establish a procedure for issuing voluntary and mandatory recalls for usable marijuana." Factors requiring a recall are listed in the section but were not fully extracted — [VERIFY full §16]. Adverse reactions are separately reportable under §33-44-01-35; law-enforcement-reportable incidents under §33-44-01-34; centre/lab incidents (security breach, surveillance failure, power failure >2 hours, embezzlement/fraud, 911/law-enforcement contact, transport incidents, attempts to obtain marijuana other than as prescribed, violations) must be reported within 24 hours under §33-44-01-22. — https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].
  • Quality system (§33-44-01-55): manufacturing facility quality control and quality assurance programme required. Detail [VERIFY].
  • Licensed/contract labs — list: There is NO licensed-lab roster and NO lab licence. The lab is a state contract under NDCC ch. 54-44.4 procurement (§33-44-01-36: "The department may contract with a laboratory or laboratories… The department shall procure the laboratory testing services in accordance with [ch. 54-44.4]. An awarded laboratory must be properly accredited as determined by the department."). Lab activities include acquiring, possessing, storing, transferring and transporting marijuana/usable marijuana/waste under ch. 19-24.1 and the rules (§33-44-01-37); lab agents hold registry identification cards (§33-44-01-38, cards shredded on destruction, department notified in writing within 2 calendar days); labs are subject to random inspection (§33-44-01-39).
  • Historical (confirmed, 2018): Keystone State Testing (Pennsylvania), d/b/a Dakota State Testing, Fargo — awarded May 2018, expected Fargo lab, A2LA-accredited, Patient Focused Certification. — https://mjbizdaily.com/news/north-dakota-selects-medical-marijuana-manufacturers-lab-for-licensing/93763/ (22 May 2018) and https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/us-states/north-dakota/news/15695747/north-dakota-chooses-medical-marijuana-laboratory-company (22 May 2018) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY reporting of the 2018 HHS release].
  • 2025 procurement (PARTIAL RESOLUTION of Run 1 Verify): HHS sought a medical marijuana compliance-testing laboratory through a noncompetitive procurement (GovTribe listing, updated 1 Apr 2025, opportunity 325-111-23.24): services for Cass County and statewide under NDAC §§33-44-01-42/43/44; lab must be located in ND, ISO 17025-accredited, operational by 1 July 2025, staff pass criminal background checks; submissions due 8 Apr 2025, 3:00 p.m. Central, via State Procurement Office Online; contract effective on execution, initially expiring 30 June 2027 with renewals to 30 June 2031; manufacturing facilities pay testing costs directly under a negotiated standardised price; procurement "appears to be targeted exclusively at Adams Independent Testing, LLC" and NDDA noted only one ND lab conducts similar (hemp) testing. — https://govtribe.com/opportunity/state-local-contract-opportunity/medical-marijuana-program-compliance-testing-3251112324 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY procurement listing — the underlying HHS Notice of Intent PDF was not opened in this run]. Current lab therefore remains [VERIFY]: Adams Independent Testing, LLC is the indicated 2025 vendor, NOT a confirmed awardee on the evidence opened; do not publish a current-lab name without the award/contract.
  • Cost: 2025 procurement states manufacturing facilities pay testing costs directly; no per-test price was located — [UNAVAILABLE].

(b) Hemp — testing, sampling, failed tests

  • Who tests: NDDA under the USDA-approved ND State Hemp Plan (NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1; 7 CFR Part 990). Plan PDF: https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/NorthDakotaDepartmentofAgricultureHempPlan.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • What is measured: total THC on a dry-weight basis (Run 1 formula: THCa × 0.877 + delta-9). Plan: "Total THC will be calculated on a dry weight basis" and "NDDA will inspect 10% of lots subject to Performance-based Sampling annually. Any variety that is not on the low-risk list is sampled." Research hemp by an ND institution under the State Board of Higher Education uses a performance-based sampling protocol. — Plan PDF, Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].
  • Pre-harvest sampling window: Plan sampling protocol: "Registered producers must inform NDDA 15 days prior to planned harvest date. Harvest shall take place within 30 days of a NDDA sampling, product may not be processed or [leave]…" [extract truncated]. This matches the federal 30-day harvest window (7 CFR 990) — the producer gives 15 days' notice, NDDA samples, harvest must occur within 30 days of sampling. Older secondary guides citing a 15-day window or a 30-days'-notice requirement describe superseded/pilot-era practice and must not be substituted for the Plan text. Full protocol [VERIFY from a directly opened Plan PDF].
  • DEA-registered labs: Plan (as filed) states "After December 31st, 2022, hemp will only be tested at a DEA registered lab. Hemp testing labs performing regulatory samples for NDDA will report results only to NDDA. NDDA will report THC results to USDA… and kept on file for at least 3 years." Against the given federal baseline, USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing is delayed to 31 December 2026 — whether NDDA enforces the Plan's 2022 DEA-lab sentence today, or follows the federal delay, was NOT located in this run and is a live conflict to resolve before advising a grower: do not state either position as NDDA's current position. [VERIFY with NDDA].
  • Failed / non-compliant lots — remediation and disposal (Plan §C, Disposal): "Remediation will be allowed on non-compliant lots where the Total THC is less than 1%. Remediation can be accomplished by hand or mechanical removal and disposal of flower material leaving only stalk and seed. Remediation can also be achieved by shredding flower material with leaf and stalk to create a biomass. Any remediated material must be retested and fall below the acceptable hemp THC level. NDDA will work with producers to dispose of noncompliant products. NDDA staff will witness and verify destruction to render plants and material unusable." Method "can be any one or com…" [extract truncated]. Federal backstop (7 CFR §990.27): plants exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level constitute marijuana; producers must use a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement, or ensure on-site disposal at the farm/facility; notify USDA of intent to dispose/remediate and verify by documentation; post-remediation re-sampling/testing required. — https://law.lawstack.com/cfr/7/990.27/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Negligent-violation threshold (federal): a producer acting in good faith whose crop tests above 0.3% but at or below 1.0% faces a negligent violation and a corrective action plan of at least 2 years — https://legalclarity.org/industrial-hemp-legal-definition-what-federal-law-says/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY summary of 7 CFR 990.6 — verify against eCFR before quoting].
  • Commingling ban (Plan): "Hemp material cannot be commingled with material from other lots until allowable THC results are issued. Hemp [that] does not meet the acceptable hemp THC level cannot enter the stream of [commerce]…" [extract truncated — VERIFY].
  • Hemp product testing/labelling for retail CBD: No ND hemp-product (finished CBD) compliance-testing panel, lab licence, or retail testing rule was located in ch. 4.1-18.1 or the Plan in this run. Finished-product testing claims on secondary sites are NOT adopted. [UNAVAILABLE — see also Part 7 territory, not repeated here].
  • Federal 11 Dec 2026 effect (analysis, not ND law): after 11 Dec 2026 the federal total-THC definition + 0.4 mg/container cap apply to hemp products (baseline). No NDDA/HHS statement adapting ND's 0.3% total-THC plan language or any ND product rule to that cap was located — [VERIFY, carried from Run 1].

11.2 Licence rules — medical

  • Renewal (§19-24.1-16): certificate expires 2 years after issuance. Renewal application may be submitted beginning 90 calendar days before expiration and must be submitted a minimum of 60 calendar days before expiration to avoid suspension. Department must approve within 60 calendar days of submission if: renewal fee paid (statutory ceiling — see Run 1 fee layers), complete application, certificate never suspended, inspections raise no serious concerns, and centre continues to meet all requirements. If renewal is denied, department gives written notice; a non-renewed centre must dispose of all marijuana/usable marijuana under the rules. — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/68-2023/regular/documents/23-0711-01000.pdf (SB 2201, 2023, amending §§19-24.1-15/16) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX of enrolled/amended text; current §19-24.1-16 to be re-verified on ndlegis.gov]. Agent cards: compassion centre agent must renew no less than 45 calendar days before expiration of the existing card (§19-24.1-18, via ch. 19-24.1 text) — https://www.legis.nd.gov/cencode/t19c24-1.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX]. This matches Run 1 (90–60 days, 2-year cycle).
  • Transfers / sales / ownership changes — NONTRANSFERABLE (§19-24.1-17, as amended SB 2210, 2019): "A registration certificate authorizing operation of a compassion center may not be transferred to another person. Unless a compassion center applies for and receives an amended registration certificate…, the registration certificate is void if there is a change in ownership of the compassion center, there is a change in the authorized physical location of the compassion center, or if the compassion center discontinues operation." Written notice of any such change at least 60 calendar days before the proposed effective date; department may waive all/part of the notice for emergent/emergency situations. Department shall authorise use of additional structures within 500 feet [152.40 m] of the original location, unless it affirmatively finds the additional structures would jeopardise public health/safety or put the centre within 1,000 feet of a pre-existing public/private school property line. — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/66-2019/documents/19-0890-04000.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. In plain English: there is no "sale of a licence" — a buyer must obtain an amended certificate; an unapproved ownership/location change voids the certificate. No separate "change of control" percentage threshold was located — [VERIFY §17 full text].
  • Ownership caps (§19-24.1-13(3), Run 1-confirmed): max 1 manufacturing facility, max 4 dispensaries, max 1 dispensary within 20 miles of another dispensary under the same ownership; anti-exclusivity §19-24.1-13(4) (a centre may not enter an exclusive supply arrangement — detail [VERIFY]). No ownership cap change in 2025 bills was located.
  • Relocation: treated as a location change under §19-24.1-17 (above) — amended certificate required, 60 days' notice, plus fresh local-zoning evidence and 1,000 ft school evidence under §19-24.1-14 for the new address (Run 1). No separate relocation fee or distance-from-old-site rule was located — [VERIFY].
  • Surrender / revocation (§19-24.1-19): department may suspend or revoke a card or certificate for (1) material misstatement in an application/renewal, (2) violation of ch. 19-24.1 or rules. Suspension may not exceed 6 months; during suspension a manufacturing facility may continue to produce/process/possess but may not transfer or sell usable marijuana, and a dispensary may continue to possess but may not purchase, dispense or transfer usable marijuana. Appeal: denial/revocation appeal to district court of Burleigh County for hearing (electronic appearance permitted). If a certificate is not renewed, centre must dispose of all marijuana/usable marijuana under the rules (§19-24.1-16(3)). Violations/penalties are in §19-24.1-20 (including, per secondary summary, Class B felony for intentional out-of-chain transfer and for dispensing without a valid card — [VERIFY §20 text; secondary summary at https://www.flourishsoftware.com/states/north-dakota — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, SECONDARY]). Run 1 enforcement count: FY2024 revocations 4, all patients (not centres) — no centre-level revocation list was located in this run either [UNAVAILABLE].
  • Insurance / bonding: statute requires "a financial assurance or security bond to ensure the protection of the public health and safety and the environment in the event of abandonment, default, or other inability or unwillingness to meet the requirements of this chapter" (§19-24.1-15(1)(b)). NDAC caps (Run 1): $100,000 dispensary / $1,000,000 manufacturing — "dollars for a dispensary and may not exceed one million dollars for a manufacturing facility" [SEARCH-INDEX, NDAC §33-44-01-06/07 extract]. Actual assessed bond amounts per centre: [VERIFY] — Run 1 correctly left actuals unverified; this run found no centre-specific bond figure. No separate insurance minimum (general liability, product liability, crop insurance) was located in ch. 19-24.1 or NDAC 33-44-01 in this run — [UNAVAILABLE; do not import another state's $100k insurance figure].
  • Inspections / compliance (§§33-44-01-31/32): centres subject to inspection; plan of correction procedure in §32. Inspection frequency (announced/unannounced, annual) was not extracted — [VERIFY].
  • Additional centres: department may register additional centres only if it determines additional entities are necessary to increase patient access (§19-24.1-12(4)) or after revocation/non-renewal (§19-24.1-12(5)); NDAC §33-44-01-07 sets the additional-centre process (including location/proximity and patient-population considerations). No open application period was found in Run 1 and none was located in this run.

Hemp — licence rules (summary; full detail belongs to Part 7)

  • Licences expire annually on 31 December (Plan: "License numbers are assigned in a format prescribed by USDA and expire annually on December 31"); producers must submit hemp crop acreage to the local FSA office. — Plan PDF, Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Old NDAC ch. 7-14-02 (Industrial Hemp Production, eff. 1 Jan 2007, under former ch. 4-41) provided for suspension/revocation for noncompliance and a bar on re-licensing a noncompliant person — https://ndlegis.gov/files/rule-changes/changes/ag010616changes.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. That chapter is pilot-era and must NOT be quoted as the current 4.1-18.1 rule set without confirming it was not repealed/superseded by the USDA-approved plan regime — [VERIFY current NDAC hemp chapter]. Current transfer/renewal/relocation rules for hemp licences: [VERIFY — Part 7].
  • Negligent vs. culpable violations follow 7 CFR 990.6 (corrective action plan ≥2 years for negligent violations; culpable violations / repeat violations carry licence consequences) — detail [VERIFY against eCFR/Plan].

11.3 Cultivation

(a) Medical

  • Indoor / enclosed requirement: cultivation and processing must be indoors within an enclosed and locked structure; a locked greenhouse is allowed; growing/manufacturing must not be visible from any area open to the public. — https://northdakotacannabis.org/cass — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY — the underlying NDAC/statute sentence was not separately extracted in this run; VERIFY against NDAC §33-44-01-12/13 or §19-24.1-14/24 before quoting as law]. No outdoor medical cultivation rule was located; home grow is prohibited (Run 1).
  • Plant / inventory limits (§19-24.1-24 + NDAC §33-44-01-08): rules may not allow a manufacturing facility to possess more than 1,000 plants, regardless of stage of growth, and may not allow a dispensary to possess more than 3,500 ounces [99.22 kg] of usable marijuana at any time, regardless of formulation. A manufacturing facility must grow an amount sufficient to meet patient-population demand. Rules may allow up to 50 additional plants exclusively for department-authorised research and development related to production/processing. Surcharge: $5,000 payable per 500 plants over 1,000 (statutory ceiling $7,500), due at increase and again at renewal (see RUN 1 CORRECTION 1 above). — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/66-2019/documents/19-0890-04000.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. NDAC §33-44-01-08(1) begins "A manufacturing facility shall grow an amount of marijuana sufficient to meet the qualifying…" [SEARCH-INDEX, truncated — VERIFY full §08].
  • No canopy/square-footage, lighting, or plant-count-per-patient rule was located beyond the 1,000-plant / 3,500-ounce ceilings.
  • Pesticides — medical (§§33-44-01-09/10): §09 is titled "Use of Pesticides Prohibited"; §10 is "Pesticide Presence". Secondary summary: "Pesticides are not allowed in medical cannabis cultivation. Pesticides with minimum risk may be allowed only with prior approval from the HHS." — https://northdakotacannabis.org/cass — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY]. The minimum-risk exception and any approved-product list were NOT verified from NDAC text in this run — [VERIFY §§09/10 full text; do not publish an approved-pesticide list].
  • Inventory control (§§33-44-01-19/20): centre must maintain an inventory control system (BioTrackTHC, Run 1); §20 governs conducting inventory (frequency, reconciliation, discrepancy reporting — detail [VERIFY]). Batches must be labelled/stored/secured per §33-44-01-46.

(b) Hemp — plot registration and pre-harvest sampling

  • Plot/lot registration: producers register sites/lots with NDDA and report acreage to FSA (Plan, above). Lot = the unit sampled and tested; material from different lots may not be commingled until allowable THC results issue (Plan). GPS/lot-reporting format, reporting deadline (days after planting), and lot-size rules: [VERIFY — Part 7].
  • Pre-harvest sampling: see §11.1(b): 15 days' notice to NDDA, harvest within 30 days of NDDA sampling; NDDA inspects/samples 10% of lots under performance-based sampling annually, all non-low-risk varieties sampled; research hemp under State Board of Higher Education institutions uses performance-based protocol. Sampler must be NDDA-authorised; federal rule requires sampling by an authorised sampler following USDA protocol (top portion of plant — the "top two inches of ~30 flowering plants per field" figure in secondary guides is pilot-era and is NOT adopted without the current Plan protocol). [VERIFY current Plan sampling protocol in full].
  • Indoor/greenhouse hemp: no ND indoor-only rule for hemp was located; hemp is a field crop under the Plan. Protected-cultivation acreage is reported separately in NASS data (Run 1) but no separate ND indoor-hemp rule was found — [VERIFY if Part 7 claims otherwise].

11.4 Security — medical and hemp

(a) Medical

  • Restricted access areas (§33-44-01-12): centres must maintain restricted access areas; operations manual (§33-44-01-11) must include employee security policies (unauthorised entrance into restricted areas), personal safety/crime prevention, and safety/security procedures including a disaster plan. — NDAC section list + https://ndlegis.gov/files/rule-changes/changes/doh040822changesmm.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].
  • Surveillance (§33-44-01-17): "To prevent unauthorized access to marijuana and usable marijuana, the compassion center shall have video surveillance equip[ment]…" [SEARCH-INDEX, truncated]. Secondary security-industry compilation states ND video retention = 60 days (citing the NDAC PDF) and that recordings in the first year must be kept ≥90 days and ≥60 days thereafter per a secondary county guide — these two retention figures CONFLICT and neither was verified from full §17 text in this run — [VERIFY §17: camera coverage, resolution, retention period, and whether 90/60 or a flat 60 days applies]. Do not publish a retention period as settled.
  • Alarm (§33-44-01-18): alarm system required; detail (monitored, backup power, failure notification) [VERIFY full §18]. Secondary summary claims 24-hour security "even during power outages" with multiple alarms and date/time-stamped cameras — [SECONDARY, unverified against §17/18 text].
  • Storage (§33-44-01-46): manufacturing facility requirements for labelling, storing and securing usable marijuana batches. Dispensary display areas (§33-44-01-13) and dispensary premises (§33-44-01-13.1, added in later amendments) govern what may be displayed/where. Detail [VERIFY].
  • Transport (§§33-44-01-29/30): transport requires department authorisation (§29) and a department-approved manifest system (§30): manifest in the vehicle, provided to law enforcement on request, chain of custody, signed by centre/lab agent on departure and by centre/lab agent, waste-facility employee, patient or caregiver on receipt; receiver verifies/documents type/quantity against manifest; manifests kept ≥7 years and made available to the department. Transported material (except waste already rendered unusable under §15) must be in tamper-evident containers, not visible/recognisable from outside the vehicle, in an unmarked vehicle (no markings/name/logo indicating marijuana), in an enclosed, locked storage compartment secured/affixed to the vehicle. Transporting agents must travel directly to the manifest destination, document refuelling/all stops (reason, duration, location, activities of agents exiting), and contact 911 if an emergency requires stopping; no person other than the designated agent may [be in the vehicle / handle the material — extract truncated]. — https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/north-dakota/N-D-A-C-33-44-01-30 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Patient delivery is NOT expressly addressed in §30 as extracted — see Correction 3.
  • Incident reporting (§22): 24-hour reporting of security breach, surveillance failure/tampering, power failure >2 hours, embezzlement/fraud, 911/law-enforcement contact, transport incidents, attempts to obtain marijuana other than as prescribed, and violations (see §11.1).

(b) Hemp — security

  • No ND hemp-specific security rule (cameras, alarms, fencing, locked storage) was located in ch. 4.1-18.1 or the Plan in this run. Hemp security is governed by general agricultural practice plus the lot/registration and sampling controls; transport of hemp seed must be covered to avoid inadvertent dissemination under pilot-era NDAC 7-14-02-04 (volunteer plants destroyed before seed stage; seed covered during transport) — that chapter's current status is [VERIFY, see §11.2]. Do not import medical-centre security rules into hemp. [UNAVAILABLE for a current hemp security rule].

11.5 Waste disposal

(a) Medical (§33-44-01-15, eff. 1 Apr 2018, amended 1 July 2022)

  • All medical marijuana waste generated during production, processing and testing must be evaluated against state hazardous-waste regulations (NDCC ch. 23-20.3); if designated hazardous waste, it is subject to ch. 23-20.3 standards. Non-hazardous waste must be rendered unusable under §15(4) before disposal: grinding the waste and incorporating it with other ground materials so the resulting mixture is less than 50% medical marijuana waste (method as described in the 2018 rulemaking record — https://ndlegis.gov/files/committees/65-2017/19_5113_03000appendixd.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX]). Rendered-unusable waste may then be (a) delivered to a permitted, state-approved solid waste facility (compostable mixed waste: compost/anaerobic digester/other facility with state/local health approval; non-compostable: landfill/incinerator/other approved facility) or (b) managed on site by the generator under NDCC ch. 23-29 (Solid Waste Management and Land Protection) — the on-site limb appears in the older text and is absent/truncated in the newest extract, so its current status is [VERIFY]. Centre/lab must maintain a record of the final destination for 7 years. — https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX].
  • Cardholder disposal (§33-44-01-04) and take-back (§33-44-01-14): patients/caregivers who are no longer registered, and usable-marijuana take-back, are governed by §§04/14. The 2018 record notes the department's position that marijuana cannot legally go through the (prescription) Take-Back Program because marijuana remains federally illegal, and that §04 imposes disposal standards on patients/caregivers — full §§04/14 text [VERIFY]. Do not advise patients to use a pharmacy take-back for marijuana.
  • Failed-batch disposal: see §11.1(a) — remediate/sterilize or dispose under §52.

(b) Hemp — non-compliant destruction

  • See §11.1(b): remediation allowed only if total THC <1% (flower removal leaving stalk/seed, or shredding flower+leaf+stalk into biomass, then retest below acceptable level); otherwise NDDA-witnessed/verified destruction to render plants/material unusable; federal methods include plowing under, mulching/composting with other biomass, disking, shredding, deep burial ≥12 inches, burning, DEA reverse distributor, or law enforcement (7 CFR 990.27 / AMS disposal guidance — secondary summary at https://legalclarity.org/industrial-hemp-legal-definition-what-federal-law-says/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY for the method list; Plan method list itself truncated — VERIFY]). Volunteer hemp not in a licensed field must be destroyed before seed stage (pilot-era NDAC 7-14-02 — current status VERIFY). Non-exempt plant material under that old chapter had to be exported/sold to a DEA-registered reverse distributor or fully incinerated on site as approved/supervised by the commissioner — do not quote as current without verification.

11.6 Environmental rules

(a) Medical

  • Water: no NDAC 33-44-01 water-use, discharge, or wastewater rule was located beyond the definition that "medical marijuana waste" includes wastewater generated during production/processing (§33-44-01-01). Wastewater disposal therefore falls under the §15 hazardous-waste evaluation and general ND water law — no cannabis-specific water rule found. [UNAVAILABLE for a cannabis-specific water standard].
  • Pesticides: see §11.3(a) — use prohibited (§09), presence governed by §10, compliance testing for pesticides/degradation compounds (§47). No approved-pesticide list located. No fertiliser, nutrient, or soil rule located in NDAC 33-44-01.
  • Energy: no ND cannabis-specific energy, lighting, HVAC, carbon, or energy-reporting rule was located. General building/fire/electrical codes apply as for any industrial facility — [UNAVAILABLE for a cannabis-specific energy standard]. Odour control is a local condition in some jurisdictions (Run 1 Part 3: Williston odor/HVAC plan, Dickinson air-filtration condition), not a statewide NDAC standard located in this run.

(b) Hemp

  • Water / pesticide / energy: no hemp-specific ND water, pesticide, or energy rule was located in ch. 4.1-18.1 or the Plan in this run. Hemp growers remain subject to general ND pesticide law (NDDA pesticide registration/applicator rules, FIFRA labels — hemp is not on most pesticide labels, a national issue, but no ND hemp pesticide list was located) and general water law. Do not state that any pesticide is "approved for hemp in ND" without an NDDA list. [UNAVAILABLE].
  • Environmental upside claimed in secondary sources (phytoremediation, soil health) is agronomic literature, not ND regulation, and is not adopted here.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

(a) Medical

  • Free product — PROHIBITED (NDAC): "A dispensary only may dispense usable marijuana when it has been purchased by a registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver. A dispensary may not provide free usable marijuana to a registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver." — NDAC §33-44-01-23 (current section list, 2025 amendments) — https://ndlegis.gov/sites/default/files/rule-changes/changes/dhhs061225changes.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX]. This is the giveaway rule for usable marijuana itself: no free samples to patients/caregivers.
  • Advertising/marketing (§33-44-01-23, eff. 1 Apr 2018, amended 1 Oct 2019, 1 July 2022): a dispensary may display its business name/logo on labels, signs, websites and informational material provided to patients/caregivers; the name/logo may not include (1) images of marijuana or paraphernalia, (2) colloquial references to marijuana, (3) names of marijuana plant strains, (4) medical symbols reasonably resembling established medical associations (e.g. AMA, AAP). A dispensary may maintain a website containing facility name, contact information, hours, usable marijuana offered, product pricing, and other department-approved information. A manufacturing facility may display its name/logo on labels, websites and informational material. All marketing/advertising activities not covered by those subsections are subject to department approval — centre must request approval, department must approve/deny within 30 calendar days. All marketing/advertising is prohibited from being marketed to a minor. Strain/brand names may not contain words referring to products commonly associated with minors, marketed to minors, or false/misleading names (§33-44-01-24). — https://www.legis.nd.gov/information/acdata/pdf/33-44-01.pdf and https://www.mediajel.com/cannabis-advertising-laws/north-dakota — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SEARCH-INDEX for NDAC text; MediaJel SECONDARY for the 30-day summary — the 30-day rule appears in the secondary summary and must be VERIFIED against full §23 before quoting as law].
  • Discounts / coupons / loyalty / contests: No express NDAC discount, coupon, loyalty-program, sweepstakes or contest rule was located in §23 as extracted. A secondary guide claims a blanket ban on giveaways, discount coupons, incentives, sweepstakes/lotteries/raffles — https://www.foodhandlersguide.com/state-requirements/marijuana-advertising-and-marketing-regulations-in-north-dakota/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY, and its opening claim ("advertising strictly prohibited") contradicts §23's permitted name/logo/website regime, so the guide is NOT reliable]. Position for the combined file: free usable marijuana is banned (§23); price display on a dispensary website is permitted; whether a price discount (as opposed to a free product) requires department approval under the "all other marketing subject to approval" limb is [VERIFY — do not state a discount ban as law]. Sampling for testing (lab samples, §41) is not patient sampling and must not be conflated with consumer free samples.
  • Patient education: dispensaries may sell related supplies and provide educational materials (§19-24.1-32(6)) — this is not a promotion rule.

(b) Hemp

  • No ND hemp-specific sampling/giveaway/discount/promotion rule was located. Hemp/CBD retailers are ordinary retailers for promotion purposes, subject to general advertising law, FDA/FTC constraints on disease claims (federal, not ND), and the state ban on intoxicating products (a "free delta-8 sample" would be a free prohibited product, not a lawful promotion). No ND rule banning CBD discounts or requiring department approval of CBD advertising was found — [UNAVAILABLE for a hemp-specific promotion rule; do not import the medical §23 regime into hemp].

11.8 Drug testing and background checks — licence holders and staff

(a) Medical — agents, caregivers, laboratory agents

  • Who is checked: compassion centre agents, designated caregivers, and laboratory agents are all subject to fingerprint-based criminal history record checks under NDCC §12-60-24 (as amended by SB 2102, 2023, adding HHS checks for "an applicant for registration as a designated caregiver or a compassion center agent under chapter 19-24.1") and §19-24.1-04.1 / §19-24.1-18. — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/68-2023/regular/documents/23-8062-02000.pdf (SB 2102 enrolled) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Agent requirements (§19-24.1-18): agent must be ≥21 years old and submit: photographic copy of department-approved identification (made available for inspection/verification), recent 2"×2" photograph, written/signed statement from a centre officer/executive stating the applicant is associated with the centre and what that association is, name/address/telephone, signature/date, nonrefundable $200 application fee, and consent to a criminal history record check (paid for by the centre or agent). Agent renewal: complete renewal application ≥45 days before card expiration (see §11.2). — https://vangst.com/reports/state-requirements/north-dakota — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY summary of §18 — verify against current §18 text] + https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/68-2023/regular/documents/23-0711-02000.pdf (SB 2201 engrossed, §18 amendments) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Disqualifying history (agents and caregivers — same test): conviction of a drug-related misdemeanour within the 5 years before application, or a felony offence ever (lifetime), yields a failed check and the person is prohibited from serving as agent/caregiver. Caregiver instructions (HHS, Mar 2026): check on initial application and every other year (biennially) thereafter, and at any other time on department request; HHS mails a fingerprint packet after application; fingerprinting cost is the applicant's (choice of fingerprinting provider), while the state/federal check fee itself is covered by the Medical Marijuana Program; a failed caregiver may not serve; knowingly submitting false records/documentation = permanent disqualification; material misstatement may result in suspension/revocation. — https://www.hhs.nd.gov/sites/default/files/documents/DOH%20Legacy/MM/Designated-Caregiver-Application-Instructions.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. SB 2201 (2023) shifted check fees from applicant to department for caregivers in one engrossment — the current fee-payer split (fingerprinting vs. check fee) above follows the HHS instructions, not the bill draft; [VERIFY current §19-24.1-04.1 fee language].
  • Employee drug testing (general ND law): North Dakota is an "open" drug-testing state with no statute restricting employer drug/alcohol testing (no ND drug-testing compliance law specifying substances, labs, or procedures for private employers); safety-sensitive (e.g. ND DOT) positions typically require testing. This is general employment law, NOT a compassion-centre rule — compassion centres are not exempt from it, and patients have no testing protection (see §19-24.1-34, Run 1). — https://www.health-street.net/state-laws/north-dakota-drug-testing-compliance/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY — verify against NDCC ch. 34-01 / DOT rules before quoting as law].
  • Laboratory agents: lab agent cards under §33-44-01-38; staff must pass criminal background checks as a 2025 procurement qualification (see §11.1). Detail [VERIFY §38].

(b) Hemp — background checks

  • Hemp licence applicants are subject to the federal/state background regime in the Plan / 7 CFR 990 (10-year felony bar for drug felonies under federal hemp law — see Part 7 for the full rule; this run did not re-extract the ND Plan's background-check section). NDDA hemp page fee list in Run 1 included a $40 background-check item (vs. $40.00 secondary) — that fee conflict is carried from Run 1, not resolved here. No hemp employee drug-testing rule was located. [VERIFY — Part 7].

11.9 Research and university work

  • NDSU — hemp (extensive, confirmed): NDSU is ND's land-grant hemp research institution by statute and history. 1998: NDSU researchers issued "Industrial Hemp as an Alternative Crop in North Dakota"; 1999 legislation (HB 1428, recognising hemp as an oilseed at ≤0.3% THC; SB 2328, authorising NDSU baseline research incl. production/processing with research/extension centres, NDCC 4-05.1) and HB 1492 (authorising NDSU to collect feral hemp seed stock and develop <0.3% THC strains) — but federal authority then blocked commercial production and DEA never acted on NDSU's application for 8 years (NDSU filed a court brief supporting producers Hauge/Monson). — http://votehemp.com/PDF/industrialhempjune2005.pdf (prepared remarks of Agriculture Commissioner Roger Johnson, 23 June 2005) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [HISTORICAL secondary source for the legislative history — verify bill numbers against ndlegis.gov session laws before quoting as statute history].
  • NDSU — modern hemp trials: 2015 NDSU Langdon Research Extension Center hemp performance trials (cultivars Alyssa, Canda, CFX-1/2, CRS-1, FINOLA, Fedora 17, Felina 32, Ferimon, Futura 75; grain/fiber yield, stand, growth rate) — https://www.ag.ndsu.edu/smallgrainS/variety-trials/langdon-rec/2015-trial-results/lindustrialhemp2016.pdf/at_download/file — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. 2021–2022 NDSU Prosper off-station trials (with National Crop Insurance Services) on stem-injury effects on hemp grain/biomass yield, cultivars Katani/Canda — https://osu-wams-blogs-uploads.s3.amazonaws.com/blogs.dir/6672/files/2023/03/2023-AAIC-abstract-format.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. USDA-ARS + NDSU hempseed-cake cattle-feeding study (CBD/THC residues in muscle/liver/kidney/fat; ~10 ppb combined CBD+THC in adipose, below detection in liver/kidney/muscle; FDA-CVM approval still required for animal-feed use) — https://www.food-safety.com/articles/print/8512-usda-scientists-determine-that-feeding-hempseed-cakes-to-cattle-does-not-pose-food-safety-risk — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY reporting of the ARS/NDSU study].
  • NDSU — medical marijuana research: No NDSU (or other ND university) medical-marijuana research programme, research licence, or clinical trial was located. The only ND research carve-out located is the §19-24.1-24 allowance of 50 additional plants for department-authorised R&D at a manufacturing facility (see §11.3) — that is industry R&D, not university research. There is no standalone research licence in ch. 19-24.1 (Run 1). [UNAVAILABLE for a university medical-cannabis research programme — do not invent one].
  • Hemp research under the Plan: research hemp by an ND institution under State Board of Higher Education control uses a performance-based sampling protocol (Plan, §11.1(b)). Whether NDSU holds a current NDDA research licence vs. operating under the Plan's research protocol was not distinguished in sources opened — [VERIFY — Part 7].
  • Federal note: only DEA-registered research institutions may keep hemp testing over 0.3% until study end (7 CFR 990) — https://www.ecfr.gov/api/renderer/v1/content/enhanced/2024-04-23/title-7?subtitle=B&chapter=IX&part=990 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. No ND institution's DEA registration was located in this run — [VERIFY if claimed].

Part 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke shops

  • State paraphernalia law (NDCC ch. 19-03.4, §19-03.4-03): Run 1 tiers adopted — marijuana production paraphernalia = Class A misdemeanour; marijuana personal-use/storage paraphernalia = infraction (Run 1 §1.3). A city-code mirror confirms the personal-use limb: Grand Forks City Code §9-0130 (as amended) makes it unlawful "to use or possess drug paraphernalia with the intent to ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body marijuana or tetrahydrocannabinol in violation of [ch.] 19-03.1" — https://www.grandforksgov.com/home/showpublisheddocument/41062/638629571682530000 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. No separate "smoke shop licence" exists in ND law (Run 1 Part 3): a smoke/vape shop is an ordinary retailer, plus a state tobacco licence if it sells tobacco/electronic smoking devices/alternative nicotine products (NDCC ch. 57-36; AG tobacco licensing: dealer/retail application $15, distributor/manufacturer/wholesaler $25 + $1,000 surety bond for new applications, licences expire 30 June each year — Run 1 Part 3, AG page). Local tobacco-retailer rules (Minot $200/yr, Devils Lake Ord. 1040 (2026) ch. 9.74) are in Run 1 Part 3 and are not repeated here.
  • What a smoke shop may NOT sell as "paraphernalia": paraphernalia intended for unlawful marijuana use remains unlawful even if sold in a shop; hemp-derived CBD hardware sold for lawful hemp use is not per se paraphernalia — but no ND case or AG opinion distinguishing "dual-use" pipes/bongs by intended use was located in this run. [VERIFY — do not advise a shop on dual-use inventory on this report alone].
  • Tobacco/vaping age (applies to smoke-shop sales of tobacco/e-cigarettes, NOT to CBD as such): NDCC §12.1-31-03: it is an infraction to sell/furnish to a person under 21 cigarettes, cigarette papers, cigars, snuff, tobacco in any smoking/chewing form, electronic smoking devices, or alternative nicotine products; it is a noncriminal offence for a person 18–<21, and an infraction for a person 14–<18, to purchase/possess/smoke/use those products (compliance-survey exception with parental permission + law-enforcement supervision). — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-12-1/chapter-12-1-31/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. The 2025 HB 1628 engrossment would add kratom to §12.1-31-03 — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/69-2025/special-2/documents/25-1453-05000.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [PROPOSED/ENROLLED — status to be confirmed on ndlegis.gov; do not present kratom as already in §12.1-31-03 without that check].
  • Smoke-free law (NDCC ch. 23-12): "smoking" as amended includes vaping (electronic smoking device defined at §23-12-09(3) as an electronic oral device providing a vapour of nicotine or any other substance simulating smoking); smoking/vaping is prohibited in virtually all enclosed public places and places of employment. — https://ecigator.com/regulation/north-dakota-vaping-laws-guide/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY summary of ch. 23-12 — verify against NDCC text before quoting]. This is the general public-smoking ban that also covers medical marijuana smoking in enclosed public places (see §12.5).

12.2 Minimum age for hemp-derived products

  • Headline: NO ND statute located sets a minimum age for lawful, non-intoxicating hemp/CBD products. Secondary sources conflict (18+ vs. 21+ vs. "no age specified, retailers voluntarily 18/21"): one secondary guide states "North Dakota law does not specify a minimum age for purchasing hemp-derived CBD products, though many retailers voluntarily implement an age restriction, typically 18 or 21" — https://legalclarity.org/is-cbd-legal-in-north-dakota-state-laws-explained/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY]. Other secondary guides assert 18+ or 21+ without citing a statute — NOT adopted. Position for the combined file: no hemp-CBD age rule in NDCC ch. 4.1-18.1 located; retailer 18+/21+ policies are private policies, not law.
  • What DOES have an age rule and must not be conflated with CBD:
  • Medical marijuana: patients may be minors (under 19) only for pediatric product (≤6% THC, no flower — Run 1); designated caregivers ≥21 (SB 2293, Run 1); compassion centre agents ≥21 (§19-24.1-18).
  • Tobacco/electronic smoking devices/alternative nicotine: 21 to sell to, 18–21 noncriminal / 14–18 infraction to purchase/possess/use (§12.1-31-03, above). A CBD vape sold as an electronic smoking device therefore falls under the 21-sale rule for the device even if the CBD liquid itself has no ND age rule — that interaction is [VERIFY — no ND authority located stating whether a hemp-CBD vape is an "electronic smoking device" under §12.1-31-03 vs. a hemp product under ch. 4.1-18.1].
  • Intoxicating hemp products (delta-8 etc.): prohibited at any age (Run 1) — there is no "21+ delta-8" rule in ND because there is no lawful delta-8 sale to age-gate. Secondary tables listing ND delta-8 as "21+" are wrong for ND.
  • Hemp-extract inhalants (including CBD vapes as inhalants): prohibited under the NDDA position reported in Run 1 ("If the answer is to produce a high… not allowed" / inhalant ban) — the exact statutory hook for a non-intoxicating CBD vape inhalant ban vs. the tobacco-device rule above is [VERIFY — Run 1 flagged the inhalant ban; this run did not locate its full statutory text].

12.3 THC driving limits and impairment testing

  • NO per se THC limit in ND law. NDCC §39-08-01(1)(a) prohibits driving/actual physical control if: (1) alcohol concentration ≥0.08% by weight at a chemical test within 2 hours; (2) under the influence of intoxicating liquor; (3) under the influence of any drug/substance/combination "to a degree which renders that person incapable of safely driving"; (4) combined alcohol + drugs to that degree; (5) refusal of a chemical test as specified. There is no THC nanogram threshold in §39-08-01 as extracted — the 0.08% figure is alcohol only. — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-39/chapter-39-08/ and https://codes.findlaw.com/nd/title-39-motor-vehicles/nd-cent-code-sect-39-08-01/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Secondary no per se THC limit [that threshold figure is removed — it is not ND law] claim REJECTED: a secondary site claims "a THC concentration of 5 nanograms or more per milliliter of blood is considered to be under the influence" in ND — https://NorthDakotaCannabis.org/consequences-having-mmj-card — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY, no statute cited, contradicts the §39-08-01 text above]. Do NOT publish no per se THC limit [that threshold figure is removed — it is not ND law] as ND law. A 2021 legislative witness made the same point in reverse: in §39-08-01(1)(c) "there is no listed level of any drug, THC or otherwise, that could quantify a person as legally impaired" — https://www.ndlegis.gov/assembly/67-2021/testimony/SHUMSER-1420-20210315-9024-A-GRINDBERG_HUGH.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [LEGISLATIVE TESTIMONY, not law — cited only to show the absence of a threshold was recognised in the legislature].
  • Medical-patient protection / non-protection in driving: §39-08-01(1)(b): "The fact any person charged… is or has been legally entitled to use alcohol or other drugs or substances is not a defense" — BUT it is an affirmative defence that a drug was used only as directed/cautioned by a practitioner who legally prescribed/dispensed it. Separately, §19-24.1-33(5): a registered qualifying patient may not be considered under the influence solely because of metabolites/components appearing in insufficient concentration to cause impairment — quoted verbatim in Run 1 materials via FindLaw: "However, a registered qualifying patient may not be considered to be under the influence of marijuana solely because of the presence of metabolites or components of marijuana that appear in insufficient concentration to cause impairment." — https://codes.findlaw.com/nd/title-19-foods-drugs-oils-and-compounds/nd-cent-code-sect-19-24-1-33/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. In practice: impairment (incapable of safely driving) must be proved; mere metabolite presence is not enough for a patient — but legal entitlement alone is not a defence to an impairment-based charge, and the practitioner-direction affirmative defence turns on "prescribed/dispensed" language that sits uneasily with ND's certification (not prescription) model — [VERIFY how courts have applied §39-08-01(1)(b) to certified (not prescribed) medical marijuana; no case located in this run].
  • Testing / implied consent: chemical tests of blood/breath/urine under §39-20-01 (and §39-06.2-10.2 for commercial vehicles) at law-enforcement direction; refusal is itself an offence under §39-08-01(2) (subject to advisement of consequences consistent with US/ND constitutions). No ND roadside THC breathalyser standard, DRE protocol, or oral-fluid threshold was located in statute in this run — impairment is proved by officer observation, field sobriety, DRE evaluation, and chemical testing for presence, not by a per se number. [VERIFY ND Highway Patrol DRE programme details if the combined file wants an enforcement section].
  • Penalties (driving): first/second DUI in the relevant period = Class B misdemeanour in the older §39-08-01 text; current penalty tiers, lookback periods (5-year/7-year), licence suspension, ignition-interlock, and plate-destruction provisions have been amended repeatedly and were NOT fully extracted — [VERIFY current §39-08-01(2)–(5) before quoting DUI penalties]. §39-08-01.4: driving under the influence while accompanied by a minor (driver ≥21) = Class A misdemeanour; prior §39-08-01.4 conviction = Class C felony. — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-39/chapter-39-08/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Commercial driving: secondary claim that medical cardholders are "prohibited from applying for/obtaining CDLs or operating commercial vehicles in ND" — https://NorthDakotaCannabis.org/consequences-having-mmj-card — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY, no statute cited]. Federal DOT drug-testing (49 CFR Part 40) treats marijuana use as a violation regardless of state medical status, which is the likely source of the claim — but no ND statute barring CDL application by cardholders was located. [VERIFY — present as federal DOT consequence, not as an ND CDL statute, unless an ND statute is located].
  • Schedule III note (analysis): under the given baseline, state-licensed medical use moves to Schedule III federally, but §39-08-01 is an impairment statute, not a scheduling statute — Schedule III does not create a driving defence and does not change the "incapable of safely driving" test. No ND amendment to §39-08-01 for Schedule III was located.

12.4 Expungement and record clearing for marijuana offences

North Dakota has NO general "expungement" (destruction) regime. It has (i) a narrow marijuana-specific sealing rule, (ii) a general sealing petition regime, and (iii) automatic closing of nonconviction records. Conflating these three is the most common secondary-source error — the combined file must keep them separate.

  • (i) Marijuana-specific sealing — NDCC §19-03.1-23(9) [subsection numbering varies by edition — (9) in FindLaw current text, (10) in National Reentry Resource Center summaries; VERIFY current subsection number on ndlegis.gov before citing a number]: "If a person pleads guilty or is found guilty of a first offense regarding possession of one ounce [28.35 grams] or less of marijuana or two grams or less of tetrahydrocannabinol and a judgment of guilt is entered, a court, upon motion, shall seal the court record of that conviction if the person is not subsequently convicted within two years of a further violation of this chapter. Once sealed, the court record may not be opened even by order of the court." — https://codes.findlaw.com/nd/title-19-foods-drugs-oils-and-compounds/nd-cent-code-sect-19-03-1-23/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Notes: petition-based (motion), not automatic; first offence only; ≤1 oz marijuana / ≤2 g THC only; 2-year clean period measured by further violation of ch. 19-03.1 (the older 2007 text added "and has not been convicted of any other criminal offense" — whether that broader bar survives in current text is [VERIFY]); sealing here is stronger than ch. 12-60.1 sealing ("may not be opened even by order of the court"). No fee is stated in §19-03.1-23(9) as extracted — any filing-fee figure is [VERIFY — do not import the $100–$300 secondary estimate].
  • (ii) General sealing — NDCC ch. 12-60.1 (enacted 2019): petition filed in the existing criminal case, served on the prosecuting official (Rule 49, ND Rules of Criminal Procedure); hearing no earlier than 45 days after filing (secondary summary — verify); court must find by clear and convincing evidence: good cause, completed imprisonment/probation, paid restitution, demonstrated reformation, and benefit outweighing public interest in openness. Grounds (§12-60.1-02(1)): (a) misdemeanour + no new conviction for ≥3 years before filing; (b) felony + no new conviction for ≥5 years before filing; (c) unconditional gubernatorial pardon. Exclusions (§12-60.1-02(2), except §12-60.1-05): felony involving violence/intimidation during the firearm-ineligibility period; offences requiring sex-offender registration; DUI records cannot be sealed under ch. 12-60.1 (ND Courts research guide). Effect: sealed ≠ destroyed — record is inaccessible to the public/prosecution/court disclosure on standard checks but remains accessible to the court, criminal-justice agencies, defendant/counsel, state's attorney and others named in §12-60.1-01; it is NOT deleted. — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-12/chapter-12-60-1/ and https://www.ndcourts.gov/Media/Default/legal-resources/legal-self-help/other-forms/Sealing%20Criminal%20Records%20Research%20Guide.pdf (ND Legal Self-Help Center guide, rev. 27 Aug 2025) and https://ndlegis.gov/prod/cencode/t12c60-1.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • (iii) Nonconviction closing — NDCC §12-60.1-05 (HB 1166, 2025, eff. 1 Aug 2025): if a court enters an order of nonconviction on/after 1 Aug 2025, the court shall close the court record upon expiration of 61 days (automatic); for nonconviction orders before 1 Aug 2025, the defendant may petition to close. — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-0400-06000.pdf (HB 1166 enrolled) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. This is the ONLY automatic track located; there is no automatic sealing/expungement of marijuana convictions in ND.
  • Related tracks (do not merge): misdemeanour case dismissed after successful drug-court completion is sealed immediately (§§19-03.1-23(11), 39-08-01.5(3) — per National Reentry Resource Center); juvenile delinquency records, human-trafficking-victim records, DNA records on reversed/dismissed convictions (§31-13-07), and unconstitutional-arrest records have separate destruction/expungement routes — detail [VERIFY if the combined file lists them]. Pardon: separate gubernatorial pardon (Pardon Advisory Board) — unconditional pardon is a ground for ch. 12-60.1 sealing, not itself an expungement.
  • Secondary error to avoid: guides stating "North Dakota does not have a statutory process for sealing/expunging" are outdated (pre-2019) and are contradicted by ch. 12-60.1 and §19-03.1-23(9); guides stating "expungement after 2 years for <1 oz" without the first-offence / motion / sealing-vs-destruction qualifications overstate §19-03.1-23(9).

12.5 Consumption rules

  • Statutory limitations (§19-24.1-33): ch. 19-24.1 "does not authorize a person to engage in, and does not prevent civil/criminal liability or other penalties for" — verbatim substance (FindLaw, last updated 1 Jan 2024):
  • Undertaking an activity under the influence if it would constitute negligence/professional malpractice.
  • Possessing/consuming usable marijuana: (a) on a school bus/van used for school purposes; (b) on the grounds of any public or private school; (c) at any location while a public/private school-sanctioned event is occurring there; (d) on the grounds of a correctional facility; (e) on the grounds of a child care facility or licensed home day care, unless authorised under department rules.
  • [Subsection 3 — smoking/vaping in a vehicle / public place — was NOT in the FindLaw extract as returned; the numbering jumps from 2 to 4. The combined file must NOT invent a subsection 3. See below for what IS established on public/vehicle consumption from other sources.]
