  • Using a combustible delivery form or vaporising if the smoke/vapour would be inhaled by a minor who is not the registered qualifying patient for whom it is intended.
  • Operating/navigating/being in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft, train or motorboat while under the influence (with the metabolite-insufficiency proviso in §12.3 above).

— https://codes.findlaw.com/nd/title-19-foods-drugs-oils-and-compounds/nd-cent-code-sect-19-24-1-33/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Full §19-24.1-33 text [VERIFY on ndlegis.gov] — especially whether a public-consumption or vehicle-consumption subsection exists that the extract omitted.

  • Public smoking — what can be stated safely: (a) ch. 23-12 smoke-free law bans smoking/vaping in enclosed public places/employment (see §12.1); (b) §19-24.1-33 bans possession/consumption on school/correctional/child-care grounds and at school events, and bans combustible/vapour exposure of a non-patient minor; (c) secondary guides state patients may smoke only at a private residence and not in public spaces/vehicles/public transport — https://NorthDakotaCannabis.org/smoke-weed-in-public — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY]. A freestanding ND "no public consumption of medical marijuana anywhere" statute was NOT located in this run beyond (a)+(b) — the combined file should state (a)+(b) as law and the broader "private residence only" claim as [SECONDARY/VERIFY], not as a quoted statute.
  • Vehicles: consumption while driving/actual physical control while under the influence is banned (§19-24.1-33(5) + §39-08-01). Passenger consumption, open-container-style transport of usable marijuana by a patient, and smoking in a parked vehicle were NOT located as express rules — [VERIFY]. Patient transport of purchased usable marijuana home from a dispensary is necessarily contemplated by the programme (purchase at dispensary, possession at home) but the transport conditions for patients (container, vehicle location) were not located — [VERIFY — do not import the centre manifest rules in §11.4 into patient transport].
  • Workplaces: §19-24.1-34 preserves employer discipline for workplace possession/consumption, working under the influence, and working with marijuana in the employee's system (Run 1). No patient right to consume at work exists.
  • Federal property / housing: federal law (Schedule III medical baseline does not change federal property rules) — consumption on federal property remains federally unlawful; no ND rule overrides that. [General federal position — verify against 41 CFR / agency policy if the combined file details federal sites].

12.6 Visitor and out-of-state patient rules

  • Pre-SB 2294 position (Run 1): ND did NOT recognise other states' cards; patients/caregivers had to hold an ND card and buy from ND dispensaries. — HHS League of Cities presentation (2018): "Patients and Caregivers must have a ND registry identification card. (We do not recognize other state's cards.)" — https://slidetodoc.com/north-dakota-department-of-health-division-of-medical/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [HISTORICAL HHS presentation].
  • SB 2294 (2025, eff. 1 Aug 2025) — nonresident pathway (RESOLVES Run 1 Verify in part): new NDCC §19-24.1-03.1 (Qualifying patients — Nonresidents): "In lieu of the written certification required under section 19-24.1-03, a nonresident who holds a valid out-of-state medical marijuana card issued by the state in which the nonresident resides, may submit to the department a copy of the nonresident's out-of-state department-approved identification and a copy of an out-of-state medical marijuana card. The department-approved identification and out-of-state medical marijuana card must be issued by the same [state]… The department may use the out-of-state department-approved identification and out-of-state medical marijuana card in place of a written certification to approve or deny the application under section 19-24.1-[03]." — https://ndlegis.gov/prod/assembly/69-2025/regular/documents/25-1233-01001m.pdf (SB 2294, Human Services committee amendments, 29 Jan 2025) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [COMMITTEE VERSION — enrolled text must be VERIFIED on ndlegis.gov; the fiscal note confirms the substance: "Section 7 allows a nonresident to obtain a qualifying patient registry identification card" — https://legis.nd.gov/assembly/69-2025/regular/fiscal-notes/25-1233-04000-fn.pdf (14 Apr 2025) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026].
  • Nonresident card terms (as reported): electronic card, valid 60 days, $40 application fee (NDAC §33-44-01-03.2 as amended 1 Oct 2025: resident $40, nonresident $40, agent $200 — https://ndlegis.gov/sites/default/files/rule-changes/changes/dhhs061225changes.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026). Secondary summary: "This law also established an electronic medical marijuana card for non-residents that is valid for 60 days" — https://northdakotacannabis.org/laws — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY — the 60-day term itself is VERIFY against enrolled §19-24.1-03.1 / NDAC §33-44-01-05]. Reciprocity is therefore NOT "ND accepts your home-state card at the dispensary" — the visitor must first obtain an ND nonresident card using the home-state card + ID as a substitute for ND certification. Whether a nonresident cardholder is subject to the same purchase/possession limits, and whether a nonresident designated caregiver exists, is [VERIFY].
  • What a visitor may NOT do: bring marijuana across state lines into ND (federal + ND law — marijuana cannot cross state lines, HHS presentation above); buy from an ND dispensary without an ND (resident or nonresident) card; use a home-state card alone at an ND dispensary. A Minnesota/Montana adult-use purchase brought into ND remains unlawful in ND (non-patient possession tiers, Run 1) even if lawfully bought next door — see Part 10 for interstate detail, not repeated here.
  • ND residents travelling out: ND card has no force outside ND; Minnesota/Montana recognition of ND cards is a matter of those states' law (Part 10) — [VERIFY there].

12.7 Landlord and tenant rules

  • Headline: NO ND statute located creates a medical-marijuana landlord/tenant rule. NDCC ch. 47-16 (Landlord and Tenant) contains no medical-marijuana section located in this run. In particular:
  • No ND "landlord may not discriminate against a patient" statute located, and no ND "landlord may prohibit medical use" statute located. The secondary claim that "landlords cannot discriminate… and must make reasonable accommodations" (StateRegsToday) cites no ND statute and is NOT adopted — https://www.stateregstoday.com/health/marijuana-legalization/medical-marijuana-laws-in-north-dakota — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY, uncited]. The opposite secondary claim ("landlords have the right to prohibit… and evict for violation") is equally uncited to ND law.
  • What IS established: (a) §19-24.1-32(1) protects a patient from "denial of any right or privilege, including a civil penalty or disciplinary action by a court or occupational or professional regulating entity" for state-legal acquisition/use/possession — whether that phrase reaches private landlord decisions has NOT been litigated in any ND case located — [VERIFY — do not present §32 as a housing anti-discrimination rule]. (b) A 2026 secondary review of ND law found "none of the six patient protections" including housing — https://drugpolicywatch.info/states/north-dakota.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY review — consistent with the no-statute finding above]. (c) General lease law applies: a lease's smoking prohibition, if drafted to cover marijuana/smoking generally, is enforceable as a contract term, and breach may support eviction under ch. 47-16 procedures — but that is general contract/lease law, not a cannabis rule, and any template addendum (e.g. "medical marijuana use shall constitute smoking in a multi-unit residence") is a private form, not ND law — https://legaltemplates.net/wp-content/uploads/forms/no-smoking-addendum-lease.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [FORM, not law — do not cite as ND law].
  • Smoking in rentals: ch. 23-12 smoke-free law governs enclosed public places/employment, not private residences as such; a landlord's smoke-free building policy is a lease/policy matter (see above). Cultivation in rentals is moot for patients (home grow prohibited, Run 1) — a tenant cannot lawfully grow medical marijuana in a rental in any event.
  • Public housing / federally assisted housing: federal law (Quality Housing and Work Responsibility Act / HUD policy) permits/required PHAs to deny/terminate for illegal drug use, and marijuana remains federally unlawful for non-medical use and, for Schedule III analysis, federally controlled — ND has no statute overriding federal housing rules. [General federal position — VERIFY current HUD guidance if the combined file details public housing; do not state an ND public-housing rule].
  • Nursing homes / care facilities (contrast — this protection DOES exist in §19-24.1-32): MPP summary notes nursing homes/similar facilities "may not unreasonably limit a registered qualifying patient's medical use of marijuana… unless failing to do so would cause the facility to lose a monetary or licensing-related benefit under federal law or regulations" — https://www.mpp.org/issues/medical-marijuana/medical-marijuana-laws-anti-discrimination-provisions/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY summary of §19-24.1-32 — VERIFY the facility subsection text]. This facility rule is NOT a landlord/tenant rule and must not be presented as one.

12.8 Gun ownership conflicts for patients

  • Federal rule (controls the conflict): 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) prohibits a person who is an "unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance" from shipping, transporting, receiving or possessing firearms/ammunition. ATF (2011 open letter to federal firearms licensees, still cited by ATF) states marijuana users are prohibited regardless of whether the state has passed legislation authorising medicinal use. ATF Form 4473 (current version, Question 21.f) asks whether the purchaser is "an unlawful user of, or addicted to, marijuana or any depressant, stimulant, narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance" — with the warning that marijuana use/possession remains unlawful under federal law regardless of state medicinal legalisation; a truthful "yes" bars the sale, and a false "no" is a separate federal felony. — https://legalclarity.org/can-you-carry-a-gun-with-a-medical-marijuana-card/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY summary of 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3), ATF letter, Form 4473 — verify against ATF's current form/letter before quoting question numbers] + https://www.roundupweb.com/story/2021/01/13/opinion/gun-owners-beware-marijuana-use-and-gun-ownership-do-not-go-together/14869.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY, quoting former Question 21(e)].
  • North Dakota law: No ND statute located restricts a medical cardholder from purchasing/owning a firearm as a matter of ND law, and no ND statute protects a cardholder's gun rights against the federal rule. Secondary ND guides agree: "While North Dakota has no specific law restricting medical marijuana cardholders from purchasing guns, medical marijuana users will not be able to purchase firearms due to federal restrictions." — https://northdakotacannabis.org/mmj-gun-ownership — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY]. ND's own §19-24.1-32 materials note disclosure of firearm possession by a marijuana possessor "may be a violation of federal law" (per that guide's summary — VERIFY the §32 firearm sentence in current text).
  • Schedule III wrinkle (analysis, NOT settled law): under the given baseline, state-licensed medical marijuana moves to Schedule III federally. §922(g)(3) turns on "unlawful user of… any controlled substance" — Schedule III substances are still controlled substances, and medical use under state law is still not federally lawful use (no federal prescription for marijuana). Whether Schedule III changes the §922(g)(3) analysis for state-licensed medical patients is [VERIFY — no ATF guidance, regulation, or court decision on the Schedule III medical carve-out and §922(g)(3) was located in this run]. Do NOT state that Schedule III restores gun rights. Note also the shifting litigation landscape flagged by secondary sources (federal appeals courts limiting §922(g)(3) on Second Amendment grounds; Supreme Court argument in early 2026) — https://legalclarity.org/can-you-carry-a-gun-with-a-medical-marijuana-card/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [SECONDARY — verify current case status before publishing any litigation claim].
  • ND location rules (separate from patient status): NDCC §62.1-02-05 generally prohibits knowing possession of a firearm/dangerous weapon at a "public gathering" (athletic/sporting event, school, church/place of worship, publicly owned/operated building), Class B misdemeanour, with permit/leader-approval exceptions — https://ndlegis.gov/assembly/66-2019/session-laws/documents/WEPNS.pdf (SB 2034, 2019) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. This applies to everyone, not only patients, and is not a patient-gun rule.
  • Practical bottom line for the combined file: ND patient + federal gun purchase = conflict; ND law does not resolve it in the patient's favour; a patient who answers Form 4473 truthfully will be denied by a licensed dealer; a patient who already owns firearms and uses medical marijuana faces federal possession exposure under §922(g)(3) as written, subject to the unresolved Schedule III / Second Amendment litigation notes above.

12.9 Pet and veterinary products

  • No ND veterinary cannabis rule located. NDCC ch. 43-29 (Veterinarians) contains no cannabis/CBD provision located in this run — https://law.justia.com/codes/north-dakota/title-43/chapter-43-29/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. A veterinarian's authority turns on the veterinarian-client-patient relationship (in-person examination) and general drug law; no ND rule authorises a veterinarian to prescribe/recommend marijuana, and marijuana-derived CBD for animals is not a lawful veterinary drug in ND (it would be marijuana under ch. 19-03.1 except for registered human patients).
  • Hemp CBD pet products — retail vs. feed: hemp-derived CBD pet treats/oils sold as pet products are sold in ND retail channels (secondary guides assert legality — NOT adopted as law). What IS regulated: pet food / specialty pet food must be registered with NDDA before distribution in ND under NDCC ch. 4.1-41 (pet foods = feeds for dogs/cats; specialty pet foods = caged/tank pets; treats and non-complete-diet products included; feed manufacturer licence $120 — NDDA Aug 2025 guidelines). — https://www.ndda.nd.gov/sites/www/files/documents/files/NDDACommFeedPetFoodRegLicGuidelines_Aug2025.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Whether a CBD pet treat must be registered as pet food, and whether NDDA accepts CBD as a pet-food ingredient, was NOT located — [VERIFY with NDDA Pet Food Program].
  • Animal feed — hemp: hempseed cake/meal as livestock feed is NOT federally approved (FDA-CVM approval pending; ARS/NDSU residue study above is research, not approval). NDDA APUC grant (2020, Healthy Oilseeds / Hemp Feed Coalition, Carrington) funded groundwork for FDA-CVM approval (nutritional/contaminant analysis for hemp seed cake/meal, hulls, screenings, post-extraction pulp, sediment, sub-human-grade seed oil) — https://hempfeedcoalition.org/2020/08/13/awarding-of-north-dakota-department-of-ag-apuc-grant-moves-the-approval-of-hemp-as-animal-feed-forward-in-a-big-way/ (13 Aug 2020) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Position: hemp as animal feed is not an approved ND use on the evidence located; do not present hempseed cake feeding as lawful commercial feed.
  • Intoxicating pet products: any THC product marketed for pets that is intoxicating hemp (delta-8 etc.) is prohibited as a hemp product (Run 1); medical marijuana may not be given to an animal under a human patient's card (card is personal; animal is not a registered qualifying patient). [No express "giving medical marijuana to a pet" statute located — stated as a consequence of the personal-card scheme, VERIFY if the combined file needs a cited prohibition].

12.10 Medical marijuana in schools

  • State statute — possession/use on school grounds is NOT authorised (§19-24.1-33(2), above): possessing/consuming usable marijuana on school grounds, on a school bus/van, at a school-sanctioned event location, on correctional grounds, or on child-care/home-day-care grounds (unless department rules authorise) is outside ch. 19-24.1 protection. There is NO ND "school administration of medical marijuana" statute (contrast states with Ashley's Law-type statutes) — no ND law requires or authorises a school to administer, store, or permit a student to self-administer medical marijuana on school property.
  • No DPI statewide guidance located: no ND Department of Public Instruction medical-marijuana guidance document was located in this run. DPI's role in the school context is limited to (a) the "Approved Schools" lists HHS uses to interpret "school" for the 1,000 ft buffer (Run 1 Part 3) and (b) the general medication programme statute NDCC §15.1-19-23 (district/nonpublic school may establish a medication programme with trained individuals + parental consent; staff may opt out of providing medication; IEP/504 plans cannot be opted out of) — https://slidetodoc.com/medication-administration-a-guide-for-training-unlicensed-school-2/ (ND Dept. of Health / DPI / Board of Nursing medication-administration guide, Feb 2016) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 [HISTORICAL guide — §15.1-19-23 current text VERIFY]. §15.1-19-23 does NOT mention marijuana and must not be presented as a medical-marijuana school-administration law.
  • District policy — uniform model policy ACBF (the operative school rule in practice): multiple ND districts have adopted the same model "ACBF — Medical Marijuana" policy (West Fargo Public Schools ACBF-AP, East Central Special Education Unit ACBF (19 Nov 2025), McKenzie County PSD #1 ACBF, Jamestown PSD ACBF), all in substance: "Although possession and use of marijuana for certain debilitating medical conditions is permitted in North Dakota, [the District/Board] recognizes that the possession and use of marijuana remains illegal under federal law. In an effort to maintain a drug-free workplace and compliance with state and federal laws, the [District/Board] prohibits the possession, administration, and use of medical marijuana while on school property." Administration of all other prescription/nonprescription medications follows the district medication policy (ACBD). The policy also provides: district shall not refuse to enroll or discriminate against a student solely on the basis of qualifying-patient status/use under ND law, and shall not refuse to hire / discharge / penalise an employee solely on cardholder/patient/caregiver status — BUT the district retains the ability to prohibit possession/use during work hours and to discipline an employee/student for being under the influence during work hours / on school property; cardholder status is confidential and may not be requested/required by the district; and "the protections provided to qualifying patients… do not apply if the qualifying patient uses or possesses marijuana while on school property." — https://www.west-fargo.k12.nd.us/about-us/administrative-policy/acbf-ap-medical-marijuana and https://www.ecsped.k12.nd.us/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/ACBF-Medical-Marijuana-11.19.25.pdf and https://resources.finalsite.net/images/v1779450679/jamestownk12ndus/z3sqfhvlfzoao/ACBF_MedicalMarijuana.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. These are district policies, not state law — the combined file must label them as such and must not present one district's ACBF as a statewide DPI rule. Whether every ND district has adopted ACBF is [VERIFY — do not claim statewide adoption].
  • Minors in schools — product limits (Run 1, restated): a minor (under 19) patient's usable marijuana is limited to pediatric medical marijuana (CBD product, ≤6% THC, no flower). That limit does not create a school-administration right.
  • Employees in schools: same ACBF position — no possession/use on school property or during work hours; discipline for under-the-influence at work preserved (consistent with §19-24.1-34). Federal Drug-Free Workplace Act considerations for federally funded schools are the stated rationale in ACBF, not an ND statute quoted in the policy.

Combined Research Notes — Gaps, Conflicts and Follow-ups (Run 1 + Run 2)

How to read this section. Every gap, unverified figure, conflicting figure, and follow-up from Run 1 (Parts 0–6, 30 Oct 2026) and Run 2 (Parts 7–12, 30 Oct 2026) is grouped by part below. Each Run 1 gap is marked Resolved, Partly resolved, or Still open. “Resolved” means Run 2 located the missing official fact; “Partly resolved” means Run 2 closed the core but a named sub-item remains Verify; “Still open” means neither run located it. Run 2 gaps are marked Run 2 — still open unless stated otherwise. Nothing marked Verify or Unavailable should be quoted as settled law or as a current official figure without the follow-up route named beside it. All facts accessed 30 Oct 2026 unless a source itself gives a different date.

Part 0 — Headlines and federal baseline

  • Board of Pharmacy Schedule III order — Still open (Run 1 + Run 2). No Board order on the 28 Apr 2026 federal Schedule III medical action was located in either run. NDCC §19-03.1-02(4) requires the Board to similarly control a federally rescheduled substance after 30 days unless it objects in that window, publishes reasons, and holds a hearing. Until an order (or a written confirmation of absence) is located, ND state-schedule status for medical marijuana is unresolved, even though the federal baseline for state-licensed medical activity is given. Follow-up: Board of Pharmacy website / order index and public contact (itself Verify — no Board public contact was located in Run 2; do not invent one).
  • ND response to federal hemp dates / 0.4 mg cap — Still open (Run 1 + Run 2). No NDDA, HHS, legislative, or ND official response was located to PL 119-37 §781 (effective 12 Nov 2026), PL 119-103 §2019 (limits it until 11 Dec 2026), the full total-THC definition, or the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap after 11 Dec 2026, nor to the USDA DEA-registered-lab enforcement delay to 31 Dec 2026. ND’s existing bans already cover the intoxicant categories the federal change targets, but ND has no per-container cap in the statute extract, and whether ND will conform its commissioner amount, adopt a per-container cap, or treat the federal cap as ceiling/floor is not determined. Follow-up: NDDA hemp page, ndlegis 2027 pre-filed bills, NDDA written position.
  • Tribal medical as “state-licensed” for federal Schedule III / 280E — Still open. A tribal medical programme licensed by a tribal commission is not on its face a state licence. Whether the federal rule’s “state-licensed” language covers tribal-licensed medical activity (SWO, §10) is Verify unless a federal rule, IRS guidance, or DEA/DOJ statement settles it. Do not advise a tribal licensee that 280E is lifted on the basis of either run.
  • Tax Commissioner Schedule III / 280E flow-through guidance — Still open (Run 1 Part 2). No Tax Commissioner guidance, ruling, or FAQ on Schedule III / §280E flow-through for ND medical licensees was located; scope for manufacturers vs. dispensaries, pass-through vs. corporation, and tribal programmes is Verify. ND does not decouple from federal §280E by statute. Follow-up: Tax Commissioner ruling/guidance; full “280E” / “Internal Revenue Code” bill search 2017–2026.

Part 1 — Laws

  • NDAC 33-44-01 full text — Still open (Run 1, carried into Run 2). Run 1 flagged full NDAC ch. 33-44-01 (packaging §-25, advertising §-23, delivery/transport §§-29/30, inventory-tracking, edible §-24.2) for re-check against the current NDAC PDF; secondary packaging summaries conflict on child-resistant requirements. Run 2 NDAC passages rest on search-index extracts (ndlegis.gov PDFs and Cornell LII mirrors) because the official NDAC PDF open failed and was not retried — they are marked [SEARCH-INDEX] and should be re-quoted from a directly opened ndlegis.gov PDF before republication as verbatim law. See also Part 11 gaps G3 below.
  • NDAC Article 7-20 (Hemp Products) full text — Still open. Run 1’s 7-20 citations remain secondary-sourced; the full text was not directly opened in Run 1 or Run 2. Do not cite the 5 mg (adopted NDAC figure) / 15:1 proposal as law. Do not confuse NDAC 7-20 with NDAC 74-03-16 (seed certification, Part 7).
  • Full §19-24.1-33 text — Still open (Run 2, Part 12). The FindLaw extract jumps from subsection 2 to subsection 4; subsection 3 (smoking/vaping in a vehicle / public place, if it exists) was not in the extract. Verify the full §19-24.1-33 text on ndlegis.gov, especially whether a public-consumption or vehicle-consumption subsection exists that the extract omitted. Do not invent a subsection 3.
  • §19-24.1-33(5) metabolite proviso vs. §39-08-01(1)(b) practitioner-direction defence — Still open. How courts have applied the practitioner-direction affirmative defence (“prescribed/dispensed”) to certified (not prescribed) medical marijuana: no case located. Verify by ND case-law search.
  • Caregiver fee/term (§19-24.1-04) and compassion-centre certification fees (§§19-24.1-14–15) — Still open in part. SB 2344 figures ($5,000 / $90,000 / $110,000) may be superseded; Run 2 confirms the fee-layer distinction (statute ceilings vs. NDAC actuals: $2,000/$3,000 applications; $60,000/$75,000 certifications through 31 Oct 2026; $90,000/$110,000 reported from 1 Nov 2026) but the 1 Nov 2026 schedule taking effect remains Verify. The plant surcharge within this fee question is Partly resolved: statutory ceiling $7,500 adopted; $5,000 payable (Correction 1), NDAC $5,000 confirmed as payable (not downgraded) Verify.
  • 2019 session law creating the <½ oz infraction tier; §19-03.1-22.3 ingestion; §19-03.1-23(9)/(12) under-21 provisions — Still open (Run 1). Not HB 1155 (which failed). Verify in full enrolled/session-law text.
  • HB 1596 tally 13–33 vs. 13–33 — Still open (Run 1 + Run 2). HB 1596 (2025, penalty reduction to a noncriminal $150 citation for <½ oz) passed the House (76–17, Feb 2025) and FAILED in the Senate (9 Apr 2025); the failure itself is confirmed, but secondary reports give the Senate tally as 13–33 vs. 13–33. Verify on ndlegis.gov bill-actions/journals. Current tiers are unchanged.
  • SB 2308 enactment; HB 1478 senior/hospice pathway; §19-24.1-03.1 veterans pathway; telehealth sentence in enrolled SB 2294 — Still open / partly resolved. SB 2308’s repeal of §§19-24.1-38/39 (annual reports, Advisory Board, effective 1 Aug 2025) is confirmed by HHS’s 2025 summary and the current NDCC PDF markings. HB 1478 (age 65+ vs. narrowed version; 14- vs. 30-day issuance), the veterans pathway text after SB 2294, and the telehealth sentence remain Verify against enrolled texts.
  • AG opinions index — Still open. No ND AG opinion on delta-8, hemp-derived THC, or medical-marijuana scheduling was located in either run. Secondary claims that an ND AG opinion bans delta-8 appear to conflate other states; the ND delta-8 ban rests on statute (HB 1045 (2021) + SB 2096 (2023)), not on a located opinion. The Delta 8 Resellers matter was an enforcement settlement, not an opinion. The one located opinion, 6 Apr 1992 Dotzenrod letter on zoning precedence, is labelled opinion, not law. Follow-up: attorneygeneral.nd.gov opinions index.
  • NDAC citation conflict (75-13 vs. 33-44) — Resolved in favour of 33-44. Official sources say Article 33-44, Chapter 33-44-01 (Run 1 Part 4 anchor, adopted in Run 2). Do not cite 75-13 as the current article without checking whether it was a former HHS article number.

Part 11 — Operations (Run 2 gaps G1–G25, plus Part 7 cross-references)

  • G1 Plant surcharge — Partly resolved. Statute $5,000 payable / $7,500 ceiling adopted; NDAC $5,000 confirmed as payable (not downgraded) Verify (Correction 1). Follow-up: directly open current NDAC §33-44-01-07.4; check whether 07.4 is a different fee layer.
  • G2 Current lab — Partly resolved, still Verify. See Correction 8. Follow-up: HHS procurement / State Procurement Office award for 325-111-23.24; HHS programme page. Per-test lab prices: Unavailable.
  • G3 Full NDAC testing / security / inventory sections — Still open. Full §§44, 45, 47, 48.1, 49, 51, 52, 55; §§09/10 pesticide detail; §§17/18 surveillance/alarm detail; §§19/20 inventory detail; §16 recall factors — all Verify from search-index extracts only. Pesticide, heavy-metal, and solvent numeric limits (§§47, 48.1, 49) were not extracted — Unavailable in Run 2; do not quote limits from secondary sites. Concentration §51 (edible deviation ≤10%) is a partial extract — Verify full §51. Random-testing frequency/rate (§54) — Verify.
  • Surveillance retention 60 vs. 90/60 days — Still open, conflict preserved. Secondary security-industry compilation states ND video retention = 60 days (citing the NDAC PDF); a secondary county guide states recordings in the first year must be kept ≥90 days and ≥60 days thereafter. Neither was verified from full §17 text. Do not publish a retention period as settled. Camera coverage, resolution, and alarm detail (§18) likewise Verify.
  • G4 Patient delivery — Unresolved (Correction 9). Follow-up: HHS dispensary guidance / NDAC §33-44-01-29 full text / HHS FAQ. Transport manifest detail adopted: manifests kept ≥7 years; tamper-evident containers, unmarked vehicle, enclosed locked compartment, direct travel, documented stops, 911 on emergency stop.
  • Discounts / coupons / loyalty / contests — Still open (G5). Free usable marijuana is banned (NDAC §33-44-01-23: “A dispensary may not provide free usable marijuana to a registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver”); price display on a dispensary website is permitted. No express NDAC discount, coupon, loyalty-program, sweepstakes, or contest rule was located in §23 as extracted. Whether a price discount (as opposed to free product) requires department approval under the “all other marketing subject to approval” limb is Verify — do not state a discount ban as law. A secondary guide claiming a blanket ban on giveaways/discount coupons/incentives/sweepstakes is not reliable (its “advertising strictly prohibited” opening contradicts §23’s permitted name/logo/website regime).
  • G6 30-day marketing-approval deadline — Still open. Secondary summary only; Verify against full §23 before quoting as law. Advertising limits adopted from §23: name/logo may not include marijuana/paraphernalia images, colloquial references, strain names, or medical symbols resembling established medical associations; no marketing to a minor; strain/brand names may not refer to products commonly associated with minors, marketed to minors, or be false/misleading (§24).
  • G7 NDDA position on DEA-registered hemp labs vs. federal 31 Dec 2026 delay — Conflict / Verify. The ND State Hemp Plan as filed states “After December 31st, 2022, hemp will only be tested at a DEA registered lab,” against the given federal baseline delaying USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing to 31 Dec 2026. Whether NDDA enforces the Plan’s 2022 sentence today or follows the federal delay was not located — do not state either position as NDDA’s current position. Follow-up: NDDA written position.
  • G8–G11 Hemp chapter / finished-product / environmental / security / insurance — Still open / Unavailable. Current ND hemp chapter and hemp transfer/renewal/relocation/background rules (Part 7); hemp finished-product (CBD) testing/labelling rule, CBD pet-treat registration as pet food, hemp animal-feed approval; ND medical/hemp water, energy, fertiliser rules and hemp pesticide list (no cannabis-specific water/energy rule located — wastewater is “medical marijuana waste” under §33-44-01-01 and falls under §15 hazardous-waste evaluation and general ND water law; odour is a local condition, not a statewide NDAC standard); ND hemp security rule and medical insurance minimum beyond the bond. Do not state any pesticide is “approved for hemp in ND” without an NDDA list; do not import medical-centre security rules into hemp.
  • Other Part 11 Verify / Unavailable items preserved: §19-24.1-17 “change of control” percentage threshold (none located); §19-24.1-20 violation detail (Class B felony for intentional out-of-chain transfer / dispensing without a valid card is a secondary summary — Verify §20 text); centre-level revocation list (Unavailable; FY2024 revocations 4, all patients, not centres); inspection frequency (announced/unannounced, annual) Verify (§§31/32, plan-of-correction procedure in §32); indoor medical cultivation sentence (enclosed/locked, not visible to public) — secondary only, Verify against NDAC §§12/13 or §§19-24.1-14/24; no canopy/square-footage/lighting/plant-count-per-patient rule beyond 1,000 plants / 3,500 oz; hemp plot GPS/lot-reporting format, reporting deadline, lot-size rules Verify (Part 7); hemp commingling-ban and disposal-method extracts truncated — Verify from a directly opened Plan PDF; negligent-violation threshold (good-faith >0.3% to ≤1.0% = negligent violation + corrective action plan ≥2 years) is a secondary summary of 7 CFR 990.6 — verify against eCFR before quoting; university medical programme — see Part 11.9 below.
  • NDSU hemp research vs. no university medical programme — Confirmed as stated. NDSU hemp work is extensive (1998–1999 legislative history, 2015 Langdon trials, 2021–2022 Prosper trials, USDA-ARS hempseed-cake cattle study — research, not feed approval). No NDSU or other ND university medical-marijuana research programme, research licence, or clinical trial was located; the only research carve-out is the §19-24.1-24 allowance of 50 additional plants for department-authorised R&D at a manufacturing facility (industry R&D, not university research). Whether NDSU holds a current NDDA research licence vs. operating under the Plan’s research protocol was not distinguished — Verify (Part 7). No ND institution’s DEA registration was located — Verify if claimed. Hemp as animal feed is not an approved ND use on the evidence located (FDA-CVM approval pending).

Part 12 — Consumer and social rules (Run 2 gaps G12–G24)

  • G12 DUI penalty tiers / lookback / suspension (current §39-08-01) — Still open. Older text only (first/second DUI = Class B misdemeanour in older text); current tiers, lookback periods (5-year/7-year), licence suspension, ignition-interlock, and plate-destruction provisions were not fully extracted — Verify current §39-08-01(2)–(5) before quoting DUI penalties. §39-08-01.4 (DUI with a minor, driver ≥21 = Class A misdemeanour; prior conviction = Class C felony) is as extracted. No per se THC driving limit: §39-08-01 is an impairment test (“incapable of safely driving”); the 0.08% figure is alcohol only. no per se THC limit [that threshold figure is removed — it is not ND law] claim rejected: secondary claim of a no per se THC limit [that threshold figure is removed — it is not ND law] THC threshold contradicts §39-08-01 text — do not publish it as ND law.
  • G13–G14 Driving defences / CDL — Still open. §39-08-01(1)(b) practitioner-direction defence applied to certified marijuana: no case located. CDL bar for cardholders as an ND statute vs. federal DOT consequence: secondary only — present as a federal DOT consequence (49 CFR Part 40), not as an ND CDL statute, unless an ND statute is located (Title 39 ch. 39-06.2 / DOT rules).
  • G15 Expungement/sealing subsection and fee — Still open. Marijuana-specific sealing §19-03.1-23(9) in FindLaw current text vs. (10) in National Reentry Resource Center summaries — Verify current subsection number on ndlegis.gov before citing a number; whether the broader “has not been convicted of any other criminal offense” bar survives in current text — Verify; filing fee — Verify (do not import a $100–$300 secondary estimate). Adopted tracks, kept separate: (i) marijuana sealing — petition-based (motion), not automatic, first offence only, ≤1 oz / ≤2 g THC, 2-year clean period, “may not be opened even by order of the court”; (ii) general sealing ch. 12-60.1 — petition, clear-and-convincing-evidence test, misdemeanour ≥3 years / felony ≥5 years without new conviction, unconditional pardon; DUI records cannot be sealed under ch. 12-60.1; sealed ≠ destroyed; (iii) nonconviction closing §12-60.1-05 (HB 1166, 2025, eff. 1 Aug 2025) — automatic closure 61 days after a nonconviction order on/after 1 Aug 2025, petition for earlier orders. There is no automatic sealing/expungement of marijuana convictions in ND.
  • G16 Full §19-24.1-33 — Still open. See Part 1 above.
  • G17 Enrolled SB 2294 §19-24.1-03.1 — Partly resolved. See Part 10 above (committee text + fiscal note + NDAC fee $40 resident / $40 nonresident / $200 agent as amended 1 Oct 2025; 60-day nonresident term Verify against enrolled §19-24.1-03.1 / NDAC §33-44-01-05).
  • G18 Hemp-CBD minimum age; CBD vape as “electronic smoking device” — Still open. No ND statute located sets a minimum age for lawful, non-intoxicating hemp/CBD products; retailer 18+/21+ policies are private policies, not law (secondary 18+ vs. 21+ vs. “no age specified” conflict — none adopted). Whether a hemp-CBD vape is an “electronic smoking device” under §12.1-31-03 (21 to sell to) vs. a hemp product under ch. 4.1-18.1 is Verify. Intoxicating hemp products are prohibited at any age (no “21+ delta-8” rule in ND). The hemp-extract inhalant ban’s exact statutory hook for a non-intoxicating CBD vape is Verify.
  • Landlord / tenant rule — None located (Run 2 G19). No ND statute located creates a medical-marijuana landlord/tenant rule in NDCC ch. 47-16: no “landlord may not discriminate against a patient” statute and no “landlord may prohibit medical use” statute located. Secondary claims on both sides are uncited to ND law and are not adopted. What is established: §19-24.1-32(1) protects against denial of a right/privilege including civil penalty/disciplinary action — whether that reaches private landlord decisions has not been litigated in any ND case located (Verify); a 2026 secondary review found “none of the six patient protections” including housing; general lease law applies (a lease smoking prohibition, if drafted to cover marijuana/smoking generally, is enforceable as a contract term); cultivation in rentals is moot (home grow prohibited). The §19-24.1-32 facility (nursing-home) protection — facilities “may not unreasonably limit” a patient’s medical use unless it would cause loss of a federal monetary/licensing benefit — is a secondary summary; Verify the facility subsection text. It is not a landlord/tenant rule and must not be presented as one. Federal/public-housing position is general federal law — Verify current HUD guidance if detailed.
  • Schedule III gun effect — Still open (Run 2 G20). Federal §922(g)(3) prohibits an “unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance” from possessing firearms/ammunition; ATF Form 4473 Question 21.f and the 2011 ATF letter state marijuana users are prohibited regardless of state medicinal legalisation. No ND statute restricts a cardholder’s firearm purchase/ownership as ND law, and no ND statute protects it against the federal rule. Whether Schedule III changes the §922(g)(3) analysis for state-licensed medical patients is Verify — no ATF guidance, regulation, or court decision on the Schedule III medical carve-out and §922(g)(3) was located. Do not state that Schedule III restores gun rights. Current §922(g)(3) Second Amendment litigation status (secondary summaries flag federal appeals limits and a Supreme Court argument in early 2026) — Verify before publishing any litigation claim. ND location rule §62.1-02-05 (public gatherings) applies to everyone, not only patients.
  • DPI / ACBF statewide adoption — Still open (Run 2 G21). No ND Department of Public Instruction medical-marijuana guidance document was located; no ND “school administration of medical marijuana” statute exists (contrast Ashley’s Law-type statutes). District model policy ACBF — Medical Marijuana (West Fargo ACBF-AP, East Central Special Education Unit ACBF 19 Nov 2025, McKenzie County PSD #1, Jamestown PSD) prohibits possession, administration, and use of medical marijuana while on school property, while also providing the district shall not refuse enrollment / discriminate solely on qualifying-patient status and shall not refuse to hire / discharge / penalise solely on cardholder status, with discipline for under-the-influence during work hours / on school property preserved and cardholder status confidential. These are district policies, not state law — do not present one district’s ACBF as a statewide DPI rule, and do not claim statewide adoption without a district-policy survey (Verify). NDCC §15.1-19-23 (general school medication programme) does not mention marijuana and must not be presented as a medical-marijuana school-administration law.
  • Veterinary rule — None located. No ND veterinary cannabis rule located in NDCC ch. 43-29; no ND rule authorises a veterinarian to prescribe/recommend marijuana, and marijuana-derived CBD for animals is not a lawful veterinary drug in ND (it would be marijuana under ch. 19-03.1 except for registered human patients). Hemp CBD pet treats sold as pet products: whether a CBD pet treat must be registered as pet food and whether NDDA accepts CBD as a pet-food ingredient was not located — Verify with NDDA Pet Food Program (pet food / specialty pet food must be registered with NDDA before distribution under NDCC ch. 4.1-41; feed manufacturer licence $120, NDDA Aug 2025 guidelines).
  • Other Part 12 Verify items preserved: dual-use paraphernalia distinction by intended use (no ND case/AG opinion located); smoke-free ch. 23-12 summary (secondary — verify against NDCC text); HB 1628 kratom addition to §12.1-31-03 — labelled PROPOSED until confirmed on ndlegis.gov (engrossment, 69th LA special session 2); patient transport conditions (container, vehicle location) and passenger consumption / parked-vehicle smoking — Verify (do not import centre manifest rules into patient transport); public-consumption “private residence only” claim — secondary/Verify, not a quoted statute, beyond ch. 23-12 + §19-24.1-33(2)/(4)/(5); §19-24.1-32 firearm-disclosure sentence — Verify in current text.
  • Proposed / Failed measures labelled as such throughout: HB 1420/HB 1501 (adult-use + tax) FAILED Senate; HB 1596 FAILED Senate; 2018 Measure 3, 2022 Measure 2, 2024 Measure 5 DEFEATED; HB 1370 (2025, oral-fluid screening) disposition Verify — not presented as enacted; HB 1628 kratom PROPOSED until confirmed; 2024 Measure 5 fiscal note ($10,227,600 revenue / $8,324,275 expenses, 2025–27) and 2022/2024 proposed caps (7 cultivation / 18 retail; 7 manufacturing / 18 dispensaries) are PROPOSED summaries, never current law. No cannabis measure on the 3 Nov 2026 ballot was confirmed as of 30 Oct 2026.
